<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> Every classical Liénard system $`\dot x=y-F(x)`$, $`\dot y=-x`$, with <i>F</i> an arbitrary real polynomial of degree at most five, has at most two geometrically distinct isolated periodic orbits, and the bound is attained. This proves the degree-five case of the Lins Neto–de Melo–Pugh conjecture.

- **claim-main-two-cycles** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): If $\deg F\le5$, the system <a href="#eq:original-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:original-system">[eq:original-system]</a> has at most two limit cycles in $\mathbb R^2$. Some members of this class have two limit cycles. Thus its exact maximum is two.
  - Explanation: Every classical Liénard system whose primitive has degree at most five has at most two limit cycles, and some members attain two, so the sharp maximum is two.
- **claim-arc-data** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-arc-data)): For every $0<h<t$, the solution through $(y,u)=(\phi(t),t)$ has exactly two intersections with $u=h$. They are transverse and satisfy $$y_<(h,t)<\phi(h)<y_>(h,t).$$ The part of the solution between them has a unique maximum $u=t$. Set $$\label{eq:arc-endpoint-data} r(h,t)=\frac{y_>(h,t)-y_<(h,t)}2,\qquad M=\frac{y_>(h,t)+y_<(h,t)}2-\phi(h).$$ At fixed $h$, the lower endpoint decreases strictly with $t$, the upper endpoint increases strictly, and both derivatives are nonzero. The map $t\mapsto r(h,t)$ is a smooth increasing bijection from $(h,\infty)$ onto $(0,\infty)$.* *Consequently $M$ is a smooth function of $(h,r)\in(0,\infty)^2$. Writing $v=M_r$, one has $$\label{eq:arc-strict-data} \|M(h,r)\|<r,\qquad \|v(h,r)\|<1,\qquad r^2\ge 2(t-h).$$ For a fixed width, the corresponding base height is strictly increasing with peak height: $\partial h/\partial t>0$.* *If $\phi$ is smooth on the whole real line, the same conclusions hold for arbitrary real $h<t$. In that case, at each fixed peak $t$, letting $h$ decrease from $t$ to $-\infty$ parametrizes the full range $0<r<\infty$.
  - Explanation: For a general profile, each arc through a peak meets every lower base height exactly twice and transversally. The half-width parametrizes the arcs bijectively, and the midpoint and its width derivative obey strict bounds.
- **claim-profile-variation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-variation)): Consider a smooth one-parameter family of profiles $\phi_\eta$, with $\phi_0=\phi$, and fix a regular base height $h$ and a width $r>0$. All profiles need only be defined on a neighborhood of the relevant compact arcs. Write $$\psi(u)=\left.\partial_\eta\phi_\eta(u)\right\|_{\eta=0}, \qquad Y=M+\phi(h),\qquad K(y)=\exp\left(-\int_{y_<}^{y}\phi'(u(z))\,dz\right).$$ Then the first variation $\delta Y$, with $h,r$ fixed, satisfies $$\label{eq:arc-variation} \bigl[(M+r)K(y_>)-(M-r)K(y_<)\bigr]\delta Y =\int_{y_<}^{y_>}K(y)\psi(u(y))\,dy.$$ The coefficient of $\delta Y$ is strictly positive. The variation of the relative midpoint is $\delta M=\delta Y-\psi(h)$.* *Consequently, if two profiles smooth on $(0,\infty)$ satisfy, at a fixed $h>0$, $$\phi_1(u)-\phi_1(h)>\phi_0(u)-\phi_0(h) \qquad\text{for every }u>h,$$ then $M_1(h,r)>M_0(h,r)$ for every $r>0$. The analogous comparison holds at any real base height for two entire smooth profiles.
  - Explanation: A first-variation formula with a positive coefficient shows how a perturbation of the profile moves the relative midpoint at fixed height and width. This gives a comparison of midpoints for ordered profiles.
- **claim-small-width-scaling** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-arc-scaling)): Near any fixed positive base height, the small-width arc data have smooth parameter-dependent expansions $$\label{eq:arc-small-width} \begin{aligned} M(h,r)&=\frac13 k(h)r^2+r^3 A(h,r),\\ v(h,r)&=\frac23 k(h)r+r^2 B(h,r),\\ t(h,r)&=h+r^2\left(\frac12+r\,C(h,r)\right), \end{aligned}$$ where the remainder functions extend smoothly through $r=0$. The same assertion holds jointly for any finite-dimensional smooth family of profiles. In particular, these expansions may be differentiated, and $\left.\partial h/\partial t\right\|_r\to1$ as $r\downarrow0$. The assertions also hold near any regular base height of a profile defined on an open interval containing that height.
  - Explanation: The arc data have smooth expansions through zero width, with explicit leading rational coefficients in the midpoint, its width derivative and the peak height.
- **claim-axis-extension** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-axis-extension)): Each peak $t>0$ has finite limiting endpoints $$a(t)=\lim_{h\downarrow0}y_<(h,t)\le0,\qquad b(t)=\lim_{h\downarrow0}y_>(h,t)\ge0.$$ The set $$\mathcal T_\phi=\{t>0:a(t)<0<b(t)\}$$ is either empty or an open ray in $(0,\infty)$. If it is nonempty, $a,b$ are smooth there, with $a_t<0<b_t$, and $$r(0,t)=\frac{b(t)-a(t)}2$$ is a smooth increasing bijection onto an open width ray $\mathcal I_\phi\subset(0,\infty)$. If $\mathcal T_\phi$ is empty, set $\mathcal I_\phi=\varnothing$.* *On $\mathcal I_\phi$, the limiting midpoint $M(0,r)$ is smooth, and $$\|M(0,r)\|<r,\qquad \|M_r(0,r)\|<1.$$ As $h\downarrow0$, all partial $r$-derivatives of $M(h,r)$ converge locally uniformly on $\mathcal I_\phi$ to the corresponding derivatives of $M(0,r)$.
  - Explanation: Arcs extend transversally down to height zero for the peaks in an open ray. On the corresponding width ray the limiting midpoint is smooth and keeps the strict bounds.
- **claim-cycle-matching** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-cycle-matching)): For the system $$\label{eq:arc-lienard-system} \dot x=y-F(x),\qquad \dot y=-x,$$ the images of nonconstant periodic solutions are in one-to-one correspondence with the zeros of $$\label{eq:cycle-matching} \Delta(r)=M_+(0,r)-M_-(0,r)$$ on the single open interval $\mathcal I=\mathcal I_+\cap\mathcal I_-$. If this interval is empty, there are no such periodic solutions. Each periodic orbit has exactly one positive and one negative intersection with the $y$-axis. Under the correspondence, isolated periodic orbits are exactly isolated zeros of $\Delta$.
  - Explanation: Periodic orbits of the Liénard system correspond one-to-one to zeros of the difference of the two height-zero midpoint functions on a common width interval, and isolated orbits correspond to isolated zeros.
- **claim-reference-characteristics** (theorem, [source](#stmt-fit-references)): The reference functions are smooth in $(\kappa,s,z)$ for $s>0$ and are defined at every finite positive width. They satisfy $$\label{eq:fit-reference-system} \partial_s j_\kappa=-s\kappa\operatorname{sech}^2\Theta_\kappa, \qquad \partial_s\Theta_\kappa =j_\kappa-\frac{\sinh(2\Theta_\kappa)}s, \qquad \partial_s\mathcal L_\kappa =\frac{\sinh(2\Theta_\kappa)}s,$$ with limiting values $j_\kappa(0,z)=z$ and $\Theta_\kappa(0,z)=\mathcal L_\kappa(0,z)=0$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:fit-reference-bounds} \|j_\kappa(s,z)-z\|\le\frac{\|\kappa\|s^2}{2},\qquad \partial_z j_\kappa(s,z)>0,\qquad \partial_z\Theta_\kappa(s,z)>0.$$ For each fixed $s>0$ and $\kappa$, the map $z\mapsto\Theta_\kappa(s,z)$ is a bijection onto $\mathbb R$. Finally, $$\label{eq:fit-L-z} \partial_z\mathcal L_\kappa(s,z) =\int_0^s \frac{2\cosh(2\Theta_\kappa(q,z))}{q} \partial_z\Theta_\kappa(q,z)\,dq>0.$$
  - Explanation: The reference quadratic characteristics are smooth, defined at every positive width, satisfy explicit transport equations, and the peak slope maps bijectively onto the angle at fixed width.
- **claim-curvature-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-curvature-comparison)): Let a smooth profile $\phi$ have an arc with peak height $t$, and parameterize that arc from its peak to a final width $r>0$ by $s\in(0,r]$. Assume $\phi$ is smooth on a neighborhood of the traversed height interval. Write $h(s)$, $\theta(s)$, and $L(s)$ for its characteristic data, and set $k=\phi'$. For any fixed $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, define $$E(s)=k(h(s))-J_\kappa(s,\theta(s)),\qquad \theta(s)=\Theta_\kappa(s,z(s)).$$ Then $z(s)\to k(t)$, $E(s)\to0$ as $s\downarrow0$, and $$\begin{aligned} E'(s)+(J_\kappa)_\theta(s,\theta(s))E(s) &=-s\bigl(\phi''(h(s))-\kappa\bigr) \operatorname{sech}^2\theta(s), \label{eq:curvature-error}\\ z'(s)&=\frac{E(s)}{\partial_z\Theta_\kappa(s,z(s))}. \label{eq:label-derivative} \end{aligned}$$ Put $a(s)=(J_\kappa)_\theta(s,\theta(s))>0$ and let $f(s)$ denote the right-hand side of Equation <a href="#eq:curvature-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-error">[eq:curvature-error]</a>. The comparison from zero is given by the convergent formula $$\label{eq:fit-error-integral} E(s)=\int_0^s \exp\left(-\int_q^s a(\xi)\,d\xi\right)f(q)\,dq.$$ In particular, if $\phi''(h(s))\ge\kappa$ for $0<s\le r$, then $E\le0$, $z$ is nonincreasing, and $$\theta(s)\ge\Theta_\kappa(s,z(r))\quad(0<s<r),\qquad L(r)\ge\mathcal L_\kappa(r,z(r)).$$ If the curvature difference is not identically zero on $(0,r)$, the last inequality is strict, as are all the preceding angle inequalities for $0<s<r$, and $E(r)<0$. All signs reverse under the assumption $\phi''(h(s))\le\kappa$.
  - Explanation: Comparing an actual arc with a quadratic reference of constant curvature through an error equation, a one-sided curvature inequality forces strict inequalities on the angles and on the final width-derivative angle.
- **claim-conditional-derivative** (theorem, [source](#stmt-fit-conditional-derivative)): For every $r>0$, $\|M\|<r$, and $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, $$\label{eq:fit-conditional-positive} \partial_\kappa\widehat L(r,M,\kappa)>0, \qquad \partial_\kappa\widehat v(r,M,\kappa)=\frac GP>0,$$ where the model derivatives on the right are evaluated at the midpoint fit. In particular, $G>0$ for every model arc.
  - Explanation: After fitting the midpoint, the fitted width derivative and its angle increase strictly with curvature. This also yields positivity of the determinant G for every model arc.
- **claim-properness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-fit-properness)): For fixed $r>0$ and $\|M\|<r$, $$\label{eq:fit-properness} \lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}\widehat L(r,M,\kappa)=+\infty, \qquad \lim_{\kappa\to-\infty}\widehat L(r,M,\kappa)=-\infty.$$
  - Explanation: The conditional angle tends to plus and minus infinity with the curvature, so it covers the whole real line.
- **claim-positive-model-derivatives** (theorem, [source](#stmt-fit-positive-derivatives)): The model derivatives $R$ and $S$ are strictly positive for every $(\lambda,\kappa,r)\in\mathbb R^2\times(0,\infty)$. The width identity is $$\label{eq:fit-width-identity} \kappa P+\lambda=\frac{rH_r+H}{r^2-H^2}.$$ With $L_{\mathrm{mod}}(\lambda,\kappa,r) =\operatorname{arctanh}H_r(\lambda,\kappa,r)$, differentiating Equation <a href="#eq:fit-width-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-width-identity">[eq:fit-width-identity]</a> with respect to $r$ at fixed $(\lambda,\kappa)$ gives $$\label{eq:fit-R-identity} \kappa R =\frac{r(1-H_r^2)}{r^2-H^2} \left((L_{\mathrm{mod}})_r-\frac{2H}{r^2-H^2}\right).$$
  - Explanation: The width derivatives R and S of the model midpoint variations are strictly positive on the whole parameter space, and a width identity relates the curvature term to the width-derivative angle.
- **claim-global-quadratic-fit** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-quadratic-fit)): For every $r>0$ and every pair $(M,v)$ satisfying $\|M\|<r$ and $\|v\|<1$, there is a unique finite pair $(\lambda,\kappa)\in\mathbb R^2$ such that $$H(\lambda,\kappa,r)=M,\qquad H_r(\lambda,\kappa,r)=v.$$ The fitted parameters depend jointly smoothly on $(M,v,r)$ in this open domain. Equivalently, the smooth map $$\label{eq:fit-joint-map} (\lambda,\kappa,r)\longmapsto (H(\lambda,\kappa,r),H_r(\lambda,\kappa,r),r)$$ is a diffeomorphism from $\mathbb R^2\times(0,\infty)$ onto $\{(M,v,r):r>0,\ \|M\|<r,\ \|v\|<1\}$. At every model arc, $P,Q,R,S,G>0$. In particular, fitted parameters converge to a finite pair whenever their data converge to an interior point of this domain, even when the width varies.
  - Explanation: At each positive width, the midpoint and its width derivative determine a unique quadratic fit, and the map to these data is a diffeomorphism onto the admissible domain.
- **claim-fit-transport** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-fit-transport)): The fitted parameters satisfy $$\label{eq:fit-transport} \begin{aligned} P\lambda_r+Q\kappa_r&=0,\\ D\kappa&=-\frac{R}{G}\bigl(\lambda-k(h)\bigr),\\ D\lambda-\kappa&=\frac{S}{G}\bigl(\lambda-k(h)\bigr). \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Along a characteristic the fitted slope and curvature obey transport equations whose forcing is proportional to the gap between the fitted slope and the actual slope.
- **claim-model-inequality** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-model-inequality)): For every $\lambda,\kappa\in\mathbb R$ and $r>0$, $$\left(\frac{R}{G}\right)_r\leq 0. \label{model:inequality}$$
  - Explanation: The ratio of the model derivatives R and G is nonincreasing in width for all model parameters. This is the central sign estimate of the paper.
- **claim-endpoint-determinant** (theorem, [source](#stmt-model-endpoint-algebra)): The exact determinant identity is $$X_mZ_{mm}-Z_mX_{mm} =\frac{\ell^2(m+n)^2}{m^2n^2(1+\ell)^3}D_* =\frac{3\kappa^2}{m_r^3}\mathcal W. \label{model:determinant-factor}$$ At a zero of $\mathcal W$ with $m\ne n$, set $\sigma=X_{mm}/X_m$. This is well defined, and $$\begin{aligned} \gamma_{rr}-\sigma_0\gamma_r &=m_r^2(\gamma_{mm}-\sigma\gamma_m), \label{model:coordinate-zero-sign}\\ \gamma_{mm}-\sigma\gamma_m &=\frac{\ell(m+n)^2}{(1+\ell)m^2n^2} \frac{w_*-w}{m-n}. \label{model:gamma-factor} \end{aligned}$$ All the prefactors displayed in Equations <a href="#model:determinant-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:determinant-factor">[model:determinant-factor]</a> and <a href="#model:gamma-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:gamma-factor">[model:gamma-factor]</a> are strictly positive.
  - Explanation: An exact determinant identity expresses the endpoint-coordinate determinant through the sign-carrying quantity W, and gives a factorized form of the second-order quantity at a zero of W.
- **claim-riccati-schwarzian** (theorem, [source](#stmt-model-riccati)): For the quadratic profile $du+bu^2$, with $b>0$, one has $$\mathcal S =2b(m+n\ell^2)+\frac{d^2}{2}(\ell^2-1) -d\left(\frac1m+\frac{\ell^2}{n}\right) +\frac32\left(\frac{\ell^2}{n^2}-\frac1{m^2}\right). \label{model:Schwarzian-formula}$$ The arc admits a positive function $V$ such that $$\begin{aligned} \ell&=\frac{mV(-m)^2}{nV(n)^2},\nonumber\\ w&=\frac1m-\frac{\ell}{n}+d(1+\ell) +2bmV(-m)^2\int_{-m}^{n}V(y)^{-2}\,dy. \label{model:variation-formulas} \end{aligned}$$ If $d<0$ and $d^2\ge4bm$, put $\nu=(d^2/4-bm)^{1/2}$. Then $$w\le\frac1m-\frac{\ell}{n}+d\ell-2\nu. \label{model:Riccati-bound}$$ This includes $\nu=0$.
  - Explanation: For a quadratic profile with positive leading coefficient, a Riccati linearization gives the endpoint Schwarzian, a positive common label function, and an integral upper bound on the logarithmic derivative of the endpoint ratio.
- **claim-zero-sign** (theorem, [source](#stmt-model-zero-sign)): If $\kappa>0$ and $\mathcal W=0$, then $\gamma_{rr}-\sigma_0\gamma_r>0$, with $\sigma_0$ defined in Equation <a href="#model:zero-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:zero-relations">[model:zero-relations]</a>.
  - Explanation: At any zero of W with positive curvature, the second-order coordinate quantity is strictly positive, which prevents W from reaching zero from positive values.
- **claim-small-width-fit** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-fit-small-width)): At each positive height, locally uniformly in that height as $r$ tends to zero, $$\label{eq:fit-small-width} \begin{aligned} \kappa(h,r)&=\phi''(h)+\frac{2}{7}\phi'''(h)r^2+O(r^3),\\ \kappa_r(h,r)&=\frac{4}{7}\phi'''(h)r+O(r^2),\\ \lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\frac{1}{35}\phi'''(h)r^4+O(r^5). \end{aligned}$$ More precisely, for every $h_0>0$ there are smooth functions $A$ and $B$ on a neighborhood of $(h_0,0)$ for which, at positive $r$ in that neighborhood, $$\label{eq:fit-smooth-remainders} \begin{aligned} \lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\frac{1}{35}\phi'''(h)r^4+r^5 A(h,r),\\ \kappa(h,r)&=\phi''(h)+\frac{2}{7}\phi'''(h)r^2+r^3 B(h,r). \end{aligned}$$ Thus the derivative estimate in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-small-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-small-width">[eq:fit-small-width]</a> comes from a smooth remainder.
  - Explanation: At small width the fitted slope and curvature have explicit expansions governed by the third derivative of the profile, with smooth remainders.
- **claim-third-derivative** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-third-derivative)): If $\phi'''(u)>0$ for every $u>0$, then, for every $h,r>0$, $$\lambda(h,r)<k(h),\qquad \kappa_r(h,r)>0.$$ If $\phi'''(u)<0$ for every $u>0$, both inequalities reverse: $$\lambda(h,r)>k(h),\qquad \kappa_r(h,r)<0.$$
  - Explanation: A strict sign of the third derivative of the profile forces the fitted slope to lie on one side of the actual slope and the fitted curvature to be strictly monotone in width.
- **claim-slope-intercept** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-intercept)): Let $d\in\mathbb R$. If $$\frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{k(u)-d}{u}\right)>0 \qquad\text{for every }u>0,$$ then $$\lambda(h,r)-h\kappa(h,r)<d \qquad\text{for every }h,r>0.$$ If the displayed derivative is strictly negative everywhere, then $\lambda(h,r)-h\kappa(h,r)>d$ everywhere.
  - Explanation: A strict sign of the derivative of the shifted slope quotient bounds the fitted slope intercept strictly on one side of the constant.
- **claim-fitted-curvature-range** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-fitted-curvature-range)): Let an arc have positive base height $h$, peak height $t>h$, and width $r$. Its fitted curvature satisfies $$\label{eq:fitted-curvature-range} \min_{h\le u\le t}\phi''(u) \le\kappa(h,r)\le \max_{h\le u\le t}\phi''(u).$$ If $\phi''$ is nonconstant on $[h,t]$, both inequalities are strict.
  - Explanation: The fitted curvature lies between the minimum and maximum of the actual second derivative along the arc, strictly when that second derivative is nonconstant.
- **claim-isolated-zero-count** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-isolated-zero-count)): Let $f$ be continuously differentiable on an open interval and suppose $f'=bq$, where $b$ is continuous and positive and $q$ is continuous and nondecreasing. Then $f$ has at most two isolated zeros. If a continuously differentiable function has strictly positive derivative at each of its zeros, it has at most one zero.
  - Explanation: A function whose derivative is a positive factor times a nondecreasing factor has at most two isolated zeros, including multiple zeros and zero intervals. If the derivative is positive at every zero, there is at most one zero.

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-axis-extension",
          "exact_quote": "Each peak $t>0$ has finite limiting endpoints $$a(t)=\\lim_{h\\downarrow0}y_<(h,t)\\le0,\\qquad\n  b(t)=\\lim_{h\\downarrow0}y_>(h,t)\\ge0.$$ The set $$\\mathcal T_\\phi=\\{t>0:a(t)<0<b(t)\\}$$ is either empty or an open ray in $(0,\\infty)$. If it is nonempty, $a,b$ are smooth there, with $a_t<0<b_t$, and $$r(0,t)=\\frac{b(t)-a(t)}2$$ is a smooth increasing bijection onto an open width ray $\\mathcal I_\\phi\\subset(0,\\infty)$. If $\\mathcal T_\\phi$ is empty, set $\\mathcal I_\\phi=\\varnothing$.*\n\n*On $\\mathcal I_\\phi$, the limiting midpoint $M(0,r)$ is smooth, and $$|M(0,r)|<r,\\qquad |M_r(0,r)|<1.$$ As $h\\downarrow0$, all partial $r$-derivatives of $M(h,r)$ converge locally uniformly on $\\mathcal I_\\phi$ to the corresponding derivatives of $M(0,r)$.",
          "source_id": "PM-143.2"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Each peak $t>0$ has finite limiting endpoints $$a(t)=\\lim_{h\\downarrow0}y_<(h,t)\\le0,\\qquad\n  b(t)=\\lim_{h\\downarrow0}y_>(h,t)\\ge0.$$ The set $$\\mathcal T_\\phi=\\{t>0:a(t)<0<b(t)\\}$$ is either empty or an open ray in $(0,\\infty)$. If it is nonempty, $a,b$ are smooth there, with $a_t<0<b_t$, and $$r(0,t)=\\frac{b(t)-a(t)}2$$ is a smooth increasing bijection onto an open width ray $\\mathcal I_\\phi\\subset(0,\\infty)$. If $\\mathcal T_\\phi$ is empty, set $\\mathcal I_\\phi=\\varnothing$.*\n\n*On $\\mathcal I_\\phi$, the limiting midpoint $M(0,r)$ is smooth, and $$|M(0,r)|<r,\\qquad |M_r(0,r)|<1.$$ As $h\\downarrow0$, all partial $r$-derivatives of $M(h,r)$ converge locally uniformly on $\\mathcal I_\\phi$ to the corresponding derivatives of $M(0,r)$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-cycle-matching",
        "exact_quote": "For the system $$\\label{eq:arc-lienard-system}\n  \\dot x=y-F(x),\\qquad \\dot y=-x,$$ the images of nonconstant periodic solutions are in one-to-one correspondence with the zeros of $$\\label{eq:cycle-matching}\n  \\Delta(r)=M_+(0,r)-M_-(0,r)$$ on the single open interval $\\mathcal I=\\mathcal I_+\\cap\\mathcal I_-$. If this interval is empty, there are no such periodic solutions. Each periodic orbit has exactly one positive and one negative intersection with the $y$-axis. Under the correspondence, isolated periodic orbits are exactly isolated zeros of $\\Delta$.",
        "source_id": "PM-143.2"
      },
      "evidence": [

If this interval is empty, there are no such periodic solutions. Each periodic orbit has exactly one positive and one negative intersection with the $y$-axis.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-fit-references",
        "exact_quote": "The reference functions are smooth in $(\\kappa,s,z)$ for $s>0$ and are defined at every finite positive width. They satisfy $$\\label{eq:fit-reference-system}\n \\partial_s j_\\kappa=-s\\kappa\\operatorname{sech}^2\\Theta_\\kappa,\n \\qquad\n \\partial_s\\Theta_\\kappa\n   =j_\\kappa-\\frac{\\sinh(2\\Theta_\\kappa)}s,\n \\qquad\n \\partial_s\\mathcal L_\\kappa\n   =\\frac{\\sinh(2\\Theta_\\kappa)}s,$$ with limiting values $j_\\kappa(0,z)=z$ and $\\Theta_\\kappa(0,z)=\\mathcal L_\\kappa(0,z)=0$. Moreover, $$\\label{eq:fit-reference-bounds}\n |j_\\kappa(s,z)-z|\\le\\frac{|\\kappa|s^2}{2},\\qquad\n \\partial_z j_\\kappa(s,z)>0,\\qquad\n \\partial_z\\Theta_\\kappa(s,z)>0.$$ For each fixed $s>0$ and $\\kappa$, the map $z\\mapsto\\Theta_\\kappa(s,z)$ is a bijection onto $\\mathbb R$. Finally, $$\\label{eq:fit-L-z}\n \\partial_z\\mathcal L_\\kappa(s,z)\n  =\\int_0^s\n    \\frac{2\\cosh(2\\Theta_\\kappa(q,z))}{q}\n       \\partial_z\\Theta_\\kappa(q,z)\\,dq>0.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-143.2"
      },
      "evidence": [

Moreover, $$\\label{eq:fit-reference-bounds}\n |j_\\kappa(s,z)-z|\\le\\frac{|\\kappa|s^2}{2},\\qquad\n \\partial_z j_\\kappa(s,z)>0,\\qquad\n \\partial_z\\Theta_\\kappa(s,z)>0.$$ For each fixed $s>0$ and $\\kappa$, the map $z\\mapsto\\Theta_\\kappa(s,z)$ is a bijection onto $\\mathbb R$. Finally, $$\\label{eq:fit-L-z}\n \\partial_z\\mathcal L_\\kappa(s,z)\n  =\\int_0^s\n    \\frac{2\\cosh(2\\Theta_\\kappa(q,z))}{q}\n       \\partial_z\\Theta_\\kappa(q,z)\\,dq>0.$$",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-curvature-comparison",
        "exact_quote": "Let a smooth profile $\\phi$ have an arc with peak height $t$, and parameterize that arc from its peak to a final width $r>0$ by $s\\in(0,r]$. Assume $\\phi$ is smooth on a neighborhood of the traversed height interval. Write $h(s)$, $\\theta(s)$, and $L(s)$ for its characteristic data, and set $k=\\phi'$. For any fixed $\\kappa\\in\\mathbb R$, define $$E(s)=k(h(s))-J_\\kappa(s,\\theta(s)),\\qquad\n \\theta(s)=\\Theta_\\kappa(s,z(s)).$$ Then $z(s)\\to k(t)$, $E(s)\\to0$ as $s\\downarrow0$, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n E'(s)+(J_\\kappa)_\\theta(s,\\theta(s))E(s)\n  &=-s\\bigl(\\phi''(h(s))-\\kappa\\bigr)\n       \\operatorname{sech}^2\\theta(s),\n       \\label{eq:curvature-error}\\\\\n z'(s)&=\\frac{E(s)}{\\partial_z\\Theta_\\kappa(s,z(s))}.\n       \\label{eq:label-derivative}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Put $a(s)=(J_\\kappa)_\\theta(s,\\theta(s))>0$ and let $f(s)$ denote the right-hand side of Equation <a href=\"#eq:curvature-error\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:curvature-error\">[eq:curvature-error]</a>. The comparison from zero is given by the convergent formula $$\\label{eq:fit-error-integral}\n E(s)=\\int_0^s\n       \\exp\\left(-\\int_q^s a(\\xi)\\,d\\xi\\right)f(q)\\,dq.$$ In particular, if $\\phi''(h(s))\\ge\\kappa$ for $0<s\\le r$, then $E\\le0$, $z$ is nonincreasing, and $$\\theta(s)\\ge\\Theta_\\kappa(s,z(r))\\quad(0<s<r),\\qquad\n L(r)\\ge\\mathcal L_\\kappa(r,z(r)).$$ If the curvature difference is not identically zero on $(0,r)$, the last inequality is strict, as are all the preceding angle inequalities for $0<s<r$, and $E(r)<0$. All signs reverse under the assumption $\\phi''(h(s))\\le\\kappa$.",
        "source_id": "PM-143.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

The comparison from zero is given by the convergent formula $$\\label{eq:fit-error-integral}\n E(s)=\\int_0^s\n       \\exp\\left(-\\int_q^s a(\\xi)\\,d\\xi\\right)f(q)\\,dq.$$ In particular, if $\\phi''(h(s))\\ge\\kappa$ for $0<s\\le r$, then $E\\le0$, $z$ is nonincreasing, and $$\\theta(s)\\ge\\Theta_\\kappa(s,z(r))\\quad(0<s<r),\\qquad\n L(r)\\ge\\mathcal L_\\kappa(r,z(r)).$$ If the curvature difference is not identically zero on $(0,r)$, the last inequality is strict, as are all the preceding angle inequalities for $0<s<r$, and $E(r)<0$.         "exact_quote": "The model derivatives $R$ and $S$ are strictly positive for every $(\\lambda,\\kappa,r)\\in\\mathbb R^2\\times(0,\\infty)$. The width identity is $$\\label{eq:fit-width-identity}\n \\kappa P+\\lambda=\\frac{rH_r+H}{r^2-H^2}.$$ With $L_{\\mathrm{mod}}(\\lambda,\\kappa,r)\n=\\operatorname{arctanh}H_r(\\lambda,\\kappa,r)$, differentiating Equation <a href=\"#eq:fit-width-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:fit-width-identity\">[eq:fit-width-identity]</a> with respect to $r$ at fixed $(\\lambda,\\kappa)$ gives $$\\label{eq:fit-R-identity}\n \\kappa R\n =\\frac{r(1-H_r^2)}{r^2-H^2}\n   \\left((L_{\\mathrm{mod}})_r-\\frac{2H}{r^2-H^2}\\right).$$",

        "exact_quote": "For every $r>0$ and every pair $(M,v)$ satisfying $|M|<r$ and $|v|<1$, there is a unique finite pair $(\\lambda,\\kappa)\\in\\mathbb R^2$ such that $$H(\\lambda,\\kappa,r)=M,\\qquad H_r(\\lambda,\\kappa,r)=v.$$ The fitted parameters depend jointly smoothly on $(M,v,r)$ in this open domain.         "anchor": "stmt-model-riccati",
        "exact_quote": "For the quadratic profile $du+bu^2$, with $b>0$, one has $$\\mathcal S\n =2b(m+n\\ell^2)+\\frac{d^2}{2}(\\ell^2-1)\n   -d\\left(\\frac1m+\\frac{\\ell^2}{n}\\right)\n   +\\frac32\\left(\\frac{\\ell^2}{n^2}-\\frac1{m^2}\\right).\n \\label{model:Schwarzian-formula}$$ The arc admits a positive function $V$ such that $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\ell&=\\frac{mV(-m)^2}{nV(n)^2},\\nonumber\\\\\n w&=\\frac1m-\\frac{\\ell}{n}+d(1+\\ell)\n       +2bmV(-m)^2\\int_{-m}^{n}V(y)^{-2}\\,dy.\n \\label{model:variation-formulas}\n\\end{aligned}$$ If $d<0$ and $d^2\\ge4bm$, put $\\nu=(d^2/4-bm)^{1/2}$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-fit-small-width",
        "exact_quote": "At each positive height, locally uniformly in that height as $r$ tends to zero, $$\\label{eq:fit-small-width}\n \\begin{aligned}\n \\kappa(h,r)&=\\phi''(h)+\\frac{2}{7}\\phi'''(h)r^2+O(r^3),\\\\\n \\kappa_r(h,r)&=\\frac{4}{7}\\phi'''(h)r+O(r^2),\\\\\n \\lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\\frac{1}{35}\\phi'''(h)r^4+O(r^5).\n \\end{aligned}$$ More precisely, for every $h_0>0$ there are smooth functions $A$ and $B$ on a neighborhood of $(h_0,0)$ for which, at positive $r$ in that neighborhood, $$\\label{eq:fit-smooth-remainders}\n \\begin{aligned}\n \\lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\\frac{1}{35}\\phi'''(h)r^4+r^5 A(h,r),\\\\\n \\kappa(h,r)&=\\phi''(h)+\\frac{2}{7}\\phi'''(h)r^2+r^3 B(h,r).\n \\end{aligned}$$ Thus the derivative estimate in Equation <a href=\"#eq:fit-small-width\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:fit-small-width\">[eq:fit-small-width]</a> comes from a smooth remainder.",
        "source_id": "PM-143.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-fit-small-width",
          "exact_quote": "At each positive height, locally uniformly in that height as $r$ tends to zero, $$\\label{eq:fit-small-width}\n \\begin{aligned}\n \\kappa(h,r)&=\\phi''(h)+\\frac{2}{7}\\phi'''(h)r^2+O(r^3),\\\\\n \\kappa_r(h,r)&=\\frac{4}{7}\\phi'''(h)r+O(r^2),\\\\\n \\lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\\frac{1}{35}\\phi'''(h)r^4+O(r^5).\n \\end{aligned}$$ More precisely, for every $h_0>0$ there are smooth functions $A$ and $B$ on a neighborhood of $(h_0,0)$ for which, at positive $r$ in that neighborhood, $$\\label{eq:fit-smooth-remainders}\n \\begin{aligned}\n \\lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\\frac{1}{35}\\phi'''(h)r^4+r^5 A(h,r),\\\\\n \\kappa(h,r)&=\\phi''(h)+\\frac{2}{7}\\phi'''(h)r^2+r^3 B(h,r).\n \\end{aligned}$$ Thus the derivative estimate in Equation <a href=\"#eq:fit-small-width\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:fit-small-width\">[eq:fit-small-width]</a> comes from a smooth remainder.",
          "source_id": "PM-143.2"

      "exact_statement": "At each positive height, locally uniformly in that height as $r$ tends to zero, $$\\label{eq:fit-small-width}\n \\begin{aligned}\n \\kappa(h,r)&=\\phi''(h)+\\frac{2}{7}\\phi'''(h)r^2+O(r^3),\\\\\n \\kappa_r(h,r)&=\\frac{4}{7}\\phi'''(h)r+O(r^2),\\\\\n \\lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\\frac{1}{35}\\phi'''(h)r^4+O(r^5).\n \\end{aligned}$$ More precisely, for every $h_0>0$ there are smooth functions $A$ and $B$ on a neighborhood of $(h_0,0)$ for which, at positive $r$ in that neighborhood, $$\\label{eq:fit-smooth-remainders}\n \\begin{aligned}\n \\lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\\frac{1}{35}\\phi'''(h)r^4+r^5 A(h,r),\\\\\n \\kappa(h,r)&=\\phi''(h)+\\frac{2}{7}\\phi'''(h)r^2+r^3 B(h,r).\n \\end{aligned}$$ Thus the derivative estimate in Equation <a href=\"#eq:fit-small-width\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:fit-small-width\">[eq:fit-small-width]</a> comes from a smooth remainder.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-third-derivative",
        "exact_quote": "If $\\phi'''(u)>0$ for every $u>0$, then, for every $h,r>0$, $$\\lambda(h,r)<k(h),\\qquad \\kappa_r(h,r)>0.$$ If $\\phi'''(u)<0$ for every $u>0$, both inequalities reverse: $$\\lambda(h,r)>k(h),\\qquad \\kappa_r(h,r)<0.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-143.2"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-143.2"
        }

If $$\\frac{d}{du}\\left(\\frac{k(u)-d}{u}\\right)>0\n \\qquad\\text{for every }u>0,$$ then $$\\lambda(h,r)-h\\kappa(h,r)<d\n \\qquad\\text{for every }h,r>0.$$ If the displayed derivative is strictly negative everywhere, then $\\lambda(h,r)-h\\kappa(h,r)>d$ everywhere.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **limit cycle** ([source](#eq:original-system)): A limit cycle is the image of a nonconstant periodic solution that is isolated among periodic orbits.
- **characteristics** ([source](#eq:arc-transport)): We call these curves in the $(h,r)$-plane *characteristics*.
- **half-width and relative midpoint at a base height** ([source](#pm-h-3)): At a fixed base height $h$, let $y_<,y_>$ be its endpoints and set $r=(y_>-y_<)/2$ and $M=(y_>+y_<)/2-\phi(h)$.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-max-limit-cycles | number of limit cycles of the Liénard system | ≤ | $2$ | primitive F real polynomial of degree at most five |
| q-midpoint-bound | absolute relative midpoint | < | $r$ | base height below peak, positive width |
| q-width-derivative-bound | absolute width derivative of the midpoint | < | $1$ | base height below peak, positive width |
| q-width-lower-bound | squared half-width | ≥ | $2(t-h)$ | base height below peak |
| q-midpoint-small-width | relative midpoint at small width | = | $\frac13 k(h)r^2+r^3 A(h,r)$ | width tending to zero |
| q-derivative-small-width | width derivative of the midpoint at small width | = | $\frac23 k(h)r+r^2 B(h,r)$ | width tending to zero |
| q-W-small-width | sign quantity W of the model at small width | = | $\frac{16}{45}r^3+O(r^4)$ | width tending to zero, finite parameters on compact sets |
| q-model-ratio-monotone | width derivative of the ratio R over G | ≤ | $0$ | all slopes, curvatures and positive widths |
| q-fitted-curvature-small-width | fitted curvature at small width | = | $\phi''(h)+\frac{2}{7}\phi'''(h)r^2+O(r^3)$ | width tending to zero, locally uniform in height |
| q-fitted-slope-small-width | fitted slope at small width | = | $k(h)-\frac{1}{35}\phi'''(h)r^4+O(r^5)$ | width tending to zero, locally uniform in height |
| q-zero-count-lemma | number of isolated zeros | ≤ | $2$ | open interval |
| q-sharp-inner-derivative | s-derivative of the averaged energy quotient at the inner root | = | $6\pi$ | amplitude equal to one |
| q-sharp-outer-derivative | s-derivative of the averaged energy quotient at the outer root | = | $-48\pi$ | amplitude equal to two |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-arc-data** [claim](#stmt-lem-arc-data) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-arc-data): Differentiate the arc equation, show nondegenerate maxima, use smooth ODE dependence and differentiate the endpoint equations in the peak and the base height. -> endpoint transversality, width bijection, strict midpoint bounds
- **step-variation** [claim](#stmt-lem-variation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-variation): Linearize the arc equation in the profile parameter and integrate with the integrating factor K; interpolate between translated profiles for the comparison. -> first variation formula and midpoint comparison
- **step-small-width-scaling** [claim](#stmt-lem-arc-scaling) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-arc-scaling): Rescale height and endpoint coordinate, embed in a smooth family through the zero profile, apply the implicit function theorem and Taylor's formula with smooth remainder. -> smooth small-width expansions of the arc data
- **step-axis-extension** [claim](#stmt-lem-axis-extension) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-axis-extension): Bound the endpoints, pass to weak limits, continue branches by the inverse equation with parameter variation, and invert the width in the peak. -> transverse ray of peaks and smooth height-zero midpoint
- **step-cycle-matching** [claim](#stmt-prop-cycle-matching) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-cycle-matching): Transform each half-plane orbit into an arc, use monotone half-plane maps to force one arc of each sign, then match endpoints and study the first return map for isolation. -> bijection between periodic orbits and zeros of the matching function
- **step-reference-characteristics** [claim](#stmt-fit-references) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fit-references): Use the entire-profile arc construction, integrate the characteristic equations, differentiate in the peak label and analyze the large-label range. -> smooth reference characteristics and label bijection
- **step-curvature-comparison** [claim](#stmt-lem-curvature-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-curvature-comparison): Subtract the reference slope field transport from the actual slope evolution, solve the error equation by variation of constants from zero. -> signed error and angle inequalities
- **step-conditional-derivative** [claim](#stmt-fit-conditional-derivative) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fit-conditional-derivative): Compare midpoint-fitted characteristics of nearby curvatures, derive a quantitative linear lower bound on the final angle difference, then differentiate the midpoint fit implicitly. -> positive derivative in curvature and positivity of G
- **step-properness** [claim](#stmt-fit-properness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fit-properness): Argue by contradiction with a sequence of growing curvatures, monotone convergence for the integrands and a divergent middle slope drop; use reflection for the other limit. -> unbounded range of the conditional angle
- **step-positive-derivatives** [claim](#stmt-fit-positive-derivatives) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fit-positive-derivatives): Differentiate the width identity, use the peak chain rule and the label derivative of the angle, then recover S from G. -> positivity of R and S
- **step-global-quadratic-fit** [claim](#stmt-thm-quadratic-fit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-quadratic-fit): Combine the monotone surjective conditional angle with unique midpoint fitting, then use the Jacobian determinant G and the inverse function theorem with global uniqueness. -> global diffeomorphism of the fit
- **step-fit-transport** [claim](#stmt-lem-fit-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-fit-transport): Apply the chain rule to the midpoint and its derivative, subtract the quadratic transport identities and solve the resulting linear system with determinant G. -> transport equations of fitted slope and curvature
- **step-endpoint-determinant** [claim](#stmt-model-endpoint-algebra) / [proof](#proof-stmt-model-endpoint-algebra): Change to endpoint coordinates, differentiate the weighted endpoint quantities and compute the determinant and the weighted-operator expressions by exact algebra. -> determinant factorization and zero-sign formula
- **step-riccati-schwarzian** [claim](#stmt-model-riccati) / [proof](#proof-stmt-model-riccati): Linearize the arc equation to a regular linear system, use a common projective label for both endpoints, apply the Schwarzian chain rule, and bound the inverse-square integral by comparison. -> Schwarzian formula, variation formulas and upper bound
- **step-zero-sign** [claim](#stmt-model-zero-sign) / [proof](#proof-stmt-model-zero-sign): Split into three cases by the order of the endpoint lengths and the endpoint ratio, and show the relevant quantity is positive in each case. -> strict positivity at a possible zero of W
- **step-model-inequality** [claim](#stmt-thm-model-inequality) / [proof](#proof-stmt-model-zero-sign-2): Start from positivity of W at small width, exclude a first zero along characteristics by the zero-sign lemma, extend by reflection and continuity to nonpositive curvature. -> nonpositive width derivative of R over G
- **step-small-width-fit** [claim](#stmt-lem-fit-small-width) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-fit-small-width): Invert the nonsingular first-variation matrix of the quadratic family and add the cubic variation, using Taylor factorizations with smooth remainders. -> small-width expansions of fitted slope and curvature
- **step-third-derivative** [claim](#stmt-prop-third-derivative) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-third-derivative): Sign the curvature along a characteristic, solve the error equation, use equal integrals of matched angles to get the slope inequality, then propagate the sign of the curvature width derivative with an integrating factor and the model inequality. -> strict slope and curvature monotonicity
- **step-slope-intercept** [claim](#stmt-prop-intercept) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-intercept): Show the intercept is negative near the peak, prove a curvature inequality at any zero by the midpoint comparison, and exclude a first zero by the sign of its transport derivative. -> strict bound on the fitted slope intercept
- **step-fitted-curvature-range** [claim](#stmt-lem-fitted-curvature-range) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-fitted-curvature-range): Argue by contradiction with the curvature comparison for a fitted curvature outside the range of the actual curvature. -> two-sided range of the fitted curvature
- **step-isolated-zero-count** [claim](#stmt-lem-isolated-zero-count) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-isolated-zero-count): Order the sign sets of the nondecreasing factor and count zeros on each monotone piece; for the second statement use a first zero argument. -> at most two isolated zeros or at most one zero
- **step-main-bound-and-sharpness** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#sec:application): Reduce to the profile decomposition, split into four coefficient-sign cases, bound zeros of the matching function in each, and prove sharpness by an explicit perturbation of the linear center. -> at most two limit cycles, attained

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-sharp-averaged-simple-zeros**: For the explicit quintic damping profile of the sharpness construction, the unperturbed energy-displacement quotient is a polynomial multiple of pi in s. Its zeros at s=1 and s=2 are simple, and its derivatives there are 6 pi and -48 pi.
- **inv-sharp-two-distinct-cycles**: Each simple root of the energy quotient gives a periodic orbit. The two orbits are geometrically distinct and hyperbolic, the inner one repelling and the outer one attracting.
- **inv-main-two-cycle-maximum**: For primitives of degree at most five the system has at most two limit cycles, and some members have two, so the exact maximum is two.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-degree-above-five**: Quote the two-cycle maximum for a primitive of higher degree. -> The source states that counterexamples with more cycles exist in higher degrees, so the bound is not available there.
- **anti-limit-cycle-convention**: Count every periodic orbit of a center as a limit cycle when applying the bound. -> Orbits of a center are not isolated, so counting them misreads the quantity the theorem bounds.
- **anti-degree-of-primitive**: Read the degree hypothesis as a degree bound on the damping polynomial F'. -> A damping polynomial of one degree higher would be admitted by mistake.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The sharpness construction is an explicit finite computation: the energy quotient at zero perturbation is a polynomial determined by exact trigonometric moments, and its values and derivatives at the two roots are rational multiples of pi.

```python
# INPUTS: one line with four exact fractions c1 c3 c5 s: odd coefficients of f(x)=c1*x+c3*x^3+c5*x^5 and the amplitude s.
# PRECONDITIONS: all four tokens parse as exact rationals.
# OUTPUTS: one line with two exact fractions: Q(s,0)/pi and dQ/ds(s,0)/pi, where Q(s,0) is minus the integral over one turn of s*sin t*f(s*sin t).
# TIME: constant number of exact rational operations.
# SPACE: constant.
# ARITHMETIC: exact fractions; trigonometric moments over one turn divided by pi are 1, 3/4, 5/8 for sin^2, sin^4, sin^6.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and exits nonzero.
import sys
from fractions import Fraction as Fr

tok = sys.stdin.read().split()
cs = [Fr(t) for t in tok[:3]]
s = Fr(tok[3])
mom = {1: Fr(1), 3: Fr(3, 4), 5: Fr(5, 8)}
q = Fr(0)
dq = Fr(0)
for j, c in zip((1, 3, 5), cs):
    q -= c * s ** (j + 1) * mom[j]
    dq -= c * (j + 1) * s ** j * mom[j]
print(q, dq)
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an existential upper bound on isolated periodic orbits. It gives no stepwise procedure, so there is no routine to implement. A library could only cite the bound as a documented guarantee. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the result concerns real-time simulation, physics engines or game mechanics. It is a qualitative count of closed orbits for one planar polynomial family, so it gives a simulator nothing to compute. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper builds no executable checker. Its arguments are analytic comparisons of arcs, and the only explicit construction is a single perturbative two-cycle example. A tool would be a bespoke numerical sanity check, not something the paper supplies. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The argument is a chain of monotonicity, comparison and sign-partition lemmas. It does not compute cycle locations, and a bound on isolated zeros of a matching function is not a finite algorithm. No complexity statement exists. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure, index or representation scheme appears in the paper. The objects are smooth profiles, arc endpoints and fitted quadratic parameters, all analytic. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No arithmetic kernel, parallel scheme or circuit implementation of an algorithm is described. Nothing here would map onto an accelerator. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result has no relation to hardness assumptions, protocols or adversaries. It concerns closed orbits of a deterministic planar oscillator. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no learning, data, loss or model-fitting component in the statistical sense. The quadratic fit is a deterministic geometric interpolation of arc data, not an estimator. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective, constraint set or solver appears. The inequalities are used to count zeros, not to optimise anything. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no notion of nodes, messages, consensus or fault tolerance. The system is a single autonomous planar flow. |
| other (certified dynamical-systems and oscillator-circuit analysis) | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | For any planar oscillator that reduces exactly to the stated Liénard form with a polynomial primitive of degree at most five, the theorem certifies that at most two isolated periodic orbits exist anywhere in the plane. A modeller can use this as a stopping criterion once two cycles are found. This is an adaptation of a non-constructive bound, not an algorithm. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-degree-at-most-five** (hypothesis): The two-cycle bound is stated only when the primitive F has degree at most five. The source reports constructions with more cycles in higher degrees, so the bound does not transfer there. Blocked use: ['Quoting the two-cycle maximum for a polynomial primitive of degree six or higher', "Reading the degree hypothesis as a degree bound on the damping polynomial F'"]
- **limit-linear-restoring-force** (formal_scope): The result concerns the autonomous planar system with restoring force exactly x. The half-plane energy coordinate and the arc equation depend on that form of the second equation. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to Liénard systems with a nonlinear restoring force', 'Applying the bound to forced or non-autonomous oscillators']
- **limit-limit-cycle-convention** (edge_case): Only isolated periodic orbits are counted, each image once, regardless of multiplicity, stability or hyperbolicity. Periodic orbits of a center are not limit cycles under this convention. Blocked use: ['Counting every periodic orbit of a center as a limit cycle', 'Using the bound as a bound on hyperbolic cycles only']
- **limit-matching-domain** (hypothesis): The matching correspondence requires a smooth primitive vanishing at zero, and it is posed on the common open interval of transverse height-zero widths. If that interval is empty there are no periodic orbits. Zero intervals and their endpoints do not count as limit cycles. Blocked use: ['Counting nonisolated zeros of the matching function as limit cycles', 'Applying the matching correspondence outside the transverse width interval']
- **limit-nonconstructive-bound** (nonconstructive): The result is an upper bound proved by comparison and sign arguments. It gives no procedure that locates cycles or computes their number for a given polynomial, and no closed formula for their positions. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem as an algorithm that finds the cycles', 'Reading the bound as a numerical certificate of cycle locations']
- **limit-coefficient-case-split** (formal_scope): The upper bound proof splits by the signs of the two odd coefficients after normalizing the quintic coefficient to be nonnegative. Case 2, with a nondecreasing curvature difference, is the only case that allows two cycles. Blocked use: ['Skipping the normalization of the sign of the highest coefficient', 'Assuming each sign case independently allows two cycles']
- **limit-sharpness-perturbative** (approximation): The lower bound is a first-order perturbative construction valid for small positive values of the perturbation parameter in a common range. It shows the maximum is attained, not that every quintic has two cycles. Blocked use: ['Claiming two cycles for every quintic primitive', 'Using the construction outside the small-parameter range']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 143 (`lean/docs/143.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Two limit cycles for quintic Liénard systems](../../preprints/two-limit-cycles-for-quintic-lienard-systems-September-24-2026/two-limit-cycles-for-quintic-lienard-systems-September-24-2026.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> For the quintic Liénard system $x'=y-F(x)$, $y'=-x$, the formalized result proves that every real polynomial $F$ of degree at most five yields at most two limit cycles, and that some such $F$ yields exactly two. Limit cycles are isolated images of nonconstant periodic solutions. No sign, parity, hyperbolicity, or amplitude restriction is imposed.

| declaration | kind |
|---|---|
| `Plane` | abbrev |
| `vectorField` | def |
| `IsSolution` | def |
| `IsPeriodicOrbit` | def |
| `IsLimitCycle` | def |
| `main` | theorem |

Comparator source `QuinticLienard.lean` (1352 bytes, sha256 `cb34f454db432f46902a17036812adde4c73fe276d6d7a8a8b21797ace40d000`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace QuinticLienard

abbrev Plane := ℝ × ℝ

def vectorField (F : Polynomial ℝ) (z : Plane) : Plane :=
  (z.2 - F.eval z.1, -z.1)

def IsSolution (F : Polynomial ℝ) (z : ℝ → Plane) : Prop :=
  ∀ t : ℝ, HasDerivAt z (vectorField F (z t)) t

def IsPeriodicOrbit (F : Polynomial ℝ) (C : Set Plane) : Prop :=
  ∃ (z : ℝ → Plane) (T : ℝ),
    IsSolution F z ∧ 0 < T ∧ Function.Periodic z T ∧
    (∃ s t : ℝ, z s ≠ z t) ∧ C = Set.range z

def IsLimitCycle (F : Polynomial ℝ) (C : Set Plane) : Prop :=
  IsPeriodicOrbit F C ∧
    ∃ U : Set Plane, IsOpen U ∧ C ⊆ U ∧
      ∀ C' : Set Plane, IsPeriodicOrbit F C' → C' ⊆ U → C' = C

open scoped Topology NNReal ContDiff Manifold
open Filter Set
open Set Filter Metric MeasureTheory
open scoped Topology NNReal ContDiff
open scoped Topology ENNReal
open Set Filter MeasureTheory
open Set Filter Asymptotics
open Set Filter Metric
open scoped Topology NNReal
open scoped Topology
open scoped Topology ContDiff
open Set Filter
open scoped Topology ContDiff NNReal

theorem main :
    (∀ F : Polynomial ℝ, F.degree ≤ 5 → {C : Set Plane | IsLimitCycle F C}.encard ≤ 2) ∧
    (∃ F : Polynomial ℝ, F.degree ≤ 5 ∧ {C : Set Plane | IsLimitCycle F C}.encard = 2) := by
  sorry

end QuinticLienard

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


Every classical Liénard system $`\dot x=y-F(x)`$, $`\dot y=-x`$, with <i>F</i> an arbitrary real polynomial of degree at most five, has at most two geometrically distinct isolated periodic orbits, and the bound is attained. This proves the degree-five case of the Lins Neto–de Melo–Pugh conjecture.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [stmt-thm-main](#stmt-thm-main) | theorem | `thm:main` | 1 | If $\deg F\le5$, the system [eq:original-system] has at most two limit cycles in $\mathbb R^2$. |
| [stmt-lem-arc-data](#stmt-lem-arc-data) | lemma | `lem:arc-data` | 2 | For every $0<h<t$, the solution through $(y,u)=(\phi(t),t)$ has exactly two intersections with $u=h$. |
| [stmt-lem-variation](#stmt-lem-variation) | lemma | `lem:variation` | 3 | Consider a smooth one-parameter family of profiles $\phi_\eta$, with $\phi_0=\phi$, and fix a regular base height $h$ and a width $r>0$. |
| [stmt-lem-arc-scaling](#stmt-lem-arc-scaling) | lemma | `lem:arc-scaling` | 4 | Near any fixed positive base height, the small-width arc data have smooth parameter-dependent expansions |
| [stmt-lem-axis-extension](#stmt-lem-axis-extension) | lemma | `lem:axis-extension` | 5 | Each peak $t>0$ has finite limiting endpoints |
| [stmt-prop-cycle-matching](#stmt-prop-cycle-matching) | proposition | `prop:cycle-matching` | 6 | For the system |
| [stmt-auto-remark-1](#stmt-auto-remark-1) | remark | - | - | Remark 7*. Every periodic orbit in Proposition 6 lies inside the open matching domain. |
| [stmt-fit-references](#stmt-fit-references) | lemma | `fit:references` | 8 | The reference functions are smooth in $(\kappa,s,z)$ for $s>0$ and are defined at every finite positive width. |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:original-system"></a>
Consider the classical Liénard system $$\label{eq:original-system}
 \dot x=y-F(x),\qquad \dot y=-x,$$ where $F$ is a real polynomial. A limit cycle is the image of a nonconstant periodic solution that is isolated among periodic orbits. We count each such image once, irrespective of its multiplicity, stability, or hyperbolicity. The periodic orbits of a center are not limit cycles under this convention.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *If $\deg F\le5$, the system <a href="#eq:original-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:original-system">[eq:original-system]</a> has at most two limit cycles in $\mathbb R^2$. Some members of this class have two limit cycles. Thus its exact maximum is two.*

</div>

Here the degree is that of the primitive $F$. The equivalent scalar equation is $$x''+F'(x)x'+x=0,$$ so its damping polynomial has degree at most four and its restoring force is exactly $x$. No parity or coefficient-sign assumption is imposed on $F$.

The problem is a restricted instance of the second part of Hilbert’s sixteenth problem, concerning the number of limit cycles of polynomial planar vector fields. Lins, de Melo, and Pugh proposed the bound $\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor$ for <a href="#eq:original-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:original-system">[eq:original-system]</a> with $\deg F=n\ge1$ <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[LMP](#ref-LMP)]. Rychkov proved the two-cycle bound for odd quintic primitives <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Rychkov](#ref-Rychkov)]. Dumortier, Panazzolo, and Roussarie constructed systems with four cycles and primitive degree seven <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[DPR](#ref-DPR)]. De Maesschalck and Dumortier obtained four cycles already in degree six <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[DMD](#ref-DMD)], and De Maesschalck and Huzak subsequently constructed at least $n-2$ cycles in every degree $n\ge6$ <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[DMH](#ref-DMH)]. These counterexamples leave degree five undecided. Li and Llibre established the unrestricted quartic bound of one cycle <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[LiLlibre](#ref-LiLlibre)]. Llibre and Zhang survey this development and the classical perturbative lower bounds <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[LlibreZhang](#ref-LlibreZhang)].

In degree five, the distinction between a global bound and a bound in a limiting regime is essential. Li and Lu proved that nondegenerate slow–fast cycles have cyclicity at most two <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[LiLu](#ref-LiLu)]. The Introduction of the August 2026 preprint by Chen, Li, Zhang, and Zhang distinguishes that result from the unrestricted polynomial problem and reports the latter as open <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[CLZZ](#ref-CLZZ)]. Hernández Rosales proposes a degree-five four-cycle example for $x''+\mu f(x)x'+x=0$ in Section 9 of <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Rosales](#ref-Rosales)], with $\mu=1/10$ and $f(x)=x^4-10x^2+5$. The proposed system has exactly two limit cycles. Indeed, the change $x=\sqrt5\,u$ transforms its scalar equation into $$u''+\frac12(5u^4-10u^2+1)u'+u=0.$$ This is Odani’s Example 3 with parameters $\widehat\mu=1/2$ and $v=10/3>\sqrt5$, for which exactly two periodic solutions are proved <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Odani](#ref-Odani)]. The discrepancy in the proposed amplitude calculation is that periodicity requires $\int_0^T f(x)\dot x^2\,dt=0$. Substituting $x(t)\approx2X\cos t$ gives $2X^4-10X^2+5=0$, the same equation used for the second proposed pair. Its roots are $X^2=(5\pm\sqrt{15})/2$, not $1$ and $4$. Thus these two leading-order calculations do not yield four distinct cycles.

Two-cycle examples are classical. Besides the perturbative constructions associated with the conjecture <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[LMP](#ref-LMP), [LlibreZhang](#ref-LlibreZhang)], rigorous transversal-region methods have been applied to Rychkov’s family by Gasull, Giacomini, and Grau <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GGG](#ref-GGG)]. Section <a href="#sec:sharpness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sharpness">7</a> supplies a direct lower-bound proof, so the exact maximum follows within this article.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Method.

The even coefficients prevent reduction to the odd quintic family, and a small-amplitude calculation cannot by itself bound distant cycles. Our comparison keeps the two half-orbits separate and uses coordinates available at every transverse amplitude. On each half-plane, $u=x^2/2$ transforms an orbit into an arch satisfying $u_y=\phi(u)-y$, where $\phi(u)=F(\sqrt{2u})$ or $F(-\sqrt{2u})$. The even coefficients give the same quadratic part in both profiles, whereas the odd coefficients change sign. This common part suggests using quadratic profiles as a comparison family. At a fixed base height $h$, let $y_<,y_>$ be its endpoints and set $r=(y_>-y_<)/2$ and $M=(y_>+y_<)/2-\phi(h)$. The first technical step is a global interpolation theorem: every admissible pair $(M,M_r)$ has a unique fit by a profile $\lambda(u-h)+\kappa(u-h)^2/2$. Both its range and its nonvanishing Jacobian are proved in Theorem <a href="#thm:quadratic-fit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:quadratic-fit">13</a>; the fit is available throughout $r>0$, $|M|<r$, and $|M_r|<1$.

The fitted slope and curvature evolve according to the two transport equations of Lemma <a href="#lem:fit-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fit-transport">14</a>. Their coefficients are derivatives of the quadratic midpoint function. The central model estimate, Theorem <a href="#thm:model-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:model-inequality">15</a>, controls how one of these coefficients changes with width while the model parameters are held fixed. Its proof combines endpoint variation, a Riccati linearization, and a Schwarzian identity for the endpoint correspondence. The solution-ratio identity and chain rule are classical projective identities; Ovsienko and Tabachnikov give a concise account <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[OT](#ref-OT)]. Schwarzian derivatives have also been used for cycle bounds through return maps, including the discontinuous systems studied by Coll, Gasull, and Prohens <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[CGP](#ref-CGP)]. Here the map pairs the two endpoints of a quadratic arc, and the model estimate concerns that correspondence.

Transporting the fitted parameters along an arch then turns conditions on $\phi'''$ and on $(\phi'-d)/u$, for a fixed real constant $d$, into monotonicity and separation statements for the fitted curvature and slope intercept. Propositions <a href="#prop:third-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:third-derivative">20</a> and <a href="#prop:intercept" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:intercept">21</a> formulate these comparisons for general smooth profiles, independently of the polynomial application.

For the original system, periodic orbits are zeros of the difference between the two height-zero midpoint functions on one common width interval. The quintic coefficient structure provides a complete sign partition. In the only case allowing two cycles, a positive integrating factor makes the derivative of the matching function have the sign of a nondecreasing function. This bounds isolated zeros even when zeros are multiple or occur in intervals. The proof therefore counts degenerate cycles directly; it does not remove them by a generic perturbation.

Section <a href="#sec:arcs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arcs">2</a> establishes the arc coordinates and the global matching interpretation. Section <a href="#sec:fitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fitting">3</a> proves the global quadratic fit and its transport law. Section <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">4</a> establishes the model inequality, and Section <a href="#sec:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transport">5</a> derives the shape comparisons for general profiles. Section <a href="#sec:application" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:application">6</a> proves the upper bound in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, and Section <a href="#sec:sharpness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sharpness">7</a> proves sharpness.

<a id="sec:arcs"></a>
### Arc coordinates and periodic-orbit matching

<a id="eq:arc-ode"></a>
We first study a general profile $\phi\in C^\infty((0,\infty))$ and the scalar equation $$\label{eq:arc-ode}
  \frac{du}{dy}=\phi(u)-y.$$ Here $y$ is an endpoint coordinate, rather than physical time. For each $t>0$, let $u(y;t)$ be the solution through $$u(\phi(t);t)=t.$$ The parameter $t$ will be the peak height. At a lower height $h$, the two endpoint values of $y$ determine a half-width $r$ and a midpoint $M$, measured relative to $\phi(h)$.

Figure <a href="#fig:arc-coordinates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:arc-coordinates">1</a> illustrates these coordinates. Our first task is to show that width parametrizes every positive-height arc. We then continue the transverse arcs to height zero, where matching the two endpoint pairs counts the periodic orbits.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Global endpoint data and their transport

<a id="stmt-lem-arc-data"></a>
<div id="lem:arc-data" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Global arc coordinates). *For every $0<h<t$, the solution through $(y,u)=(\phi(t),t)$ has exactly two intersections with $u=h$. They are transverse and satisfy $$y_<(h,t)<\phi(h)<y_>(h,t).$$ The part of the solution between them has a unique maximum $u=t$. Set <a id="eq:arc-endpoint-data"></a>$$\label{eq:arc-endpoint-data}
  r(h,t)=\frac{y_>(h,t)-y_<(h,t)}2,\qquad
  M=\frac{y_>(h,t)+y_<(h,t)}2-\phi(h).$$ At fixed $h$, the lower endpoint decreases strictly with $t$, the upper endpoint increases strictly, and both derivatives are nonzero. The map $t\mapsto r(h,t)$ is a smooth increasing bijection from $(h,\infty)$ onto $(0,\infty)$.*

*Consequently $M$ is a smooth function of $(h,r)\in(0,\infty)^2$. Writing $v=M_r$, one has <a id="eq:arc-strict-data"></a>$$\label{eq:arc-strict-data}
  |M(h,r)|<r,\qquad |v(h,r)|<1,\qquad
  r^2\ge 2(t-h).$$ For a fixed width, the corresponding base height is strictly increasing with peak height: $\partial h/\partial t>0$.*

*If $\phi$ is smooth on the whole real line, the same conclusions hold for arbitrary real $h<t$. In that case, at each fixed peak $t$, letting $h$ decrease from $t$ to $-\infty$ parametrizes the full range $0<r<\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-arc-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-arc-data</summary>

*Proof.* Differentiating Equation <a href="#eq:arc-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-ode">[eq:arc-ode]</a> gives $$u_{yy}=\phi'(u)u_y-1.$$ Thus every critical point in the profile domain is a nondegenerate maximum, with $u_{yy}=-1$. On either side of the prescribed peak the height therefore decreases strictly as one moves away from it, until the level $h$ is reached. A second critical point on either branch would require a minimum between two maxima, which is impossible.

These intersections occur at finite $y$. Indeed, while a branch remains in $[h,t]$, the profile is bounded there. For sufficiently large positive $y$, Equation <a href="#eq:arc-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-ode">[eq:arc-ode]</a> gives $u_y<-1$; for sufficiently large negative $y$, it gives $u_y>1$. In the respective outward directions these inequalities force the branch to reach $h$. There is also no finite-$y$ failure of continuation while $u$ stays in the compact interval $[h,t]$. The strict derivative signs at the intersections give the displayed endpoint inequalities.

All the endpoints depend smoothly on $(h,t)$, by smooth dependence of the differential equation and transversality; see, for example, <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Perko](#ref-Perko), Sections 2.2–2.3] for the standard differential-equation facts. To find their peak derivatives, let $\xi(y;t)=\partial_t u(y;t)$, with $y$ held fixed. Differentiating the moving initial condition gives $\xi(\phi(t);t)=1$, because $u_y=0$ at the peak. Moreover, $$\xi_y=\phi'(u)\xi,$$ so $\xi>0$ throughout the arc. Differentiating the endpoint equations now gives $$(y_<)_t=-\frac{\xi(y_<;t)}{r-M}<0,\qquad
  (y_>)_t=\frac{\xi(y_>;t)}{r+M}>0.$$ In particular $r_t>0$. After changing from $t$ to $r$, $$v=\frac{(y_>)_t+(y_<)_t}{(y_>)_t-(y_<)_t}\in(-1,1).$$ The other strict inequality, $|M|<r$, follows directly from the endpoint inequalities.

<a id="eq:arc-width-height"></a>
At fixed $t$, differentiation with respect to $h$ gives $$(y_<)_h=\frac1{r-M},\qquad
  (y_>)_h=-\frac1{r+M}.$$ Hence $$\label{eq:arc-width-height}
  \left.\frac{dr}{dh}\right|_t=-\frac{r}{r^2-M^2},\qquad
  \left.\frac{dM}{dh}\right|_t
       =\frac{M}{r^2-M^2}-\phi'(h).$$ The first identity implies $d(r^2)/dh\le-2$. The nondegenerate maximum gives $r\to0$ as $t\downarrow h$, or as $h\uparrow t$ with the peak fixed. Integrating the inequality yields $r^2\ge2(t-h)$. At fixed $h$, it follows that $r\to\infty$ as $t\to\infty$. This proves the asserted bijection and its smooth inverse. Also, at fixed width, $$\left.\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}\right|_r
    =-\frac{r_t}{r_h}>0.$$ The same proof applies to any compact interval $[h,t]$ in the domain of a smooth profile. For a profile on the whole real line, the bound $r^2\ge2(t-h)$ also proves the last assertion as $h\to-\infty$. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:arc-coordinates">

<figcaption>An arc at base height $h$ and peak height $t$, drawn schematically with $M > 0$. Its endpoint half-width is $r$; its midpoint is measured relative to $ϕ(h)$. Both endpoints move outwards as the peak rises, giving $|M_{r}| < 1$. The two physical half-orbits form a periodic orbit precisely when their height-zero endpoint pairs agree, as proved in Proposition <a href="#prop:cycle-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cycle-matching">6</a>.</figcaption>
</figure>

Visual witness description of PDF page 5 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A Cartesian coordinate system with vertical axis $u$ and horizontal axis $y$. A downward-opening arc begins at height $h$ with left endpoint $y_<$ and right endpoint $y_>$, rising to a peak at point $(\phi(t), t)$ marked with a solid dot. Horizontal dashed lines extend from the $u$-axis at height values $h$ and $t$. Vertical dashed lines drop from $y_<$, the midpoint of the arc base, and $y_>$ down toward the horizontal baseline. Dimension arrows at height $h$ show the half-width $r$ from $y_<$ to the midpoint and $r$ from the midpoint to $y_>$. On the baseline below, ticks mark positions $y_<$, $\phi(h)$, $\phi(h) + M$, and $y_>$. A double-headed horizontal arrow spans the distance between $\phi(h)$ and $\phi(h) + M$, labeled $M$.
```


<a id="eq:arc-transport"></a>
Put $$k(h)=\phi'(h),\qquad q=r^2-M^2,\qquad
  \alpha=\frac rq,\qquad
  D=\partial_h-\alpha\partial_r.$$ By Equation <a href="#eq:arc-width-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-width-height">[eq:arc-width-height]</a>, $D$ differentiates along a fixed-peak arc as its base height increases. We call these curves in the $(h,r)$-plane *characteristics*. Their basic transport identities are $$\label{eq:arc-transport}
  DM=\frac Mq-k(h),\qquad
  Dv=-\frac{2Mr(1-v^2)}{q^2}.$$ For clarity, the second identity includes a commutator term. For any smooth function $f(h,r)$, $$D(f_r)=(Df)_r+\alpha_r f_r,\qquad
  \alpha_r=\frac1q-\frac{2r(r-Mv)}{q^2}.$$ Using the first identity in Equation <a href="#eq:arc-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-transport">[eq:arc-transport]</a> therefore gives $$Dv=\frac{2v}{q}
       -\frac{2(M+rv)(r-Mv)}{q^2}
     =-\frac{2Mr(1-v^2)}{q^2},$$ as claimed. All partial $r$-derivatives here are taken at fixed base height.

<a id="eq:arc-characteristics"></a>
<a id="eq:arc-height-bound"></a>
Introduce the angles $$\theta=\operatorname{arctanh}(M/r),\qquad
  L=\operatorname{arctanh}(v).$$ When a characteristic is parametrized by increasing width $r$, a prime denotes the total derivative along it; thus $f'=-Df/\alpha$. Equation <a href="#eq:arc-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-transport">[eq:arc-transport]</a> first gives $$M'=-\frac Mr+\frac qr k(h).$$ Substituting $M=r\tanh\theta$ and $v=\tanh L$ then yields $$\label{eq:arc-characteristics}
\begin{aligned}
  h'&=-r\operatorname{sech}^2\theta,\\
  \theta'&=k(h)-\frac{\sinh(2\theta)}r,&
  L'&=\frac{\sinh(2\theta)}r,\\
  k(h)'&=-r\operatorname{sech}^2\theta\,\phi''(h).
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, $$\label{eq:arc-height-bound}
  t-\frac{r^2}{2}\le h\le t.$$ The initial values of the angles at width zero, and the corresponding integral formula for $L$, will follow from the local scaling below.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Profile variation and smooth small-width data

<a id="stmt-lem-variation"></a>
<div id="lem:variation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:arc-variation"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Variation at fixed height and width). *Consider a smooth one-parameter family of profiles $\phi_\eta$, with $\phi_0=\phi$, and fix a regular base height $h$ and a width $r>0$. All profiles need only be defined on a neighborhood of the relevant compact arcs. Write $$\psi(u)=\left.\partial_\eta\phi_\eta(u)\right|_{\eta=0},
  \qquad Y=M+\phi(h),\qquad
  K(y)=\exp\left(-\int_{y_<}^{y}\phi'(u(z))\,dz\right).$$ Then the first variation $\delta Y$, with $h,r$ fixed, satisfies $$\label{eq:arc-variation}
 \bigl[(M+r)K(y_>)-(M-r)K(y_<)\bigr]\delta Y
       =\int_{y_<}^{y_>}K(y)\psi(u(y))\,dy.$$ The coefficient of $\delta Y$ is strictly positive. The variation of the relative midpoint is $\delta M=\delta Y-\psi(h)$.*

*Consequently, if two profiles smooth on $(0,\infty)$ satisfy, at a fixed $h>0$, $$\phi_1(u)-\phi_1(h)>\phi_0(u)-\phi_0(h)
  \qquad\text{for every }u>h,$$ then $M_1(h,r)>M_0(h,r)$ for every $r>0$. The analogous comparison holds at any real base height for two entire smooth profiles.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-variation</summary>

*Proof.* Both endpoint coordinates have variation $\delta Y$, since their difference $2r$ is fixed. Let $\delta u$ denote variation at fixed $y$. Differentiating the differential equation and endpoint conditions gives $$(\delta u)_y=\phi'(u)\delta u+\psi(u),\qquad
  \delta u(y_<)=(M-r)\delta Y,\quad
  \delta u(y_>)=(M+r)\delta Y.$$ Multiplication by $K$ and integration prove Equation <a href="#eq:arc-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-variation">[eq:arc-variation]</a>. Its coefficient is positive because $K>0$, $M+r>0$, and $M-r<0$.

For the comparison, first translate each endpoint coordinate by its own base value $\phi_i(h)$. This replaces its profile by $\phi_i(u)-\phi_i(h)$, without changing $M_i$. Interpolate linearly between the translated profiles. The variation vanishes at the base and is strictly positive in every arc interior. Equation <a href="#eq:arc-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-variation">[eq:arc-variation]</a> then makes the derivative of the midpoint strictly positive along the interpolation. Integrating in the interpolation parameter proves the comparison. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:parabolic-variations"></a>
We will use three exact variations at the zero profile. The entire profile version of Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a> permits base height zero. At width $w>0$, the arc is $$u_w(y)=\frac{w^2-y^2}{2},\qquad -w\le y\le w,$$ with $M=0$ and $K=1$. Thus a profile variation $\psi$ with $\psi(0)=0$ gives $$\delta M(0,w)=\frac1{2w}\int_{-w}^{w}\psi(u_w(y))\,dy.$$ Direct polynomial integration gives $$\int_{-w}^{w}u_w\,dy=\frac{2w^3}{3},\qquad
 \int_{-w}^{w}\frac{u_w^2}{2}\,dy=\frac{2w^5}{15},\qquad
 \int_{-w}^{w}\frac{u_w^3}{6}\,dy=\frac{2w^7}{105}.$$ Smooth dependence allows differentiation of these first variations with respect to the positive width $w$. Therefore $$\label{eq:parabolic-variations}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
  \psi(s)&\delta M(0,w)&\delta M_w(0,w)\\ \hline
  s&w^2/3&2w/3\\
  s^2/2&w^4/15&4w^3/15\\
  s^3/6&w^6/105&6w^5/105
\end{array}$$ These constants will determine the local quadratic fit.

<a id="stmt-lem-arc-scaling"></a>
<div id="lem:arc-scaling" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:arc-small-width"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Smooth scaling at zero width). *Near any fixed positive base height, the small-width arc data have smooth parameter-dependent expansions $$\label{eq:arc-small-width}
\begin{aligned}
  M(h,r)&=\frac13 k(h)r^2+r^3 A(h,r),\\
  v(h,r)&=\frac23 k(h)r+r^2 B(h,r),\\
  t(h,r)&=h+r^2\left(\frac12+r\,C(h,r)\right),
\end{aligned}$$ where the remainder functions extend smoothly through $r=0$. The same assertion holds jointly for any finite-dimensional smooth family of profiles. In particular, these expansions may be differentiated, and $\left.\partial h/\partial t\right|_r\to1$ as $r\downarrow0$. The assertions also hold near any regular base height of a profile defined on an open interval containing that height.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-arc-scaling"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-arc-scaling</summary>

*Proof.* For positive $r$, introduce the scaled height and endpoint coordinate $$s=\frac{u-h}{r^2},\qquad
  \zeta=\frac{y-\phi(h)}r.$$ The scaled equation is $$\frac{ds}{d\zeta}=g(s)-\zeta,\qquad
  g(s)=\frac{\phi(h+r^2s)-\phi(h)}r.$$ Its relative midpoint at scaled width $1$ is $M(h,r)/r$. More generally, if the scaled profile is held fixed while its width $w$ varies near $1$, its midpoint is $M(h,rw)/r$. Its width derivative at $w=1$ is therefore $v(h,r)$.

<a id="eq:arc-scaling-profile"></a>
To justify smoothness through zero, introduce a signed auxiliary parameter $\rho$, while keeping the scaled width positive and near $1$. Define $$\label{eq:arc-scaling-profile}
  g(h,\rho,s)
   =\rho s\int_0^1\phi'(h+\tau\rho^2s)\,d\tau.$$ Choose a fixed compact scaled-height interval containing a neighborhood of $[0,1/2]$, and restrict $h$ to a neighborhood of the given regular base height. For sufficiently small $|\rho|$, all arguments of $\phi'$ lie in its smooth domain. Equation <a href="#eq:arc-scaling-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-scaling-profile">[eq:arc-scaling-profile]</a> is then a smooth family through $\rho=0$, equals the preceding difference quotient when $\rho\ne0$, and is the zero profile at $\rho=0$. Negative $\rho$ is only a parameter in this family; no negative physical width is used.

At the zero profile, an arc of scaled peak height $T$ has endpoints $\pm\sqrt{2T}$, and its width derivative with respect to $T$ equals $1$ at $T=1/2$. Smooth dependence of the scaled differential equation, endpoint transversality, and the implicit function theorem therefore give smooth peak, midpoint, and midpoint-width-derivative data at scaled width $1$ for the family in Equation <a href="#eq:arc-scaling-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-scaling-profile">[eq:arc-scaling-profile]</a>. All of this uses only a neighborhood of the compact parabolic arc, so the local profile domain is sufficient.

The derivative of $g$ with respect to $\rho$ at zero is $k(h)s$. Equation <a href="#eq:parabolic-variations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parabolic-variations">[eq:parabolic-variations]</a> consequently gives first derivatives $k(h)/3$ and $2k(h)/3$ for the scaled midpoint and its width derivative. Their values at $\rho=0$ are zero. The scaled peak is $1/2+O(\rho)$. Taylor’s formula with a smooth remainder now gives Equation <a href="#eq:arc-small-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-small-width">[eq:arc-small-width]</a> on setting $\rho=r>0$ and undoing the scaling. It also proves the stated smoothness of the remainder functions, including any smooth profile parameters. Finally $t_h=1+O(r^2)$ at fixed $r$, so the inverse derivative $\partial h/\partial t$ tends to $1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:arc-L-integral"></a>
On a characteristic with fixed smooth peak $t$, Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-scaling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-scaling">4</a> and Equation <a href="#eq:arc-height-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-height-bound">[eq:arc-height-bound]</a> give $$\theta(r)=\frac13 k(t)r+O(r^2),\qquad
  L(r)=\frac23 k(t)r+O(r^2).$$ Both angles therefore tend to zero, and their transport equation integrates to $$\label{eq:arc-L-integral}
  L(r)=\int_0^r\frac{\sinh(2\theta(s))}{s}\,ds.$$ The integrand is bounded near zero. All these expansions are smooth in finite profile parameters. In particular, for a quadratic profile whose slope and curvature at the peak are $z$ and $\kappa$, let $j$ be its slope at the moving base. Translating the peak to height zero and applying the local form of Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-scaling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-scaling">4</a> yields, locally uniformly for finite $(z,\kappa)$, <a id="eq:arc-peak-expansions"></a>$$\label{eq:arc-peak-expansions}
\begin{aligned}
  \theta(s,z,\kappa)&=\frac13 zs+O(s^2),&
  \theta_z(s,z,\kappa)&=\frac13 s+O(s^2),\\
  L(s,z,\kappa)&=\frac23 zs+O(s^2),&
  j(s,z,\kappa)&=z+O(s^2),\qquad j_z\longrightarrow1 .
\end{aligned}$$ Here the differentiated statements follow from smooth remainders, rather than from differentiating an unspecified error bound.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Continuation to a nonsmooth boundary height

For the next lemma, assume additionally that $\phi$ extends continuously to $[0,\infty)$ and that $\phi(0)=0$. Derivatives of $\phi$ need not extend to zero.

<a id="stmt-lem-axis-extension"></a>
<div id="lem:axis-extension" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Transverse extension to the axis). *Each peak $t>0$ has finite limiting endpoints $$a(t)=\lim_{h\downarrow0}y_<(h,t)\le0,\qquad
  b(t)=\lim_{h\downarrow0}y_>(h,t)\ge0.$$ The set $$\mathcal T_\phi=\{t>0:a(t)<0<b(t)\}$$ is either empty or an open ray in $(0,\infty)$. If it is nonempty, $a,b$ are smooth there, with $a_t<0<b_t$, and $$r(0,t)=\frac{b(t)-a(t)}2$$ is a smooth increasing bijection onto an open width ray $\mathcal I_\phi\subset(0,\infty)$. If $\mathcal T_\phi$ is empty, set $\mathcal I_\phi=\varnothing$.*

*On $\mathcal I_\phi$, the limiting midpoint $M(0,r)$ is smooth, and $$|M(0,r)|<r,\qquad |M_r(0,r)|<1.$$ As $h\downarrow0$, all partial $r$-derivatives of $M(h,r)$ converge locally uniformly on $\mathcal I_\phi$ to the corresponding derivatives of $M(0,r)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-axis-extension"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-axis-extension</summary>

*Proof.* At fixed peak, the endpoint derivatives in Equation <a href="#eq:arc-width-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-width-height">[eq:arc-width-height]</a> show that the lower endpoint decreases and the upper endpoint increases as $h\downarrow0$. They remain bounded. To see this explicitly, put $B=\sup_{0\le u\le t}|\phi(u)|$. If an upper endpoint lies beyond $B$, integrate $u_y\le B-y$ from $B$ to that endpoint, using $u(B)\le t$.
