This gives $y_>\le B+\sqrt{2t}$. The inequality $u_y\ge-B-y$ similarly gives $y_<\ge-B-\sqrt{2t}$. The remaining bounds follow from $y_<<\phi(h)<y_>$ and $|\phi(h)|\le B$. The finite limits thus exist, and the signs follow by continuity of $\phi$ at zero.

For two peaks $t_2>t_1$, the positive-height endpoint order passes to the weak limiting order $$a(t_2)\le a(t_1),\qquad b(t_2)\ge b(t_1).$$ Consequently $\mathcal T_\phi$ is upward closed. We next show both its openness and the local smoothness asserted in the lemma.

<a id="eq:arc-inverse-branch"></a>
Fix $t_0\in\mathcal T_\phi$. At a sufficiently small fixed positive level $\delta<t_0$, the two endpoint branches can be continued downwards by $$\label{eq:arc-inverse-branch}
  \frac{dy}{du}=\frac1{\phi(u)-y}=:g(u,y).$$ Choose compact neighborhoods of the limiting endpoints $a(t_0)$ and $b(t_0)$ and make $\delta$ smaller if necessary. On each resulting branch rectangle, $|\phi(u)-y|$ is bounded away from zero for $0\le u\le\delta$. The function $g$ is continuous in $u$, smooth in $y$, and has bounded derivatives of every fixed order with respect to $y$.

At $u=\delta$, the branch initial values are smooth functions of the peak $t$ near $t_0$. The integral equation for Equation <a href="#eq:arc-inverse-branch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-inverse-branch">[eq:arc-inverse-branch]</a> and its parameter-variation equations show that the continued branches and all their $t$ derivatives are continuous down to $u=0$, locally uniformly in $t$. For the first derivative the formula is explicit: $$y_t(u,t)=y_t(\delta,t)
      \exp\left(\int_\delta^u g_y(v,y(v,t))\,dv\right).$$ The exponential factor is positive and bounded away from zero. Thus the two nonzero variation signs persist uniformly near $t_0$. Higher derivatives satisfy linear variation equations with continuous bounded coefficients and forcing built from lower derivatives, which proves the same assertion inductively. In particular, the strict endpoint signs persist for nearby peaks. This proves openness. An open upward-closed subset of $(0,\infty)$ is an open ray, or is empty.

On that ray, $r_t=(b_t-a_t)/2>0$. Passing to the limit in $r^2\ge2(t-h)$ gives $r(0,t)^2\ge2t$; hence the width tends to infinity as the peak does. Its image is therefore one open ray, and its inverse is smooth.

It remains to justify convergence at fixed width, rather than only at fixed peak. Near $t_0$, the preceding bounds give $r_t(h,t)\ge c_0>0$ uniformly for all sufficiently small $h\ge0$. Uniform strict monotonicity brackets the inverse peak $t=T(h,r)$ in a common compact interval for nearby widths. This inverse is continuous in $(h,r)$, smooth in $r$, and $$T_r=\frac1{r_t(h,T(h,r))}.$$ Higher $r$-derivatives are rational expressions in the continuous peak derivatives, with powers of $r_t$ in the denominator. They consequently converge locally uniformly as $h\downarrow0$. Composing the endpoint data with $T(h,r)$, and subtracting $\phi(h)$ from their midpoint, proves the claimed convergence of all $r$-derivatives. No derivative of $\phi$ at zero is needed. Finally the strict endpoint signs give $|M|<r$, and the retained opposite peak derivatives give $|M_r|<1$, exactly as in Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Matching half-arcs and isolated periodic orbits

We now connect the general construction to a smooth classical Liénard system. This step requires only $F\in C^\infty(\mathbb R)$ and $F(0)=0$, not a degree restriction. Define $$\phi_\pm(u)=F(\pm\sqrt{2u}),\qquad u\ge0,$$ and let $M_\pm$ and $\mathcal I_\pm$ be their midpoint data and transverse width domains from Lemma <a href="#lem:axis-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:axis-extension">5</a>. These profiles are smooth for $u>0$, continuous at zero, and vanish there.

<a id="stmt-prop-cycle-matching"></a>
<div id="prop:cycle-matching" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:cycle-matching"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Periodic-orbit matching). *For the system <a id="eq:arc-lienard-system"></a>$$\label{eq:arc-lienard-system}
  \dot x=y-F(x),\qquad \dot y=-x,$$ the images of nonconstant periodic solutions are in one-to-one correspondence with the zeros of $$\label{eq:cycle-matching}
  \Delta(r)=M_+(0,r)-M_-(0,r)$$ on the single open interval $\mathcal I=\mathcal I_+\cap\mathcal I_-$. If this interval is empty, there are no such periodic solutions. Each periodic orbit has exactly one positive and one negative intersection with the $y$-axis. Under the correspondence, isolated periodic orbits are exactly isolated zeros of $\Delta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-cycle-matching"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-cycle-matching</summary>

*Proof.* On either open half-plane, $y$ is strictly monotone in physical time. With $u=x^2/2$, division of the two time derivatives gives $$\frac{du}{dy}=F(x)-y=\phi_\pm(u)-y.$$ Thus any excursion in one half-plane is an arc of Equation <a href="#eq:arc-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-ode">[eq:arc-ode]</a>.

Every nonconstant periodic solution has both signs of $x$, since integrating $\dot y=-x$ over a period gives $\int x\,dt=0$. The only equilibrium is $(0,0)$, and uniqueness prevents a nonconstant orbit from containing it. At any axis intersection, $\dot x=y\ne0$; the intersection is transverse. There are finitely many in a period. Otherwise an accumulation time would give an axis point at which either the crossing derivative is nonzero, contradicting accumulation, or the solution reaches the equilibrium.

A positive axis intersection enters $x>0$, and a negative one enters $x<0$. Between consecutive intersections the transformed height is positive, vanishes at the endpoints, and has a unique positive peak by Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a>. Both endpoint signs are strict, so the arc belongs to the transverse domain of Lemma <a href="#lem:axis-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:axis-extension">5</a>.

For either profile, increasing the positive endpoint strictly decreases the negative endpoint, by peak order. The physical right-half-plane map from positive to negative endpoints is therefore strictly decreasing, and so is the physical left-half-plane map from negative to positive endpoints. Their composition on successive positive intersections is strictly increasing. An increasing map cannot have a finite periodic sequence of distinct points: if one iterate is larger, or smaller, than its predecessor, strict order persists under further iteration. Hence a closed orbit has one distinct positive intersection and one distinct negative intersection, and consists of one arc of each sign.

The two arcs of such an orbit have the same endpoints, so their widths and midpoints agree. This gives a zero of Equation <a href="#eq:cycle-matching" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cycle-matching">[eq:cycle-matching]</a>. Conversely, a zero at width $r$ gives the same endpoints $$a=M_\pm(0,r)-r<0,\qquad
  b=M_\pm(0,r)+r>0$$ for the two arcs. Recover $x$ as $+\sqrt{2u_+(y)}$ on the right and $-\sqrt{2u_-(y)}$ on the left, and introduce physical time by $\dot y=-x$. On each open arc, $\dot x=-u_y=y-F(x)$, as required. The one-sided endpoint derivatives are $$(u_\pm)_y(a)=-a>0,\qquad (u_\pm)_y(b)=-b<0.$$ Thus $u_\pm$ has a simple zero at each endpoint and $dy/\sqrt{2u_\pm(y)}$ is integrable there. The physical travel times are finite. The derivatives of the reconstructed solution extend to the axis with values $(\dot x,\dot y)=(y,0)$. The two arcs consequently join as a solution of the original smooth vector field; uniqueness gives a closed orbit.

Different zeros yield different positive intersections, because $$\frac{d}{dr}\bigl(r+M_+(0,r)\bigr)=1+M_{+,r}(0,r)>0.$$ They therefore yield different geometric orbits. This proves the bijection.

Finally fix a matched orbit, its positive intersection $(0,s_0)$, and its first positive return time $T>0$. On a short positive-axis section $S$ around $s_0$, the first return ordinate $P(s)$ and time $\tau(s)$ are smooth and satisfy $\tau(s)\to T$ as $s\to s_0$. To see that these are the first returns, take disjoint small time windows about the reference axis crossings. On the intervening compact time segments, $|x|$ is bounded away from zero. Smooth flow dependence and transversality give exactly the nearby crossings in those windows and no others. The function $s=r+M_+(0,r)$ is a local smooth coordinate with positive derivative. In this coordinate, $$\Delta(r)=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad P(s)=s$$ near the reference root.

If distinct roots tend to the reference root, the corresponding section points tend to $s_0$, their return times tend to $T$, and smooth flow dependence on a common compact time interval makes their entire orbit images tend to the reference image. They are distinct by the preceding monotonicity. Hence a nonisolated root cannot give an isolated periodic orbit.

For the converse, suppose the root is isolated and shrink $S$ so that $s_0$ is its only return fixed point. If $\Phi_\tau$ denotes the local flow, the map $(s,\tau)\mapsto\Phi_\tau(0,s)$ is a local diffeomorphism near each point $(s_0,\tau)$, $0\le\tau\le T$, since the initial section is transverse. Finitely many such flow neighborhoods cover the compact reference orbit and give an open neighborhood $U$ whose every point lies on a trajectory meeting $S$. Any periodic orbit meeting $U$ therefore meets $S$. Its unique positive-axis intersection must be a fixed point of the local return map, and hence equals $s_0$. Uniqueness then makes it the reference orbit. This proves isolation, without an assumption on hyperbolicity or a global bound on periods. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 7*. Every periodic orbit in Proposition <a href="#prop:cycle-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cycle-matching">6</a> lies inside the open matching domain. A limiting endpoint at the equilibrium is not an additional periodic orbit. Nonisolated zeros, including a zero interval and its endpoints within the domain, do not count as limit cycles. Isolated multiple zeros remain covered by the same correspondence.

</div>

<a id="sec:fitting"></a>
### Quadratic fitting and its transport law

<a id="eq:fit-open-data"></a>
<a id="eq:fit-PQ-positive"></a>
For the reference profile $$q_{\lambda,\kappa}(u)=\lambda u+\frac{\kappa}{2}u^2,
 \qquad (\lambda,\kappa)\in\mathbb R^2,$$ let $H(\lambda,\kappa,r)$ be its midpoint data at base height zero and width $r>0$. The entire-profile version of Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a> makes this definition valid for every such triple. Set <a id="eq:fit-notation"></a>$$\label{eq:fit-notation}
 P=H_\lambda,\qquad Q=H_\kappa,\qquad
 R=P_r,\qquad S=Q_r,\qquad G=PS-QR.$$ Unless stated otherwise, a subscript on a model function denotes an ordinary partial derivative with its other arguments held fixed. Smooth arc dependence gives smoothness of these functions for $r>0$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a> gives $$\label{eq:fit-open-data}
 |H|<r,\qquad |H_r|<1.$$ The variations $u$ and $u^2/2$ of the profile are strictly positive in the interior of every base-zero arc. The positive denominator in Lemma <a href="#lem:variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:variation">3</a> therefore gives, already at this stage, $$\label{eq:fit-PQ-positive}
 P>0,\qquad Q>0.$$

<a id="eq:fit-reflection"></a>
Reflecting the endpoint coordinate $y$ negates the profile and the midpoint, while preserving the width. Consequently $$\label{eq:fit-reflection}
 H(-\lambda,-\kappa,r)=-H(\lambda,\kappa,r),\qquad
 H_r(-\lambda,-\kappa,r)=-H_r(\lambda,\kappa,r).$$

The goal of this section is Theorem <a href="#thm:quadratic-fit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:quadratic-fit">13</a>: at each positive width, $(H,H_r)$ gives global coordinates on the model parameter plane. We first fit the midpoint by varying the slope at fixed curvature. Keeping the midpoint and width fixed, we then vary the curvature and show that the model width derivative increases through $(-1,1)$. The final subsection expresses the transport of a general smooth profile in these fitted coordinates.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Reference characteristics and curvature comparison

It is useful to specify a quadratic arc by its slope at the peak. For $\kappa,z\in\mathbb R$, take the entire profile $zu+\kappa u^2/2$ with peak height zero. As the base moves downwards from that peak, let $$j_\kappa(s,z),\qquad \Theta_\kappa(s,z),\qquad
 \mathcal L_\kappa(s,z)$$ denote its base slope, its angle $\operatorname{arctanh}(M/s)$, and $\operatorname{arctanh}v$, respectively, at characteristic width $s>0$. Here $v=M_r$ is the width derivative at fixed base height of the given profile, evaluated on the characteristic. Translating the height coordinate and subtracting the profile value at the translated origin shows that these relative data describe every quadratic characteristic with peak slope $z$ and curvature $\kappa$.

<a id="stmt-fit-references"></a>
<div id="fit:references" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fit-reference-system"></a>
<a id="eq:fit-reference-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:fit-L-z"></a>
**Lemma 8**. *The reference functions are smooth in $(\kappa,s,z)$ for $s>0$ and are defined at every finite positive width. They satisfy $$\label{eq:fit-reference-system}
 \partial_s j_\kappa=-s\kappa\operatorname{sech}^2\Theta_\kappa,
 \qquad
 \partial_s\Theta_\kappa
   =j_\kappa-\frac{\sinh(2\Theta_\kappa)}s,
 \qquad
 \partial_s\mathcal L_\kappa
   =\frac{\sinh(2\Theta_\kappa)}s,$$ with limiting values $j_\kappa(0,z)=z$ and $\Theta_\kappa(0,z)=\mathcal L_\kappa(0,z)=0$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:fit-reference-bounds}
 |j_\kappa(s,z)-z|\le\frac{|\kappa|s^2}{2},\qquad
 \partial_z j_\kappa(s,z)>0,\qquad
 \partial_z\Theta_\kappa(s,z)>0.$$ For each fixed $s>0$ and $\kappa$, the map $z\mapsto\Theta_\kappa(s,z)$ is a bijection onto $\mathbb R$. Finally, $$\label{eq:fit-L-z}
 \partial_z\mathcal L_\kappa(s,z)
  =\int_0^s
    \frac{2\cosh(2\Theta_\kappa(q,z))}{q}
       \partial_z\Theta_\kappa(q,z)\,dq>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fit-references"><summary>Proof of stmt-fit-references</summary>

*Proof.* The entire-profile construction in Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a> gives an arc at every lower base height. Its width tends to zero at the peak and tends to infinity as the base tends to minus infinity, since $s^2\ge 2(t-h)$. Its strictly negative base derivative gives the smooth inversion from base height to any $s>0$, including smooth dependence on the quadratic coefficients. Thus no finite-width existence claim is being inferred from the singular equations at $s=0$. Equation <a href="#eq:arc-characteristics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-characteristics">[eq:arc-characteristics]</a> gives Equation <a href="#eq:fit-reference-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-reference-system">[eq:fit-reference-system]</a> and, by integration of the slope equation, the first bound in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-reference-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-reference-bounds">[eq:fit-reference-bounds]</a>.

For $\kappa\ne0$, consider instead the single profile $\kappa u^2/2$. Let $\bar h(t,s)$ be its base height when the peak height is $t$ and the width is $s$. Its peak slope is $z=\kappa t$, and its base slope is $\kappa\bar h(t,s)$. Therefore $$\partial_z j_\kappa(s,z)=\partial_t\bar h(t,s)>0$$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a>. This calculation is valid for either sign of $\kappa$. For $\kappa=0$, one has $j_0(s,z)=z$.

<a id="eq:fit-reference-small-width"></a>
The smooth scaling in Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-scaling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-scaling">4</a> gives, locally uniformly in the finite parameters, $$\label{eq:fit-reference-small-width}
 \Theta_\kappa(s,z)=\frac{z}{3}s+O(s^2),\qquad
 \partial_z\Theta_\kappa(s,z)=\frac{s}{3}+O(s^2).$$ Differentiating the angle equation at positive width gives $$\partial_s(\partial_z\Theta_\kappa)
  =\partial_z j_\kappa
     -\frac{2\cosh(2\Theta_\kappa)}s\partial_z\Theta_\kappa.$$ The initial sign supplied by Equation <a href="#eq:fit-reference-small-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-reference-small-width">[eq:fit-reference-small-width]</a> and the positive forcing prove $\partial_z\Theta_\kappa>0$ by the scalar integrating-factor formula.

To verify the range, fix $r>0$. For $0<s\le r$, $$j_\kappa(s,z)\ge z-\frac{|\kappa|r^2}{2}.$$ For sufficiently large positive $z$, the angle is positive near zero and cannot cross zero downwards, since its derivative there would be positive. Given $N>0$, on $[r/2,r]$ and whenever $0\le\Theta_\kappa\le N$, $$\partial_s\Theta_\kappa
 \ge z-\frac{|\kappa|r^2}{2}-\frac{2\sinh(2N)}r.$$ For large enough $z$, this lower bound forces a crossing of $N$ within that interval if the angle has not already exceeded $N$; it also prohibits a downward crossing of $N$. Thus $\Theta_\kappa(r,z)\to+\infty$ as $z\to+\infty$. Applying the same argument to $(-j,-\Theta,-\kappa)$ proves the negative limit as $z\to-\infty$. Strict monotonicity and smoothness give the asserted bijection.

Integrating the last equation in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-reference-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-reference-system">[eq:fit-reference-system]</a> gives $$\mathcal L_\kappa(s,z)
   =\int_0^s\frac{\sinh(2\Theta_\kappa(q,z))}{q}\,dq.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:fit-reference-small-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-reference-small-width">[eq:fit-reference-small-width]</a>, including its locally uniform parameter derivative, bounds the differentiated integrand near zero. Differentiation under this integral is therefore valid and yields Equation <a href="#eq:fit-L-z" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-L-z">[eq:fit-L-z]</a>. Its integrand is strictly positive at every positive width. ◻

</details>

For $s>0$, let $z=z(\kappa,s,\theta)$ be the inverse supplied by Lemma <a href="#fit:references" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:references">8</a>, and define the reference slope field <a id="eq:fit-slope-field"></a>$$\label{eq:fit-slope-field}
 J_\kappa(s,\theta)
   =j_\kappa\bigl(s,z(\kappa,s,\theta)\bigr).$$ It is smooth, and <a id="eq:fit-field-positive"></a>$$\label{eq:fit-field-positive}
 (J_\kappa)_\theta
   =\frac{\partial_z j_\kappa}{\partial_z\Theta_\kappa}>0.$$ The following comparison will also be used for nonquadratic profiles.

<a id="stmt-lem-curvature-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:curvature-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:curvature-error"></a>
<a id="eq:label-derivative"></a>
<a id="eq:fit-error-integral"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Curvature comparison). *Let a smooth profile $\phi$ have an arc with peak height $t$, and parameterize that arc from its peak to a final width $r>0$ by $s\in(0,r]$. Assume $\phi$ is smooth on a neighborhood of the traversed height interval. Write $h(s)$, $\theta(s)$, and $L(s)$ for its characteristic data, and set $k=\phi'$. For any fixed $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, define $$E(s)=k(h(s))-J_\kappa(s,\theta(s)),\qquad
 \theta(s)=\Theta_\kappa(s,z(s)).$$ Then $z(s)\to k(t)$, $E(s)\to0$ as $s\downarrow0$, and $$\begin{aligned}
 E'(s)+(J_\kappa)_\theta(s,\theta(s))E(s)
  &=-s\bigl(\phi''(h(s))-\kappa\bigr)
       \operatorname{sech}^2\theta(s),
       \label{eq:curvature-error}\\
 z'(s)&=\frac{E(s)}{\partial_z\Theta_\kappa(s,z(s))}.
       \label{eq:label-derivative}
\end{aligned}$$ Put $a(s)=(J_\kappa)_\theta(s,\theta(s))>0$ and let $f(s)$ denote the right-hand side of Equation <a href="#eq:curvature-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-error">[eq:curvature-error]</a>. The comparison from zero is given by the convergent formula $$\label{eq:fit-error-integral}
 E(s)=\int_0^s
       \exp\left(-\int_q^s a(\xi)\,d\xi\right)f(q)\,dq.$$ In particular, if $\phi''(h(s))\ge\kappa$ for $0<s\le r$, then $E\le0$, $z$ is nonincreasing, and $$\theta(s)\ge\Theta_\kappa(s,z(r))\quad(0<s<r),\qquad
 L(r)\ge\mathcal L_\kappa(r,z(r)).$$ If the curvature difference is not identically zero on $(0,r)$, the last inequality is strict, as are all the preceding angle inequalities for $0<s<r$, and $E(r)<0$. All signs reverse under the assumption $\phi''(h(s))\le\kappa$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-curvature-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-curvature-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Along every reference characteristic, the definition of the field and Equation <a href="#eq:fit-reference-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-reference-system">[eq:fit-reference-system]</a> give $$(J_\kappa)_s+(J_\kappa)_\theta
       \left(J_\kappa-\frac{\sinh(2\theta)}s\right)
   =-s\kappa\operatorname{sech}^2\theta.$$ Subtracting this identity from the evolution of the actual slope in Equation <a href="#eq:arc-characteristics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-characteristics">[eq:arc-characteristics]</a> proves Equation <a href="#eq:curvature-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-error">[eq:curvature-error]</a>. Differentiating $\theta(s)=\Theta_\kappa(s,z(s))$ and subtracting the two angle equations gives Equation <a href="#eq:label-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-derivative">[eq:label-derivative]</a>.

The small-width expansions give $\theta(s)/s\to k(t)/3$. For each $\varepsilon>0$, the reference angles with labels $k(t)-\varepsilon$ and $k(t)+\varepsilon$ bracket this actual angle at all sufficiently small positive widths. The strict monotonicity in Lemma <a href="#fit:references" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:references">8</a> therefore implies $z(s)\to k(t)$. The bound $\lvert j_\kappa(s,z(s))-z(s)\rvert\le |\kappa|s^2/2$ then proves $E(s)\to0$.

For $0<\varepsilon<s$, variation of constants gives $$E(s)=E(\varepsilon)
       \exp\left(-\int_\varepsilon^s a\right)
      +\int_\varepsilon^s
       \exp\left(-\int_q^s a\right)f(q)\,dq.$$ The first term tends to zero because $a>0$ and $E(\varepsilon)\to0$. Also $f(q)=O(q)$ near the smooth peak, and the exponential is between zero and one for $0<q\le s$. Dominated convergence proves Equation <a href="#eq:fit-error-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-error-integral">[eq:fit-error-integral]</a>, without any assumption that $a$ is integrable at zero.

If the curvature difference is nonnegative, then $f\le0$, so $E\le0$ and $z'\le0$. This proves the weak angle inequality by Lemma <a href="#fit:references" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:references">8</a>. If the difference is positive somewhere in $(0,r)$, continuity gives a subinterval on which $f<0$. Equation <a href="#eq:fit-error-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-error-integral">[eq:fit-error-integral]</a> makes $E$ strictly negative from the end of that subinterval through $r$. Thus $z(s)>z(r)$ for every $s<r$, giving strict angle inequalities. The integral formula for $L$ in Equation <a href="#eq:arc-L-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-L-integral">[eq:arc-L-integral]</a> then gives the stated strict inequality at the final width. Negating the inequalities proves the other case. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Midpoint fitting and the positive conditional derivative

<a id="eq:fit-midpoint-slope"></a>
<a id="eq:fit-conditional-data"></a>
For a prescribed $r>0$, $|M|<r$, and curvature $\kappa$, Lemma <a href="#fit:references" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:references">8</a> gives a unique peak label satisfying $\Theta_\kappa(r,z)=\operatorname{arctanh}(M/r)$. Translate that reference arc to base height zero and subtract its profile value at the base. The resulting quadratic has base slope $\lambda=j_\kappa(r,z)$, curvature $\kappa$, and midpoint $M$. Conversely, each base-zero quadratic arc has a finite peak and arises in this way. Since $P>0$, midpoint fitting is unique. We denote its slope by $$\label{eq:fit-midpoint-slope}
 \lambda_*(r,M,\kappa),\qquad
 H(\lambda_*(r,M,\kappa),\kappa,r)=M.$$ The implicit-function theorem and uniqueness make $\lambda_*$ jointly smooth throughout $r>0$, $|M|<r$, $\kappa\in\mathbb R$. Define the conditional derivative and its angle by $$\label{eq:fit-conditional-data}
 \widehat v(r,M,\kappa)
  =H_r(\lambda_*(r,M,\kappa),\kappa,r),\qquad
 \widehat L(r,M,\kappa)=\operatorname{arctanh}\widehat v(r,M,\kappa).$$ Both functions are smooth, by Equation <a href="#eq:fit-open-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-open-data">[eq:fit-open-data]</a>.

<a id="stmt-fit-conditional-derivative"></a>
<div id="fit:conditional-derivative" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *For every $r>0$, $|M|<r$, and $\kappa\in\mathbb R$, <a id="eq:fit-conditional-positive"></a>$$\label{eq:fit-conditional-positive}
 \partial_\kappa\widehat L(r,M,\kappa)>0,
 \qquad
 \partial_\kappa\widehat v(r,M,\kappa)=\frac GP>0,$$ where the model derivatives on the right are evaluated at the midpoint fit. In particular, $G>0$ for every model arc.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fit-conditional-derivative"><summary>Proof of stmt-fit-conditional-derivative</summary>

<a id="eq:fit-interior-order"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $r,M,\kappa$. For $\delta>0$, let $\theta_\delta(s)$ be the characteristic of the midpoint-fitted model of curvature $\kappa+\delta$; let $\theta_0$ be the one of curvature $\kappa$. Their final angles are equal. Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature-comparison">9</a> gives $$\label{eq:fit-interior-order}
 \theta_\delta(s)>\theta_0(s)\qquad(0<s<r).$$ It follows already that the conditional $\widehat L$ is strictly increasing. We prove a quantitative first-order bound to obtain the asserted strict derivative.

<a id="eq:fit-error-quantitative"></a>
Use the field $J_\kappa$ to compare the curvature-$\kappa+\delta$ characteristic, and write its error as $E_\delta$. Then $$E_\delta'+a_\delta E_\delta
    =-\delta s\operatorname{sech}^2\theta_\delta,
 \qquad
 a_\delta(s)=(J_\kappa)_\theta(s,\theta_\delta(s))>0,
 \qquad E_\delta\le0.$$ Choose $0<a<b<c<r$. The already established smooth midpoint fit and smooth finite-parameter arc dependence imply that, for all sufficiently small $\delta\ge0$, there are constants $A,q_0>0$, independent of $\delta$, such that on $[a,r]$, $$a_\delta(s)\le A,\qquad
 s\operatorname{sech}^2\theta_\delta(s)\ge q_0.$$ Variation of constants starting at $a$, using $E_\delta(a)\le0$, consequently gives $$\label{eq:fit-error-quantitative}
 E_\delta(s)\le-c_0\delta\quad(b\le s\le r),
 \qquad c_0=q_0(b-a)e^{-Ar}>0.$$ This estimate needs no bound uniform in $\delta$ near $s=0$. The sign at $a$ follows from the from-zero comparison separately for every $\delta>0$.

Let $z_\delta(s)$ be the reference label defined by $\theta_\delta(s)=\Theta_\kappa(s,z_\delta(s))$, and let $z_0$ be the constant label of the curvature-$\kappa$ arc. Final angle matching gives $z_\delta(r)=z_0$. Smoothness of the inverse reference-label map puts all these labels, for $b\le s\le r$ and small $\delta\ge0$, in a common compact set. Enlarge the set to include the intervals between $z_0$ and $z_\delta(s)$. Lemma <a href="#fit:references" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:references">8</a> then gives constants $m_0,B_0>0$ with $$m_0\le\partial_z\Theta_\kappa(s,z)\le B_0$$ throughout that set. Equations <a href="#eq:label-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-derivative">[eq:label-derivative]</a> and <a href="#eq:fit-error-quantitative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-error-quantitative">[eq:fit-error-quantitative]</a> yield $$z_\delta'(s)\le-\frac{c_0\delta}{B_0}\quad(b\le s\le r).$$ Integrating backwards from the common final label gives, for $b\le s\le c$, $$z_\delta(s)-z_0\ge\frac{c_0\delta(r-c)}{B_0},\qquad
 \theta_\delta(s)-\theta_0(s)
    \ge\frac{m_0c_0\delta(r-c)}{B_0}.$$ Since the derivative of $\sinh(2\theta)$ is at least $2$, the integral formulas for the final $L$-values and Equation <a href="#eq:fit-interior-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-interior-order">[eq:fit-interior-order]</a> now imply <a id="eq:fit-L-quantitative"></a>$$\label{eq:fit-L-quantitative}
 \widehat L(r,M,\kappa+\delta)-\widehat L(r,M,\kappa)
 \ge \frac{2m_0c_0(r-c)}{B_0}\log\!\frac cb\;\delta.$$ Thus ordinary smoothness of the conditional function at the final positive width proves $\partial_\kappa\widehat L>0$. No differentiation of the improper $L$-integral is used here.

Finally, implicit differentiation of Equation <a href="#eq:fit-midpoint-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-midpoint-slope">[eq:fit-midpoint-slope]</a> gives $\partial_\kappa\lambda_*=-Q/P$, and hence $$\partial_\kappa\widehat v
     =S-R\frac QP=\frac GP
     =(1-\widehat v^2)\partial_\kappa\widehat L>0.$$ Since every model arc is its own midpoint fit, this proves $G>0$ everywhere. The argument used $P>0$, but did not assume the sign or nonvanishing of $G$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The full range of the conditional fit

<a id="stmt-fit-properness"></a>
<div id="fit:properness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11**. *For fixed $r>0$ and $|M|<r$, <a id="eq:fit-properness"></a>$$\label{eq:fit-properness}
 \lim_{\kappa\to+\infty}\widehat L(r,M,\kappa)=+\infty,
 \qquad
 \lim_{\kappa\to-\infty}\widehat L(r,M,\kappa)=-\infty.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fit-properness"><summary>Proof of stmt-fit-properness</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose the first limit fails. Strict monotonicity then makes $\widehat L(r,M,\kappa)$ bounded above as $\kappa\to+\infty$. Choose positive curvatures $\kappa_n\uparrow+\infty$, and denote the matched-final-angle characteristics by $\theta_n(s)$, $j_n(s)$, and their final $L$-values by $L_n$. Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature-comparison">9</a> gives pointwise increase of $\theta_n(s)$ for $0<s<r$. Put $$f_n(s)=\frac{\sinh(2\theta_n(s))}{s}.$$ Each $f_n$ is integrable at zero by its smooth-peak expansion, and $$f_n-f_1\ge0,\qquad
 \int_0^r(f_n-f_1)\,ds=L_n-L_1\le C$$ for some finite $C$. Monotone convergence shows that the limiting $f_n$, and therefore the limiting $\theta_n$, are finite almost everywhere in $(0,r)$.

<a id="eq:fit-outer-averages"></a>
Choose four such widths $0<a<b<c<d<r$. The angle equation gives $$\begin{aligned}
 A_n:=\int_a^b j_n(s)\,ds
    &=\theta_n(b)-\theta_n(a)+\int_a^b f_n(s)\,ds,\\
 B_n:=\int_c^d j_n(s)\,ds
    &=\theta_n(d)-\theta_n(c)+\int_c^d f_n(s)\,ds.
\end{aligned}$$ Both sequences are bounded above and below: the four endpoint angle sequences are bounded, and for every subinterval $I$, $$0\le\int_I(f_n-f_1)\,ds\le C.$$ Choose constants $A_*,B_*$ with $A_n\le A_*$ and $B_n\ge B_*$. Because $\kappa_n>0$, the slope $j_n$ is decreasing, so $$\label{eq:fit-outer-averages}
 j_n(b)\le\frac{A_n}{b-a}\le\frac{A_*}{b-a},\qquad
 j_n(c)\ge\frac{B_n}{d-c}\ge\frac{B_*}{d-c}.$$ Thus $j_n(b)-j_n(c)$ has a uniform upper bound.

<a id="eq:fit-middle-drop"></a>
On the other hand, its exact evolution is $$\label{eq:fit-middle-drop}
 j_n(b)-j_n(c)
    =\kappa_n\int_b^c s\operatorname{sech}^2\theta_n(s)\,ds.$$ The integrand is bounded by $s$ and converges almost everywhere to a strictly positive function, because the limiting angle is finite almost everywhere. Dominated convergence gives a strictly positive limiting integral in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-middle-drop" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-middle-drop">[eq:fit-middle-drop]</a>. Its right-hand side therefore tends to infinity, contradicting Equation <a href="#eq:fit-outer-averages" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-outer-averages">[eq:fit-outer-averages]</a>. This proof uses no uniform angle bound on the middle interval.

For the second limit, Equation <a href="#eq:fit-reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-reflection">[eq:fit-reflection]</a> and uniqueness of midpoint fitting give $$\lambda_*(r,-M,-\kappa)=-\lambda_*(r,M,\kappa),\qquad
 \widehat L(r,M,\kappa)=-\widehat L(r,-M,-\kappa).$$ The positive-curvature result holds for every fixed midpoint, including $-M$. It therefore proves the negative-curvature limit at the original fixed midpoint $M$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The remaining derivative signs and the global inverse

<a id="stmt-fit-positive-derivatives"></a>
<div id="fit:positive-derivatives" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fit-width-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:fit-R-identity"></a>
**Lemma 12**. *The model derivatives $R$ and $S$ are strictly positive for every $(\lambda,\kappa,r)\in\mathbb R^2\times(0,\infty)$. The width identity is $$\label{eq:fit-width-identity}
 \kappa P+\lambda=\frac{rH_r+H}{r^2-H^2}.$$ With $L_{\mathrm{mod}}(\lambda,\kappa,r)
=\operatorname{arctanh}H_r(\lambda,\kappa,r)$, differentiating Equation <a href="#eq:fit-width-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-width-identity">[eq:fit-width-identity]</a> with respect to $r$ at fixed $(\lambda,\kappa)$ gives $$\label{eq:fit-R-identity}
 \kappa R
 =\frac{r(1-H_r^2)}{r^2-H^2}
   \left((L_{\mathrm{mod}})_r-\frac{2H}{r^2-H^2}\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fit-positive-derivatives"><summary>Proof of stmt-fit-positive-derivatives</summary>

*Proof.* At base height $h$, translating the quadratic and subtracting its base value changes its slope to $\lambda+\kappa h$ and leaves its curvature unchanged. Its midpoint data are therefore $H(\lambda+\kappa h,\kappa,r)$. At $h=0$, the first transport identity in Equation <a href="#eq:arc-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-transport">[eq:arc-transport]</a> becomes $$\kappa P-\frac r{r^2-H^2}H_r
     =\frac H{r^2-H^2}-\lambda,$$ which is Equation <a href="#eq:fit-width-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-width-identity">[eq:fit-width-identity]</a>. For completeness, put $D_0=r^2-H^2$ and $v=H_r$. Differentiating at fixed $\lambda,\kappa$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \kappa R
 &=\frac{rv_r+2v}{D_0}
      -\frac{2(r-Hv)(rv+H)}{D_0^2}\\
 &=\frac{r(1-v^2)}{D_0}
      \left(\frac{v_r}{1-v^2}-\frac{2H}{D_0}\right),
\end{aligned}$$ proving Equation <a href="#eq:fit-R-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-R-identity">[eq:fit-R-identity]</a>.

Let $t=t(\lambda,\kappa,r)>0$ be the peak of this base-zero model arc. Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a> gives $t_r>0$ at fixed model parameters. Its peak slope is $z=\lambda+\kappa t$, so $$L_{\mathrm{mod}}(\lambda,\kappa,r)
       =\mathcal L_\kappa(r,\lambda+\kappa t(\lambda,\kappa,r)).$$ The chain rule and Equation <a href="#eq:fit-reference-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-reference-system">[eq:fit-reference-system]</a> yield <a id="eq:fit-base-width-L"></a>$$\label{eq:fit-base-width-L}
 (L_{\mathrm{mod}})_r
    =\frac{2H}{r^2-H^2}
       +\kappa t_r\partial_z\mathcal L_\kappa(r,z).$$ For $\kappa\ne0$, substitution into Equation <a href="#eq:fit-R-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-R-identity">[eq:fit-R-identity]</a> and cancellation of $\kappa$ give $$R=\frac{r(1-H_r^2)}{r^2-H^2}
       t_r\partial_z\mathcal L_\kappa(r,z)>0$$ by Lemma <a href="#fit:references" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:references">8</a>. This cancellation is valid for either sign of $\kappa$. For $\kappa=0$, the peak slope is $z=\lambda$, so direct parameter differentiation gives $$R=\partial_\lambda H_r
      =(1-H_r^2)\partial_z\mathcal L_0(r,\lambda)>0.$$ Finally, $S=(G+QR)/P>0$ by Equation <a href="#eq:fit-PQ-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-PQ-positive">[eq:fit-PQ-positive]</a> and Lemma <a href="#fit:conditional-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:conditional-derivative">10</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-thm-quadratic-fit"></a>
<div id="thm:quadratic-fit" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:fit-joint-map"></a>
**Theorem 13** (Global quadratic fit). *For every $r>0$ and every pair $(M,v)$ satisfying $|M|<r$ and $|v|<1$, there is a unique finite pair $(\lambda,\kappa)\in\mathbb R^2$ such that $$H(\lambda,\kappa,r)=M,\qquad H_r(\lambda,\kappa,r)=v.$$ The fitted parameters depend jointly smoothly on $(M,v,r)$ in this open domain. Equivalently, the smooth map $$\label{eq:fit-joint-map}
 (\lambda,\kappa,r)\longmapsto
       (H(\lambda,\kappa,r),H_r(\lambda,\kappa,r),r)$$ is a diffeomorphism from $\mathbb R^2\times(0,\infty)$ onto $\{(M,v,r):r>0,\ |M|<r,\ |v|<1\}$. At every model arc, $P,Q,R,S,G>0$. In particular, fitted parameters converge to a finite pair whenever their data converge to an interior point of this domain, even when the width varies.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-quadratic-fit"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-quadratic-fit</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $(M,v,r)$ in the stated domain. For each curvature there is exactly one slope fit by Equation <a href="#eq:fit-midpoint-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-midpoint-slope">[eq:fit-midpoint-slope]</a>. Lemmas <a href="#fit:conditional-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:conditional-derivative">10</a> and <a href="#fit:properness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:properness">11</a> show that its conditional $\widehat L$ is a strictly increasing map of $\mathbb R$ onto $\mathbb R$. There is therefore exactly one curvature for which $\widehat L=\operatorname{arctanh}v$, and then exactly one slope. The sign assertions have been proved in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-PQ-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-PQ-positive">[eq:fit-PQ-positive]</a> and Lemmas <a href="#fit:conditional-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:conditional-derivative">10</a> and <a href="#fit:positive-derivatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:positive-derivatives">12</a>.

The Jacobian of Equation <a href="#eq:fit-joint-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-joint-map">[eq:fit-joint-map]</a> has last row $(0,0,1)$, and its determinant is $G>0$. The inverse-function theorem gives a smooth local inverse at every point. These inverses agree wherever their domains overlap, by the global uniqueness just proved, so the inverse is jointly smooth on the entire target domain. Its continuity gives the final convergence statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Transport of an actual profile in fitted coordinates

<a id="eq:fit-identities"></a>
We now express the motion of a general arc in the model coordinates. Let $\phi\in C^\infty((0,\infty),\mathbb R)$, set $k=\phi'$, and let $M(h,r)$ and $v(h,r)=M_r(h,r)$ be its endpoint data from Section <a href="#sec:arcs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arcs">2</a>. The fit records both the midpoint and its response to a change in width; their two transport equations determine the evolution of the fitted slope and curvature. Theorem <a href="#thm:quadratic-fit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:quadratic-fit">13</a> gives unique smooth functions $\lambda(h,r)$ and $\kappa(h,r)$ such that $$\label{eq:fit-identities}
 M(h,r)=H(\lambda(h,r),\kappa(h,r),r),\qquad
 v(h,r)=H_r(\lambda(h,r),\kappa(h,r),r),
 \qquad h,r>0.$$ In the following identities, $P,Q,R,S,G$ denote the quadratic-model quantities evaluated at this fit. In particular, $$P,Q,R,S,G>0.$$ Recall the notation $$\alpha(h,r)=\frac{r}{r^2-M(h,r)^2},\qquad
 D=\partial_h-\alpha\partial_r.$$ Along an arc with its peak fixed, $D$ is differentiation with respect to the base height. If the same characteristic is parametrized by its outward-increasing width $s$, then differentiation along it is $-D/\alpha$.

<a id="stmt-lem-fit-transport"></a>
<div id="lem:fit-transport" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fit-transport"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Transport of the fit). *The fitted parameters satisfy $$\label{eq:fit-transport}
 \begin{aligned}
 P\lambda_r+Q\kappa_r&=0,\\
 D\kappa&=-\frac{R}{G}\bigl(\lambda-k(h)\bigr),\\
 D\lambda-\kappa&=\frac{S}{G}\bigl(\lambda-k(h)\bigr).
 \end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fit-transport"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fit-transport</summary>

*Proof.* Differentiating the first identity in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-identities">[eq:fit-identities]</a> at fixed $h$ and using the second one gives $P\lambda_r+Q\kappa_r=0$.

For a quadratic profile, increasing its base height changes the slope at rate $\kappa$, leaves its curvature fixed, and changes the width at rate $-\alpha$. Applying Equation <a href="#eq:arc-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-transport">[eq:arc-transport]</a> to that profile, at the fitted data, therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \kappa P-\alpha H_r
   &=\frac{M}{r^2-M^2}-\lambda,\\
 \kappa R-\alpha H_{rr}
   &=-\frac{2Mr(1-v^2)}{(r^2-M^2)^2}.
 \end{aligned}$$ For the actual profile, the chain rule gives $$\begin{aligned}
 DM&=P D\lambda+Q D\kappa-\alpha H_r,\\
 Dv&=R D\lambda+S D\kappa-\alpha H_{rr}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The expression for $Dv$ in Equation <a href="#eq:arc-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-transport">[eq:arc-transport]</a> depends only on $M,v,r$, so it is identical for the two matched sets of data. Subtracting the preceding identities consequently yields $$\begin{aligned}
 P(D\lambda-\kappa)+Q D\kappa&=\lambda-k(h),\\
 R(D\lambda-\kappa)+S D\kappa&=0.
 \end{aligned}$$ Their determinant is $G=PS-QR>0$. Solving this system proves Equation <a href="#eq:fit-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-transport">[eq:fit-transport]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:model"></a>
### A differential inequality for the quadratic model

The transport law of Lemma <a href="#lem:fit-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fit-transport">14</a> contains the coefficient $$C(\lambda,\kappa,r)=\frac{R}{G},\qquad
 D\kappa=-C(\lambda,\kappa,r)\bigl(\lambda-\phi'(h)\bigr).$$ When this identity is differentiated with respect to width, the implicit changes of the fitted parameters contribute terms proportional to $\kappa_r$. The remaining source contains the explicit model derivative $C_r$. The shape comparison in Section <a href="#sec:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transport">5</a> will use its sign, which is the purpose of this section.

From this point onward $\lambda$ and $\kappa$ are independent model parameters. The model quantities are $$H=H(\lambda,\kappa,r),\qquad
 P=H_\lambda,\quad Q=H_\kappa,\quad
 R=P_r,\quad S=Q_r,\quad G=PS-QR,$$ and $P,Q,R,S,G>0$ by Theorem <a href="#thm:quadratic-fit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:quadratic-fit">13</a>. Throughout this section, an $r$-subscript on a model function denotes a partial derivative with $\lambda,\kappa$ fixed. Derivatives along a characteristic will be identified explicitly.

<a id="stmt-thm-model-inequality"></a>
<div id="thm:model-inequality" class="theorem">

**Theorem 15**. *For every $\lambda,\kappa\in\mathbb R$ and $r>0$, <a id="model:inequality"></a>$$\left(\frac{R}{G}\right)_r\leq 0.
 \label{model:inequality}$$*

</div>

We prove the inequality by transporting the sign of $\mathcal W=RS_r-SR_r$ from small width. Endpoint identities and a Riccati linearization determine the sign at any possible zero; a characteristic argument then excludes such zeros when $\kappa\ne0$, and continuity treats $\kappa=0$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Transport and the quantity whose sign is required

<a id="model:definitions"></a>
<a id="model:quotient-derivative"></a>
Set $$\mathcal W=RS_r-SR_r,\qquad
 \alpha=\frac{r}{r^2-H^2},\qquad
 \gamma=\frac{r^2+H^2+2rHH_r}{(r^2-H^2)^2}.
 \label{model:definitions}$$ Since $P_r=R$ and $Q_r=S$, we have $$G_r=PS_r-QR_r,\qquad
 \left(\frac RG\right)_r
 =\frac{R_rG-RG_r}{G^2}
 =-\frac{P\mathcal W}{G^2}.
 \label{model:quotient-derivative}$$ It therefore suffices to prove $\mathcal W\geq0$.

<a id="model:PQ-transport"></a>
For a quadratic profile, raising the base height while keeping the peak fixed changes the base slope at rate $\kappa$. The corresponding transport operator on model data is <a id="model:transport-operator"></a>$$\mathcal D=\kappa\partial_\lambda-\alpha\partial_r.
 \label{model:transport-operator}$$ The arc transport identity from Section <a href="#sec:arcs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arcs">2</a> becomes $\mathcal DH=H/(r^2-H^2)-\lambda$. Differentiating this identity in $\lambda$ and in $\kappa$, including the derivatives of $\mathcal D$, gives $$\mathcal DP=\gamma P-1,\qquad
 \mathcal DQ=\gamma Q-P.
 \label{model:PQ-transport}$$ For example, differentiating in $\lambda$ contributes $-\alpha_\lambda H_r$ on the left, where $\alpha_\lambda=2rHP/(r^2-H^2)^2$. Differentiating in $\kappa$ also contributes $P$, because the coefficient of $\partial_\lambda$ is $\kappa$. These are exactly the terms accounted for in Equation <a href="#model:PQ-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:PQ-transport">[model:PQ-transport]</a>.

<a id="model:W-transport"></a>
The commutator identity is $\mathcal D(f_r)=(\mathcal Df)_r+\alpha_r f_r$. It yields <a id="model:RS-transport"></a><a id="model:RSr-transport"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal DR&=(\gamma+\alpha_r)R+\gamma_rP,\nonumber\\
 \mathcal DS&=(\gamma+\alpha_r)S+\gamma_rQ-R,
 \label{model:RS-transport}\\
 \mathcal D(R_r)&=(\gamma+2\alpha_r)R_r
                  +(2\gamma_r+\alpha_{rr})R+\gamma_{rr}P,\nonumber\\
 \mathcal D(S_r)&=(\gamma+2\alpha_r)S_r
                  +(2\gamma_r+\alpha_{rr})S+\gamma_{rr}Q-R_r.
 \label{model:RSr-transport}
\end{aligned}$$ Applying these four formulas to $RS_r-SR_r$, the terms containing $\alpha_{rr}$ cancel, and the remaining terms give $$\mathcal D\mathcal W
 =(2\gamma+3\alpha_r)\mathcal W
   +\gamma_rG_r-\gamma_{rr}G.
 \label{model:W-transport}$$

<a id="model:W-small-width"></a>
The variation formula in Lemma <a href="#lem:variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:variation">3</a>, together with the smooth arc scaling in Section <a href="#sec:arcs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arcs">2</a>, gives the following differentiated small-width expansions: <a id="model:small-width"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 P&=\frac{r^2}{3}+O(r^3),&
 R&=\frac{2r}{3}+O(r^2),&
 R_r&=\frac23+O(r),\nonumber\\
 Q&=\frac{r^4}{15}+O(r^5),&
 S&=\frac{4r^3}{15}+O(r^4),&
 S_r&=\frac{4r^2}{5}+O(r^3).
 \label{model:small-width}
\end{aligned}$$ The remainders are uniform on compact sets of finite $(\lambda,\kappa)$. Indeed, after width-one scaling, the model parameters are $\lambda r,\kappa r^3$, the limiting arc is a regular parabola, and the first variations in the profiles $u$ and $u^2/2$ are $1/3$ and $1/15$. Smooth dependence at that scaled arc permits the two displayed $r$-differentiations. In particular, $$\mathcal W=\frac{16}{45}r^3+O(r^4).
 \label{model:W-small-width}$$

<a id="model:zero-relations"></a>
At a zero of $\mathcal W$, the already established inequality $R>0$ allows us to set $\sigma_0=R_r/R$. Then $$R_r=\sigma_0R,\qquad S_r=\sigma_0S,\qquad G_r=\sigma_0G.
 \label{model:zero-relations}$$ At such a zero, Equation <a href="#model:W-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:W-transport">[model:W-transport]</a> becomes $$\mathcal D\mathcal W
 =-G(\gamma_{rr}-\sigma_0\gamma_r).$$ The outward derivative along a characteristic is $-\mathcal D/\alpha$. Thus the sign needed to prevent a first zero from positive values is <a id="model:required-zero-sign"></a>$$\gamma_{rr}-\sigma_0\gamma_r>0
 \qquad(\kappa>0,\ \mathcal W=0).
 \label{model:required-zero-sign}$$ The next three subsections establish this strict inequality from the quadratic arc equation. We then apply it on the entire model domain.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Endpoint identities and two exact algebraic reductions

<a id="model:Schwarzian-definition"></a>
For the remainder of the sign calculation, write $\kappa=2b>0$ and $d=\lambda$. The profile is $du+bu^2$. Its endpoints at base zero are $-m,n$, where <a id="model:endpoint-variables"></a>$$m=r-H,\qquad n=r+H,\qquad
 \ell=\frac{dn}{dm}=\frac{1+H_r}{1-H_r}>0,\qquad
 w=\frac{\ell_m}{\ell}.
 \label{model:endpoint-variables}$$ Here $m_r=1-H_r>0$, so $m$ is a valid local coordinate along the fixed-$(d,b)$ family. Subscripts $m$ below refer to this coordinate. We also write $$\mathcal S=\frac{\ell_{mm}}{\ell}-\frac32w^2,
 \qquad
 w_m=\mathcal S+\frac12w^2.
 \label{model:Schwarzian-definition}$$ Thus $\mathcal S$ is the Schwarzian derivative of $n=n(m)$.

<a id="model:endpoint-identities"></a>
The following weighted endpoint quantities will be useful: $$\begin{aligned}
 X&=\frac{\ell/m-1/n}{1+\ell}=\kappa P+d,\nonumber\\
 Z&=\frac{\ell m-n}{1+\ell}=dP+3\kappa Q,\nonumber\\
 \gamma&=\frac{\ell/m^2+1/n^2}{1+\ell}.
 \label{model:endpoint-identities}
\end{aligned}$$ For the first identity, use Equation <a href="#eq:fit-width-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-width-identity">[eq:fit-width-identity]</a>, $$\kappa P+d=\frac{rH_r+H}{r^2-H^2},$$ and substitute $r=(m+n)/2$, $H=(n-m)/2$. For the second, width scaling gives <a id="model:scaling"></a>$$H(\lambda,\kappa,r)
 =rH(\lambda r,\kappa r^3,1),\qquad
 rH_r-H=\lambda P+3\kappa Q.
 \label{model:scaling}$$ The endpoint form of $rH_r-H$ is the displayed expression for $Z$. The formula for $\gamma$ follows directly from Equation <a href="#model:definitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:definitions">[model:definitions]</a>.

<a id="model:Dstar"></a>
Define <a id="model:wstar"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 D_*={}&(m-n)\mathcal S
 +\left[1-\frac{2n}{m}
       +\ell\left(\frac{2m}{n}-1\right)\right]w
 +\frac{m+n}{2}w^2,
 \label{model:Dstar}\\
 w_*={}&
 \frac{2(\ell m^4-2\ell m^3n+2mn^3-n^4)}
      {m^2n^2(m+n)}.
 \label{model:wstar}
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="stmt-model-endpoint-algebra"></a>
<div id="model:endpoint-algebra" class="lemma">

<a id="model:determinant-factor"></a>
<a id="model:coordinate-zero-sign"></a>
<a id="model:gamma-factor"></a>
**Lemma 16**. *The exact determinant identity is $$X_mZ_{mm}-Z_mX_{mm}
 =\frac{\ell^2(m+n)^2}{m^2n^2(1+\ell)^3}D_*
 =\frac{3\kappa^2}{m_r^3}\mathcal W.
 \label{model:determinant-factor}$$ At a zero of $\mathcal W$ with $m\ne n$, set $\sigma=X_{mm}/X_m$. This is well defined, and $$\begin{aligned}
 \gamma_{rr}-\sigma_0\gamma_r
 &=m_r^2(\gamma_{mm}-\sigma\gamma_m),
 \label{model:coordinate-zero-sign}\\
 \gamma_{mm}-\sigma\gamma_m
 &=\frac{\ell(m+n)^2}{(1+\ell)m^2n^2}
       \frac{w_*-w}{m-n}.
 \label{model:gamma-factor}
\end{aligned}$$ All the prefactors displayed in Equations <a href="#model:determinant-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:determinant-factor">[model:determinant-factor]</a> and <a href="#model:gamma-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:gamma-factor">[model:gamma-factor]</a> are strictly positive.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-endpoint-algebra"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-endpoint-algebra</summary>

<a id="model:determinant-intermediate"></a>
*Proof.* Put $B=1/n^2-1/m^2$. Differentiation of Equation <a href="#model:endpoint-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:endpoint-identities">[model:endpoint-identities]</a>, using $n_m=\ell$, gives <a id="model:first-endpoint-derivatives"></a>$$Z_m=\frac{w\ell(m+n)}{(1+\ell)^2},\qquad
 X_m=\frac{\ell B}{1+\ell}+\frac{Z_m}{mn},\qquad
 B_m=\frac2{m^3}-\frac{2\ell}{n^3}.
 \label{model:first-endpoint-derivatives}$$ Differentiate the middle identity and insert the first one into the determinant. After division by $\ell^2(m+n)/(1+\ell)^3$, the result is $$B\mathcal S+
 \left(\frac{B(1+\ell)}{m+n}-B_m\right)w
 +\frac{(m+n)^2}{2m^2n^2}w^2.
 \label{model:determinant-intermediate}$$ For example, in this calculation $$\frac{d}{dm}\log\frac{\ell(m+n)}{(1+\ell)^2}
 =\frac{1-\ell}{1+\ell}w+\frac{1+\ell}{m+n},
 \qquad
 \left(\frac1{mn}\right)_m=-\frac{n+m\ell}{m^2n^2}.$$ These two derivatives, together with $w_m=\mathcal S+w^2/2$, give the three coefficients in Equation <a href="#model:determinant-intermediate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:determinant-intermediate">[model:determinant-intermediate]</a>. Now $$B=\frac{(m-n)(m+n)}{m^2n^2},$$ and substitution of $B_m$ shows that the coefficient of $w$ in Equation <a href="#model:determinant-intermediate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:determinant-intermediate">[model:determinant-intermediate]</a> is $$\frac{m+n}{m^2n^2}
 \left[1-\frac{2n}{m}
       +\ell\left(\frac{2m}{n}-1\right)\right].$$ This proves its first factorization in Equation <a href="#model:determinant-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:determinant-factor">[model:determinant-factor]</a>.

On the other hand, differentiating $X=\kappa P+d$ and $Z=dP+3\kappa Q$ with respect to $r$ gives $$X_rZ_{rr}-Z_rX_{rr}=3\kappa^2(RS_r-SR_r).$$ The chain rule gives $$X_rZ_{rr}-Z_rX_{rr}
 =m_r^3\bigl(X_mZ_{mm}-Z_mX_{mm}\bigr).$$ This proves the other equality in Equation <a href="#model:determinant-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:determinant-factor">[model:determinant-factor]</a>. It also gives <a id="model:Xm-positive"></a>$$X_m=\frac{\kappa R}{m_r}>0.
 \label{model:Xm-positive}$$ Thus, at a zero, $\sigma=X_{mm}/X_m$ is legitimate, even if $Z_m=0$ or $w=0$. The determinant identity then implies $Z_{mm}=\sigma Z_m$. Using $X_{rr}=\sigma_0X_r$ at that point gives $$\sigma m_r^2=\sigma_0m_r-m_{rr}.$$ The chain rule for $\gamma$ proves Equation <a href="#model:coordinate-zero-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:coordinate-zero-sign">[model:coordinate-zero-sign]</a>.

<a id="model:weighted-operator"></a>
<a id="model:rho-relations"></a>
It remains to compute $\gamma_{mm}-\sigma\gamma_m$. For a function $f$ smooth near $m$ and $-n$, write $$T_f=\frac{\ell f(m)+f(-n)}{1+\ell},\qquad
 a_0=\frac{\ell}{1+\ell},\qquad
 \beta=\frac{w}{1+\ell},\qquad
 \rho=(\log a_0)_m-\sigma.$$ A first differentiation gives $$(T_f)_m=a_0\{f'(m)-f'(-n)+\beta[f(m)-f(-n)]\}.$$ A second differentiation therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{(T_f)_{mm}-\sigma(T_f)_m}{a_0}
 ={}&f''(m)+\ell f''(-n)
       +\beta\{f'(m)+\ell f'(-n)\}\nonumber\\
 &+\rho\{f'(m)-f'(-n)\}
       +(\beta_m+\rho\beta)\{f(m)-f(-n)\}.
 \label{model:weighted-operator}
\end{aligned}$$ At the zero under consideration, this expression vanishes for $f(x)=x$ and $f(x)=1/x$, since $T_x=Z$ and $T_{1/x}=X$. Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 \beta_m+\rho\beta
   &=-\frac{\beta(1+\ell)}{m+n},\nonumber\\
 \rho B
   &=\frac{2\ell}{n^3}-\frac2{m^3}
     +\beta\left(\frac1{m^2}+\frac{\ell}{n^2}
                         +\frac{1+\ell}{mn}\right).
 \label{model:rho-relations}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $B\ne0$ because $m\ne n$; no division by $\beta$ is made.

<a id="model:gamma-coefficients"></a>
For $f(x)=1/x^2$, Equation <a href="#model:weighted-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:weighted-operator">[model:weighted-operator]</a> becomes <a id="model:gamma-intermediate"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\gamma_{mm}-\sigma\gamma_m}{a_0}
 ={}&\frac6{m^4}+\frac{6\ell}{n^4}
       +\beta\left(-\frac2{m^3}+\frac{2\ell}{n^3}\right)
       -2\rho\left(\frac1{m^3}+\frac1{n^3}\right)
       +\frac{\beta(1+\ell)B}{m+n}.
 \label{model:gamma-intermediate}
\end{aligned}$$ Eliminate $\rho$ using Equation <a href="#model:rho-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:rho-relations">[model:rho-relations]</a>. The resulting constant coefficient and coefficient of $\beta$ are, respectively, $$\begin{aligned}
 A_0={}&\frac6{m^4}+\frac{6\ell}{n^4}
   -\frac{2(1/m^3+1/n^3)(2\ell/n^3-2/m^3)}{B}
 =\frac{2(m+n)N}{m^4n^4(m-n)},\nonumber\\
 A_\beta={}&-\frac2{m^3}+\frac{2\ell}{n^3}
 -\frac{2(1/m^3+1/n^3)
       (1/m^2+\ell/n^2+(1+\ell)/(mn))}{B}
 +\frac{(1+\ell)B}{m+n}\nonumber\\
 ={}&-\frac{(1+\ell)(m+n)^2}{m^2n^2(m-n)},
 \label{model:gamma-coefficients}\\
 N={}&\ell m^4-2\ell m^3n+2mn^3-n^4.\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ For completeness, the two numerator reductions in Equation <a href="#model:gamma-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:gamma-coefficients">[model:gamma-coefficients]</a> are $$\begin{aligned}
 &6(n^4+\ell m^4)(m^2-n^2)
       -4(m^3+n^3)(\ell m^3-n^3)=2(m+n)^2N,\\
 &(m+n)\bigl[2(\ell m^3-n^3)(m-n)
       -2(m^3+n^3)(\ell m+n)
       +(1+\ell)mn(m-n)^2\bigr]\\
 &\hspace{35mm}=-(1+\ell)mn(m+n)^3.
\end{aligned}$$ They correspond to the common denominators $m^4n^4(m^2-n^2)$ and $m^3n^3(m^2-n^2)$. Substituting $\beta=w/(1+\ell)$ into $A_0+\beta A_\beta$ now gives $$\frac{(m+n)^2}{m^2n^2}\frac{w_*-w}{m-n}.$$ Multiplication by $a_0$ proves Equation <a href="#model:gamma-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:gamma-factor">[model:gamma-factor]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Riccati equations, a common endpoint label, and an estimate

The algebra has reduced the desired sign at a possible zero to the ordering of $w$ and $w_*$. To establish that ordering we now use the differential equation of the actual quadratic arc. Its Riccati linearization gives a common projective label for the two endpoints; the classical Schwarzian ratio identity and chain rule <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[OT](#ref-OT)] then determine the endpoint Schwarzian. The next lemma derives these relations and the additional integral estimate used in the sign argument.

<a id="stmt-model-riccati"></a>
<div id="model:Riccati" class="lemma">

<a id="model:Schwarzian-formula"></a>
<a id="model:variation-formulas"></a>
<a id="model:Riccati-bound"></a>
**Lemma 17**. *For the quadratic profile $du+bu^2$, with $b>0$, one has $$\mathcal S
 =2b(m+n\ell^2)+\frac{d^2}{2}(\ell^2-1)
   -d\left(\frac1m+\frac{\ell^2}{n}\right)
   +\frac32\left(\frac{\ell^2}{n^2}-\frac1{m^2}\right).
 \label{model:Schwarzian-formula}$$ The arc admits a positive function $V$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \ell&=\frac{mV(-m)^2}{nV(n)^2},\nonumber\\
 w&=\frac1m-\frac{\ell}{n}+d(1+\ell)
       +2bmV(-m)^2\int_{-m}^{n}V(y)^{-2}\,dy.
 \label{model:variation-formulas}
\end{aligned}$$ If $d<0$ and $d^2\ge4bm$, put $\nu=(d^2/4-bm)^{1/2}$. Then $$w\le\frac1m-\frac{\ell}{n}+d\ell-2\nu.
 \label{model:Riccati-bound}$$ This includes $\nu=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-riccati"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-riccati</summary>

<a id="model:positive-V"></a>
<a id="model:linear-system"></a>
<a id="model:scalar-U"></a>
*Proof.* The arc equation is $u_y=du+bu^2-y$. On its finite interval $[-m,n]$, define $$V(y)=\exp\left[-\int_{-m}^{y}(bu(s)+d/2)\,ds\right]>0,
 \qquad U(y)=u(y)V(y).
 \label{model:positive-V}$$ The integral is finite because the actual arc is bounded on a compact interval. In the following system, primes denote derivatives with respect to $y$. Direct differentiation gives the regular linear system $$U'=\frac d2U-yV,\qquad V'=-bU-\frac d2V.
 \label{model:linear-system}$$ In particular $U(-m)=U(n)=0$. Eliminating $V$ where $y\ne0$ gives $$U''=\frac{U'}{y}
       +\left(by+\frac{d^2}{4}-\frac{d}{2y}\right)U.
 \label{model:scalar-U}$$

We now justify the use of one projective solution label at both endpoints despite the singularity of Equation <a href="#model:scalar-U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:scalar-U">[model:scalar-U]</a> at zero. Take $(U_1,V_1)=(U,V)$ from Equation <a href="#model:positive-V" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:positive-V">[model:positive-V]</a>, and complete it to a fundamental pair $(U_1,V_1),(U_2,V_2)$ of the regular system in Equation <a href="#model:linear-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:linear-system">[model:linear-system]</a>. Its coefficient matrix has trace zero, so $$\Omega=U_1V_2-U_2V_1$$ is a nonzero constant. At either endpoint, $U_1=0$ and $V_1>0$; hence $U_2=-\Omega/V_1\ne0$. Therefore the same ratio $f=U_1/U_2$ is finite near both endpoints, and <a id="model:common-label"></a>$$f(-m)=f(n)=0,\qquad f'(y)=\frac{y\Omega}{U_2(y)^2}\ne0
 \quad\hbox{at }y=-m,n.
 \label{model:common-label}$$ A nearby arc has coefficients that can be normalized as $(1,-q)$ in this fixed fundamental pair: continuous dependence keeps its first coefficient nonzero near the original arc. Its endpoint condition is $U_1-qU_2=0$. Thus the nearby endpoint functions satisfy the same local identity $f(n(m))=f(-m)$. No scalar solution is continued through the singular equation at zero. The scalar determinant $U_1'U_2-U_1U_2'=y\Omega$ is nonzero in each endpoint neighborhood, so $U_1,U_2$ form a scalar basis in each such neighborhood.

Writing $U=\sqrt{|y|}\,\widetilde U$ in Equation <a href="#model:scalar-U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:scalar-U">[model:scalar-U]</a> gives $$\widetilde U''
 =\left(by+\frac{d^2}{4}-\frac{d}{2y}
                    +\frac3{4y^2}\right)\widetilde U.$$ For two solutions of $a''=Q_0a$, $c''=Q_0c$, their ratio has Schwarzian $-2Q_0$: locally, $(a/c)''/(a/c)'=-2c'/c$, and differentiating this expression and subtracting half its square gives $-2c''/c$. The ratio is unchanged by division of both solutions by $\sqrt{|y|}$. Consequently $$\{f,y\}=-2by-\frac{d^2}{2}+\frac d y-\frac3{2y^2},
 \qquad
 \{f,y\}=\frac{f'''}{f'}-\frac32\left(\frac{f''}{f'}\right)^2.$$ Apply the Schwarzian chain rule to the common-label identity $f(n(m))=f(-m)$. The affine map $m\mapsto-m$ has zero Schwarzian and squared derivative one, so $$\mathcal S=\{f,y\}\big|_{y=-m}
              -\ell^2\{f,y\}\big|_{y=n}.$$ Expanding these two evaluations proves Equation <a href="#model:Schwarzian-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Schwarzian-formula">[model:Schwarzian-formula]</a>.

For Equation <a href="#model:variation-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:variation-formulas">[model:variation-formulas]</a>, vary the initial endpoint in $u(-m;m)=0$. Since $u_y(-m)=m$, the initial variation is $u_m(-m)=m$. Its scalar variational equation yields $$u_m(y)=m\exp\left[\int_{-m}^{y}(d+2bu(s))\,ds\right]
       =m\,\frac{V(-m)^2}{V(y)^2}.$$ Differentiating $u(n(m);m)=0$, and using $u_y(n)=-n$, gives the formula for $\ell$. Its logarithm can be written $$\log\ell=\log m-\log n+\int_{-m}^{n}(d+2bu(y))\,dy.$$ Differentiate in $m$. The two boundary contributions from $2bu$ vanish, while the constant term contributes $d(1+\ell)$. Substituting the expression for $u_m$ proves the formula for $w$.

Finally, differentiating the second equation in Equation <a href="#model:linear-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:linear-system">[model:linear-system]</a> gives $$V''=(by+d^2/4)V,\qquad V'(-m)=-dV(-m)/2.$$ Suppose $d<0$, $d^2\ge4bm$, and set $s=y+m$. For $v_0(s)=V(-m+s)/V(-m)$ one has $$v_0''=(\nu^2+bs)v_0,\qquad v_0(0)=1,\qquad v_0'(0)=-d/2.$$ Let $$f_\nu(s)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\cosh(\nu s)-\frac d{2\nu}\sinh(\nu s),&\nu>0,\\[1mm]
 1-ds/2,&\nu=0.
 \end{cases}$$ Variation of constants gives, for $0\le s\le m+n$, $$v_0(s)-f_\nu(s)
 =b\int_0^s K_\nu(s-t)\,t\,v_0(t)\,dt\ge0,
 \qquad
 K_\nu(t)=
 \begin{cases}\sinh(\nu t)/\nu,&\nu>0,\\t,&\nu=0.\end{cases}$$ The inequality follows from the already constructed positivity of $v_0$, and from $K_\nu\ge0$. Also $f_\nu>0$ on the positive half-line because $d<0$. Therefore <a id="model:inverse-square-bound"></a>$$V(-m)^2\int_{-m}^{n}V(y)^{-2}\,dy
 \le\int_0^\infty f_\nu(s)^{-2}\,ds
 =\frac1{\nu-d/2}.
 \label{model:inverse-square-bound}$$ For $\nu>0$, the substitution $q=\tanh(\nu s)$, with $A=-d/(2\nu)>0$, evaluates the last integral as $$\frac1\nu\int_0^1(1+Aq)^{-2}\,dq
 =\frac1{\nu(1+A)}.$$ For $\nu=0$, direct integration of $(1-ds/2)^{-2}$ gives $-2/d$, the same formula. Since $\nu^2=d^2/4-bm$, $$\frac{2bm}{\nu-d/2}=-d-2\nu.$$ Substitution into Equation <a href="#model:variation-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:variation-formulas">[model:variation-formulas]</a> proves Equation <a href="#model:Riccati-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Riccati-bound">[model:Riccati-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### The signs at a possible zero

<a id="stmt-model-zero-sign"></a>
<div id="model:zero-sign" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18**. *If $\kappa>0$ and $\mathcal W=0$, then $\gamma_{rr}-\sigma_0\gamma_r>0$, with $\sigma_0$ defined in Equation <a href="#model:zero-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:zero-relations">[model:zero-relations]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-model-zero-sign"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-zero-sign</summary>

*Proof.* We partition the possibilities for $m,n,\ell$. The quantities $\theta=\operatorname{arctanh}(H/r)$ and $L=\operatorname{arctanh}(H_r)$, and the finite-peak reference characteristics used below, are those of Lemma <a href="#fit:references" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:references">8</a>.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### Case 1: $m\le n$.

The final angle is nonnegative. Along the characteristic leading to this data point, the driving slope satisfies $$j'=-s\kappa\operatorname{sech}^2\theta<0.$$ Its initial value $z$ must be positive. Indeed, if $z\le0$, then $j(s)<0$ for every $s>0$, and $\theta'+a(s)\theta=j(s)$, where $a(s)=\sinh(2\theta)/(s\theta)>0$, with value $2/s$ at $\theta=0$, forces $\theta(s)<0$. This last assertion follows by integrating from a positive $\varepsilon$ and letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$: $\theta(\varepsilon)\to0$, and the forward homogeneous factor is at most one. It would contradict the nonnegative final angle.

Since $z>0$, $\theta(s)=zs/3+O(s^2)>0$ initially. If it first reached zero at some earlier width $s_1<r$, then $j(s_1)=\theta'(s_1)\le0$. The strict decrease of $j$ would give $j(s)<0$ thereafter, forcing $\theta(s)<0$ for $s>s_1$. Again this contradicts the final angle. Thus $\theta(s)>0$ for all $0<s<r$, and $$L(r)=\int_0^r\frac{\sinh(2\theta(s))}{s}\,ds>0,
 \qquad \ell=e^{2L}>1.$$

We also need the sign of $w=(\log\ell)_m$, where the derivative is taken along the fixed-profile family at base zero. Let $t=t(r)$ be the peak height of that profile. Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a> gives $t_r>0$, and its peak slope is $z=d+\kappa t$. The final model value $L=\operatorname{arctanh}H_r$ equals $\mathcal L_\kappa(r,z(r))$, and Lemma <a href="#fit:references" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:references">8</a> gives $\partial_z\mathcal L_\kappa>0$. Consequently <a id="model:w-positive-nonnegative-angle"></a>$$L_r\big|_{d,\kappa}
 =\frac{\sinh(2\theta)}r
   +\kappa t_r\partial_z\mathcal L_\kappa(r,z)>0,\qquad
 w=\frac{2L_r|_{d,\kappa}}{m_r}>0.
 \label{model:w-positive-nonnegative-angle}$$ If $m=n$, Equation <a href="#model:Dstar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Dstar">[model:Dstar]</a> now reduces to $$D_*=(\ell-1)w+mw^2>0.$$ Hence a zero of $\mathcal W$ cannot have $m=n$.

If $m<n$, put $z_1=m/n\in(0,1)$. The sign of $w_*$ is the sign of $$\ell z_1^3(z_1-2)+2z_1-1
 \le z_1^3(z_1-2)+2z_1-1
 =(z_1-1)^3(z_1+1)<0.$$ Since $w>0$, the quotient $(w_*-w)/(m-n)$ is positive. Equations <a href="#model:gamma-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:gamma-factor">[model:gamma-factor]</a> and <a href="#model:coordinate-zero-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:coordinate-zero-sign">[model:coordinate-zero-sign]</a> prove the required sign.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### Case 2: $m>n$ and $\ell\ge1$.

Here the final angle is negative, which forces $d<0$. In fact, if its final driving slope $d$ were nonnegative, then the strictly decreasing driving slope would be positive at all earlier widths, and the same scalar angle equation would give a positive final angle.

<a id="model:S-positive-ell-large"></a>
Each group in Equation <a href="#model:Schwarzian-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Schwarzian-formula">[model:Schwarzian-formula]</a> is now nonnegative, and several are strictly positive. In particular $$\mathcal S>0
 \quad\hbox{whenever }m>n,\ \ell\ge1.
 \label{model:S-positive-ell-large}$$ We justify $w\ge0$ on this region without assuming that $m$ ranges to infinity.

For the fixed quadratic profile, Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-data">2</a> gives arcs at all widths $r\in(0,\infty)$. Since $m_r>0$ and $0<m(r)<2r$, the $m$-domain is an interval $(0,M_*)$, with $M_*$ finite or infinite. At its left end, $$\ell=1+\frac43dr+O(r^2)<1$$ because $d<0$. Fix a point in the open set $\{m-n>0,\ell>1\}$, and let $(a,c)$ be its connected component. The expansion just given implies $a>0$; also $a<M_*$, since the chosen point is to its right. Thus $a$ is an interior point of the smooth parameter domain. On the component, $(m-n)_m=1-\ell<0$. Moving backwards to $a$ therefore increases $m-n$, so its value at $a$ is strictly positive. The component cannot enter through $m=n$. It must have $\ell(a)=1$, and the right derivative there satisfies $\ell_m(a)\ge0$, or $w(a)\ge0$. Equations <a href="#model:Schwarzian-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Schwarzian-definition">[model:Schwarzian-definition]</a> and <a href="#model:S-positive-ell-large" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:S-positive-ell-large">[model:S-positive-ell-large]</a> give $w_m>0$ throughout the component. Hence $w>0$ inside it.

At a point with $m>n,\ell=1,w<0$, points immediately to the left would have $\ell>1,w<0$, contrary to the result just proved. A point with $m>n,\ell=1,w=0$ is also impossible: there $w_m=\mathcal S>0$ and $\ell_{mm}=\ell(w_m+w^2)=\mathcal S>0$. Immediately to the left one would again have $\ell>1,w<0$. Thus $w\ge0$ at every point required in this case. The component argument applies to each chosen component separately; it makes no assumption about their number.

Writing $z_1=m/n>1$, the coefficient of $w$ in Equation <a href="#model:Dstar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Dstar">[model:Dstar]</a> satisfies $$1-\frac2{z_1}+\ell(2z_1-1)
 \ge 2z_1-\frac2{z_1}>0.$$ Together with $(m-n)\mathcal S>0$ and $w\ge0$, this gives $D_*>0$. Hence no zero of $\mathcal W$ occurs in this case.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### Case 3: $m>n$ and $0<\ell<1$.

<a id="model:last-case-target"></a>
Again $d<0$. It suffices to prove that $$w\ge w_*\quad\Longrightarrow\quad D_*>0.
 \label{model:last-case-target}$$ Indeed, at a zero this will give $w<w_*$, and the denominator $m-n$ in Equation <a href="#model:gamma-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:gamma-factor">[model:gamma-factor]</a> is positive.

We may set $n=1,m=z_1>1$ at the point. To specify this scaling, fix the original endpoint value $N=n>0$ as a constant and put $y=N\widetilde y$, $u=N^2\widetilde u$. The new model parameters are $\widetilde d=Nd$, $\widetilde b=N^3b$, while $$\widetilde m=m/N,\quad \widetilde n=n/N,\quad
 \widetilde\ell=\ell,\quad
 \widetilde w=Nw,\quad
 \widetilde{\mathcal S}=N^2\mathcal S,\quad
 \widetilde D_*=ND_*,\quad \widetilde w_*=Nw_*.$$ Thus the equations and the signs needed for Equation <a href="#model:last-case-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:last-case-target">[model:last-case-target]</a> are preserved. We suppress the tildes and use $n=1,m=z_1$ from now on.

<a id="model:scaled-wstar"></a>
In these units, $$w_*=\frac{2(\ell z_1^3(z_1-2)+2z_1-1)}{z_1^2(z_1+1)}>0.
 \label{model:scaled-wstar}$$ For $z_1\ge2$ positivity is immediate. For $1<z_1<2$, the coefficient of $\ell$ in its numerator is negative; replacing $\ell<1$ by one decreases that numerator to $(z_1-1)^3(z_1+1)>0$.

For fixed $\mathcal S$, write $$D_*=(z_1-1)\mathcal S+
       [1-2/z_1+\ell(2z_1-1)]w+\frac{z_1+1}{2}w^2.$$ The derivative of this expression in $w$, evaluated at $w_*$ and multiplied by $z_1^2$, is <a id="model:J-polynomial"></a>$$J(\ell,z_1)=2\ell z_1^4-2\ell z_1^3-\ell z_1^2+z_1^2+2z_1-2.
 \label{model:J-polynomial}$$ This polynomial is affine in $\ell$. With $\xi=z_1-1>0$, its endpoint values are $$J(0,1+\xi)=\xi^2+4\xi+1,\qquad
 J(1,1+\xi)=2\xi(\xi+2)(\xi^2+\xi+1).$$ Both are strictly positive. Therefore $J>0$ for $0<\ell<1$. Since the derivative in $w$ increases with $w$, $D_*$ is strictly increasing for $w\ge w_*$.

<a id="model:baseline-factorization"></a>
<a id="model:Delta-factorization"></a>
Set <a id="model:T-and-baseline"></a>$$T=2\ell z_1^3-\ell z_1^2-z_1+2,\qquad
 A=2\ell z_1^3-3\ell z_1^2+3z_1-2,\qquad
 \mathcal S_0=\frac32(\ell^2-z_1^{-2}).
 \label{model:T-and-baseline}$$ Substitution of Equation <a href="#model:scaled-wstar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:scaled-wstar">[model:scaled-wstar]</a> gives the two polynomial identities $$\begin{aligned}
 (z_1-1)\mathcal S_0+
 [1-2/z_1+\ell(2z_1-1)]w_*+\frac{z_1+1}{2}w_*^2
 &=\frac{z_1-1}{2z_1^4}AT,
 \label{model:baseline-factorization}\\
 \Delta_0:=\frac1{z_1}-\ell-w_*
 &=-\frac{z_1-1}{(z_1+1)z_1^2}T.
 \label{model:Delta-factorization}
\end{aligned}$$ These factorizations can be checked by collecting powers of $\ell$. For Equation <a href="#model:baseline-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:baseline-factorization">[model:baseline-factorization]</a>, multiplying its left side by $2z_1^4(z_1+1)$ gives $$(z_1^2-1)\bigl[
   \ell^2z_1^4(2z_1-3)(2z_1-1)
   +4\ell z_1^2(z_1^2-1)+(3z_1-2)(2-z_1)\bigr].$$ The bracket is $AT$, because the cross coefficient in the product is $$z_1^2\bigl[(2z_1-3)(2-z_1)+(3z_1-2)(2z_1-1)\bigr]
 =4z_1^2(z_1^2-1).$$ For Equation <a href="#model:Delta-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Delta-factorization">[model:Delta-factorization]</a>, the numerator over $z_1^2(z_1+1)$ is $(z_1-1)[z_1-2-\ell z_1^2(2z_1-1)]=-(z_1-1)T$. The sign of $A$ requires no further restriction on the parameters. It too is affine in $\ell$, and $$A(0,1+\xi)=3\xi+1,\qquad
 A(1,1+\xi)=\xi(2\xi^2+3\xi+3),$$ so $A>0$.

<a id="model:baseline-improvement"></a>
Assume $w\ge w_*$. First suppose $T\ge0$. If $d^2\ge4bz_1$, Equation <a href="#model:Riccati-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Riccati-bound">[model:Riccati-bound]</a> would imply $$\Delta_0
 =\frac1{z_1}-\ell-w_*
 \ge\frac1{z_1}-\ell-w
 \ge-d\ell+2\nu>2\nu\ge0.$$ This contradicts Equation <a href="#model:Delta-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Delta-factorization">[model:Delta-factorization]</a>. Consequently $d^2<4bz_1$. In Equation <a href="#model:Schwarzian-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Schwarzian-formula">[model:Schwarzian-formula]</a>, the difference from the baseline is $$\mathcal S-\mathcal S_0
 =\left(2bz_1-\frac{d^2}{2}\right)
   +\ell^2\left(2b+\frac{d^2}{2}-d\right)-\frac d{z_1}>0.
 \label{model:baseline-improvement}$$ Each term on the right is positive in this subcase. Equation <a href="#model:baseline-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:baseline-factorization">[model:baseline-factorization]</a> is nonnegative because $A>0,T\ge0$. Since $z_1-1>0$, the strict improvement in Equation <a href="#model:baseline-improvement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:baseline-improvement">[model:baseline-improvement]</a>, followed by monotonicity in $w$, gives $D_*>0$.

<a id="model:S-last-subcase"></a>
It remains to consider $T<0$. Then $$z_1-2>\ell z_1^2(2z_1-1)>0.$$ In particular $z_1>2$, and the same inequality implies $z_1-2>3\ell$. Equation <a href="#model:endpoint-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:endpoint-identities">[model:endpoint-identities]</a>, with $\kappa P>0$, gives $$d<\frac{\ell/z_1-1}{1+\ell},\qquad
 -dz_1>\frac{z_1-\ell}{1+\ell}>2.$$ Rewrite Equation <a href="#model:Schwarzian-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Schwarzian-formula">[model:Schwarzian-formula]</a> as $$\mathcal S=
 \left(2bz_1-\frac{d^2}{2}\right)
 +\ell^2\left(2b+\frac{d^2}{2}-d+\frac32\right)
 -\frac d{z_1}-\frac3{2z_1^2}.
 \label{model:S-last-subcase}$$ If the first parentheses are nonnegative, then $-d/z_1>2/z_1^2$ immediately gives $\mathcal S>0$. If they are negative, put $\nu=(d^2/4-bz_1)^{1/2}>0$. The preceding use of Equation <a href="#model:Riccati-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:Riccati-bound">[model:Riccati-bound]</a> still gives $2\nu<\Delta_0$, while $w_*>0,\ell>0$ give $\Delta_0<1/z_1$. Hence $$2bz_1-\frac{d^2}{2}=-2\nu^2>-\frac1{2z_1^2}.$$ Together with $-d/z_1>2/z_1^2$, this strictly dominates the negative term $-3/(2z_1^2)$ in Equation <a href="#model:S-last-subcase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:S-last-subcase">[model:S-last-subcase]</a>. Its remaining $\ell^2$-term is positive, so again $\mathcal S>0$. Since $z_1>2$, the coefficient $1-2/z_1+\ell(2z_1-1)$ is positive, and $w\ge w_*>0$. Therefore every term in $D_*$ is positive. This proves Equation <a href="#model:last-case-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:last-case-target">[model:last-case-target]</a> also when $T<0$.

The three cases cover all endpoint configurations. At a zero of $\mathcal W$, Cases 1 and 3 give $(w_*-w)/(m-n)>0$, and the excluded diagonal and Case 2 contain no zeros. Equations <a href="#model:gamma-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:gamma-factor">[model:gamma-factor]</a> and <a href="#model:coordinate-zero-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:coordinate-zero-sign">[model:coordinate-zero-sign]</a> finish the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Completion of the characteristic argument

<details id="proof-stmt-model-zero-sign-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-model-zero-sign</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:model-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:model-inequality">15</a>.* First suppose $\kappa>0$. Fix any model data point $(\lambda,\kappa,r)$. By the arc construction and Lemma <a href="#fit:references" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fit:references">8</a>, it belongs to a characteristic coming from a finite peak. Along that characteristic, use $s$ for outward-increasing width. The base slope tends to a finite peak slope as $s\downarrow0$, so the uniform expansion in Equation <a href="#model:W-small-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:W-small-width">[model:W-small-width]</a> makes $\mathcal W$ strictly positive for all sufficiently small positive $s$.

<a id="model:outward-orientation"></a>
On this curve $\kappa$ is constant and $d\lambda/ds=-\kappa/\alpha$. Thus the total outward derivative is $$\frac{d}{ds}\mathcal W(\lambda(s),\kappa,s)
 =\mathcal W_r-\frac{\kappa}{\alpha}\mathcal W_\lambda
 =-\frac1\alpha\,\mathcal D\mathcal W.
 \label{model:outward-orientation}$$ At any zero, Equations <a href="#model:W-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:W-transport">[model:W-transport]</a> and <a href="#model:zero-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:zero-relations">[model:zero-relations]</a>, and Lemma <a href="#model:zero-sign" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="model:zero-sign">18</a>, give $$\mathcal D\mathcal W
