 =-G(\gamma_{rr}-\sigma_0\gamma_r)<0.$$ Since $\alpha>0$, Equation <a href="#model:outward-orientation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:outward-orientation">[model:outward-orientation]</a> makes the outward derivative strictly positive. A first zero reached from strictly positive values would instead have a nonpositive outward derivative. This contradiction proves $\mathcal W>0$ at every point with $\kappa>0$.

Reflection of the endpoint coordinate gives $$H(-\lambda,-\kappa,r)=-H(\lambda,\kappa,r).$$ Differentiating this symmetry shows that $P,Q$, their $r$-derivatives, and $\mathcal W$ are unchanged by simultaneous sign reversal of $\lambda,\kappa$. Thus $\mathcal W>0$ also when $\kappa<0$. Smooth dependence of the model on finite parameters at every $r>0$ now gives $\mathcal W\ge0$ at $\kappa=0$. Only this non-strict conclusion at zero curvature is required. Locally the passage is uniform on compact parameter sets with $r>0$; $G>0$ there is already known from Theorem <a href="#thm:quadratic-fit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:quadratic-fit">13</a>.

Finally, apply the pointwise fixed-parameter identity Equation <a href="#model:quotient-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="model:quotient-derivative">[model:quotient-derivative]</a>. It gives $(R/G)_r\le0$ for every $\lambda,\kappa,r$, as asserted. The characteristic transport established the sign of a scalar function on the whole model domain; it did not identify its partial $r$-derivative with a total derivative along a characteristic. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:transport"></a>
### Shape conditions and fitted parameters

We use the fitted functions $\lambda(h,r),\kappa(h,r)$ defined in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-identities">[eq:fit-identities]</a> for a smooth profile $\phi$, and retain $k=\phi'$, $D=\partial_h-\alpha\partial_r$ and the model derivatives evaluated at the fit. We will prove two comparisons: the sign of $\phi'''$ controls the width derivative of fitted curvature, while the sign of $((\phi'-d)/u)'$, for a fixed real $d$, controls the fitted slope intercept $\lambda-h\kappa$. These are the shape conditions used in Section <a href="#sec:application" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:application">6</a>. The transport law is already available from Lemma <a href="#lem:fit-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fit-transport">14</a>; its evolution starts from the small-width data established next.

<a id="stmt-lem-fit-small-width"></a>
<div id="lem:fit-small-width" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fit-small-width"></a>
<a id="eq:fit-smooth-remainders"></a>
**Lemma 19** (The small-width fit). *At each positive height, locally uniformly in that height as $r$ tends to zero, $$\label{eq:fit-small-width}
 \begin{aligned}
 \kappa(h,r)&=\phi''(h)+\frac{2}{7}\phi'''(h)r^2+O(r^3),\\
 \kappa_r(h,r)&=\frac{4}{7}\phi'''(h)r+O(r^2),\\
 \lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\frac{1}{35}\phi'''(h)r^4+O(r^5).
 \end{aligned}$$ More precisely, for every $h_0>0$ there are smooth functions $A$ and $B$ on a neighborhood of $(h_0,0)$ for which, at positive $r$ in that neighborhood, $$\label{eq:fit-smooth-remainders}
 \begin{aligned}
 \lambda(h,r)&=k(h)-\frac{1}{35}\phi'''(h)r^4+r^5 A(h,r),\\
 \kappa(h,r)&=\phi''(h)+\frac{2}{7}\phi'''(h)r^2+r^3 B(h,r).
 \end{aligned}$$ Thus the derivative estimate in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-small-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-small-width">[eq:fit-small-width]</a> comes from a smooth remainder.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fit-small-width"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fit-small-width</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $h_0>0$. Use the smooth width-one profile family $g(h,\rho,s)$ of Equation <a href="#eq:arc-scaling-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-scaling-profile">[eq:arc-scaling-profile]</a>, with signed auxiliary parameter $\rho$ near zero and $h$ near $h_0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:arc-scaling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:arc-scaling">4</a> provides smooth endpoint data at scaled width one for this family and for the finite-parameter perturbations below. In particular, the local implicit inversion that fixes the width is performed near the same transverse parabolic arc.

For a scaled profile $\psi$, let $\mathcal A(\psi)$ denote the pair consisting of its midpoint and its midpoint derivative with respect to scaled width, evaluated at base height $0$ and width $1$. Only the smooth finite-parameter families of profiles just described are used in this notation. For the quadratic profile $\psi(s)=a_1s+a_2s^2/2$, the derivative of this pair at $(a_1,a_2)=(0,0)$ is, by Equation <a href="#eq:parabolic-variations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parabolic-variations">[eq:parabolic-variations]</a>, $$J=\begin{pmatrix}
       1/3&1/15\\
       2/3&4/15
    \end{pmatrix}.$$ It is nonsingular. The cubic profile variation $s^3/6$ gives the column $$b=\binom{1/105}{6/105},\qquad
 J^{-1}b=\binom{-1/35}{2/7}.$$

Taylor expansion of Equation <a href="#eq:arc-scaling-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-scaling-profile">[eq:arc-scaling-profile]</a>, with a smooth remainder on the fixed scaled-height interval, gives <a id="eq:fit-scaled-taylor"></a>$$\label{eq:fit-scaled-taylor}
 g(h,\rho,s)
 =\rho k(h)s+\rho^3\phi''(h)\frac{s^2}{2}
   +\rho^5\phi'''(h)\frac{s^3}{6}
   +\rho^7\mathcal R(h,\rho,s),$$ where $\mathcal R$ is smooth. Write $$g_2(h,\rho,s)=\rho k(h)s+\rho^3\phi''(h)\frac{s^2}{2},
 \qquad
 \eta(h,\rho,s)=\phi'''(h)\frac{s^3}{6}
                    +\rho^2\mathcal R(h,\rho,s).$$ Then $g=g_2+\rho^5\eta$. Apply the local inverse defined by $J$ to the data of the smooth family $g_2+\varepsilon\eta$, with $(h,\rho,\varepsilon)$ near $(h_0,0,0)$. Denote its fitted scaled coefficients by $a_1(h,\rho,\varepsilon)$ and $a_2(h,\rho,\varepsilon)$. At $\varepsilon=0$ these are exactly $\rho k(h)$ and $\rho^3\phi''(h)$, respectively. The integral form of Taylor’s formula in $\varepsilon$ shows that $$\binom{a_1(h,\rho,\rho^5)-\rho k(h)}
       {a_2(h,\rho,\rho^5)-\rho^3\phi''(h)}
   =\rho^5 q(h,\rho)$$ for a smooth vector-valued function $q$. Differentiating at $\rho=\varepsilon=0$ in the profile-variation direction yields $$q(h,0)=\phi'''(h)J^{-1}b
       =\phi'''(h)\binom{-1/35}{2/7}.$$ Another smooth Taylor factorization in $\rho$ therefore writes $$q(h,\rho)=\phi'''(h)\binom{-1/35}{2/7}
                    +\rho\binom{A(h,\rho)}{B(h,\rho)}.$$

For $\rho=r>0$, the scaled actual data are $(M(h,r)/r,v(h,r))$. Scaling the quadratic fit in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-identities">[eq:fit-identities]</a> gives scaled coefficients $(r\lambda,r^3\kappa)$. Uniqueness in Theorem <a href="#thm:quadratic-fit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:quadratic-fit">13</a> identifies them with the local coefficients just constructed. Dividing the two corrections by $r$ and $r^3$, respectively, proves Equation <a href="#eq:fit-smooth-remainders" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-smooth-remainders">[eq:fit-smooth-remainders]</a>. Differentiating its second identity at fixed $h$ gives $$\kappa_r(h,r)=\frac47\phi'''(h)r
                 +3r^2B(h,r)+r^3B_r(h,r).$$ This proves all the locally uniform estimates in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-small-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-small-width">[eq:fit-small-width]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-third-derivative"></a>
<div id="prop:third-derivative" class="proposition">

**Proposition 20** (A strict third derivative). *If $\phi'''(u)>0$ for every $u>0$, then, for every $h,r>0$, $$\lambda(h,r)<k(h),\qquad \kappa_r(h,r)>0.$$ If $\phi'''(u)<0$ for every $u>0$, both inequalities reverse: $$\lambda(h,r)>k(h),\qquad \kappa_r(h,r)<0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-third-derivative"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-third-derivative</summary>

<a id="eq:actual-curvature-decrease"></a>
*Proof.* Assume first that $\phi'''>0$. Fix a fitted arc with base height $h$ and width $r$, and let its peak height be $t$. Parametrize its characteristic by outward width $s\in(0,r]$, writing the base height as $h(s)$, with $h(r)=h$ and $h(s)\to t$ as $s\downarrow0$. Put $$\theta(s)=\operatorname{arctanh}\frac{M(h(s),s)}s,
 \qquad
 L(s)=\operatorname{arctanh}v(h(s),s),
 \qquad c(s)=\phi''(h(s)).$$ The characteristic equations in Equation <a href="#eq:arc-characteristics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-characteristics">[eq:arc-characteristics]</a> give $$\label{eq:actual-curvature-decrease}
 c'(s)=-s\operatorname{sech}^2\theta(s)\,
                       \phi'''(h(s))<0\qquad(s>0).$$ In particular $c$ extends continuously to $s=0$ with $c(0)=\phi''(t)$ and is strictly decreasing on $[0,r]$.

<a id="eq:third-derivative-error"></a>
In the following comparison, hold the curvature equal to the final fitted value $\kappa=\kappa(h,r)$. Let $J_\kappa$ and $\Theta_\kappa(s,z)$ be the slope field and the reference angle family of Section <a href="#sec:fitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fitting">3</a>. Define $$E(s)=k(h(s))-J_\kappa(s,\theta(s)),\qquad
 a(s)=(J_\kappa)_\theta(s,\theta(s))>0,$$ and $$f(s)=-s\operatorname{sech}^2\theta(s)\,(c(s)-\kappa).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature-comparison">9</a>, specifically Equation <a href="#eq:curvature-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-error">[eq:curvature-error]</a>, gives $$\label{eq:third-derivative-error}
 E'(s)+a(s)E(s)=f(s),\qquad \lim_{s\downarrow0}E(s)=0.$$ The integral formula in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-error-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-error-integral">[eq:fit-error-integral]</a> applies with this forcing $f$. Its positive kernel preserves the sign of $f$, even though $a$ may be singular at the peak.

The strict decrease of $c$ gives the following exhaustive sign cases. If $\kappa\ge c(0)$, then $f(s)>0$ for every $s>0$, and Equation <a href="#eq:fit-error-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-error-integral">[eq:fit-error-integral]</a> gives $E(s)>0$. If $\kappa\le c(r)$, then $f(s)<0$ for $0<s<r$, and $E(s)<0$ for $0<s\le r$. In the remaining case there is a unique $s_0\in(0,r)$ with $c(s_0)=\kappa$. Here $f<0$ before $s_0$ and $f>0$ after it. The same integral formula gives $E<0$ on $(0,s_0]$, whereas $$\frac{d}{ds}\left[
 E(s)\exp\left(\int_{s_0}^s a(q)\,dq\right)\right]
 =f(s)\exp\left(\int_{s_0}^s a(q)\,dq\right)>0
 \qquad(s>s_0).$$ Consequently $E$ either remains negative up to the final width, possibly having $E(r)=0$, or crosses zero exactly once before $r$ and is strictly positive thereafter. It cannot vanish on a nontrivial interval: Equation <a href="#eq:third-derivative-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:third-derivative-error">[eq:third-derivative-error]</a> would then force $c=\kappa$ on that interval, contrary to Equation <a href="#eq:actual-curvature-decrease" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:actual-curvature-decrease">[eq:actual-curvature-decrease]</a>.

<a id="eq:matched-angle-integrals"></a>
Let $z(s)$ be the reference label determined by $$\theta(s)=\Theta_\kappa(s,z(s)).$$ By Equation <a href="#eq:label-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-derivative">[eq:label-derivative]</a>, $$z'(s)=\frac{E(s)}{\partial_z\Theta_\kappa(s,z(s))},
 \qquad \partial_z\Theta_\kappa(s,z)>0.$$ The constant label $z_* = z(r)$ describes the quadratic reference whose final angle agrees with the actual one. Its final slope is $\lambda(h,r)$, by the construction of the fit. The fit also makes the final values of $v$, and hence of $L$, equal. Therefore Equation <a href="#eq:arc-characteristics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:arc-characteristics">[eq:arc-characteristics]</a> gives $$\label{eq:matched-angle-integrals}
 \int_0^r\frac{\sinh(2\theta(s))}{s}\,ds
 =\int_0^r\frac{\sinh(2\Theta_\kappa(s,z_*))}{s}\,ds.$$ Both integrals converge: the two angles are $O(s)$ near their smooth peaks.

<a id="eq:fitted-slope-strict"></a>
If $z(s)$ were monotone and nonconstant on $(0,r]$, monotonicity of $\Theta_\kappa$ in its label would order the two integrands in Equation <a href="#eq:matched-angle-integrals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matched-angle-integrals">[eq:matched-angle-integrals]</a> in one direction, with strict inequality on a nonempty open subinterval. This contradicts their equal integrals. The first two sign cases above therefore cannot occur. In the two-sign forcing case, $E$ must cross zero strictly before $r$: otherwise $z$ is strictly decreasing on $(0,r)$, even if $E(r)=0$, giving the same contradiction. A constant label is also impossible, since it would give $E\equiv0$ and hence constant $c$. It follows that $$\label{eq:fitted-slope-strict}
 E(r)=k(h)-\lambda(h,r)>0.$$

<a id="eq:curvature-derivative-transport"></a>
It remains to propagate the sign of the width derivative of fitted curvature. As a function of independent model parameters, set $$C(\lambda,\kappa,r)=\frac{R(\lambda,\kappa,r)}
                          {G(\lambda,\kappa,r)}.$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:model-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:model-inequality">15</a> asserts that its explicit partial derivative satisfies $C_r\le0$ when $\lambda$ and $\kappa$ are held fixed. Differentiating the second identity in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-transport">[eq:fit-transport]</a> at fixed $h$, we must also account for $$\partial_r(D\kappa)=D(\kappa_r)-\alpha_r\kappa_r.$$ Using $\lambda_r=-Q\kappa_r/P$ gives $$\label{eq:curvature-derivative-transport}
 D(\kappa_r)=\mathcal T(h,r)\kappa_r
                 -C_r(\lambda,\kappa,r)(\lambda-k(h)),$$ where <a id="eq:curvature-derivative-coefficient"></a>$$\label{eq:curvature-derivative-coefficient}
 \mathcal T
  =\alpha_r+\frac{CQ}{P}
     -\left(C_\kappa-\frac{Q}{P}C_\lambda\right)(\lambda-k(h)).$$ Here $\alpha_r$ is the derivative of the actual function $\alpha(h,r)$, whereas $C_\lambda,C_\kappa,C_r$ are model partial derivatives evaluated at the fit. All terms in $\mathcal T$ are smooth at positive height and width. The dependence of $\alpha$ on $M$ is thus included in the coefficient of $\kappa_r$; it creates no additional independent source.

On the fixed characteristic, write $w(s)=\kappa_r(h(s),s)$. Equation <a href="#eq:fit-small-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-small-width">[eq:fit-small-width]</a>, locally uniformly near its peak $t$, gives $$w(s)=\frac47\phi'''(h(s))s+O(s^2)>0$$ for all sufficiently small positive $s$. Equations <a href="#eq:fitted-slope-strict" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fitted-slope-strict">[eq:fitted-slope-strict]</a> and <a href="#eq:curvature-derivative-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-derivative-transport">[eq:curvature-derivative-transport]</a> give, along the characteristic, $$w'(s)=-\frac{\mathcal T}{\alpha}w(s)
              +\frac{C_r}{\alpha}(\lambda-k),
 \qquad \frac{C_r}{\alpha}(\lambda-k)\ge0.$$ Choose a sufficiently small positive $s_1$ with $w(s_1)>0$. On $[s_1,r]$ all coefficients are continuous. Multiplication by the positive integrating factor $$\mu(s)=\exp\left(\int_{s_1}^s\frac{\mathcal T}{\alpha}\,dq\right)$$ gives $(\mu w)'\ge0$. Thus $w(r)>0$, proving $\kappa_r(h,r)>0$. Since the chosen fit was arbitrary, both assertions hold for every $h,r>0$.

Finally, reflection of the endpoint coordinate changes $\phi$ to $-\phi$ and changes $(M,v)$ to $(-M,-v)$. Uniqueness of the quadratic fit changes $(\lambda,\kappa)$ to $(-\lambda,-\kappa)$. Applying the proved case to $-\phi$ proves the two reversed inequalities when $\phi'''<0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-intercept"></a>
<div id="prop:intercept" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (The fitted slope intercept). *Let $d\in\mathbb R$. If $$\frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{k(u)-d}{u}\right)>0
 \qquad\text{for every }u>0,$$ then $$\lambda(h,r)-h\kappa(h,r)<d
 \qquad\text{for every }h,r>0.$$ If the displayed derivative is strictly negative everywhere, then $\lambda(h,r)-h\kappa(h,r)>d$ everywhere.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-intercept"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-intercept</summary>

*Proof.* Assume the displayed derivative is positive and set $$I(h,r)=\lambda(h,r)-h\kappa(h,r)-d.$$ Fix a characteristic with peak height $t$. By Equation <a href="#eq:fit-small-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-small-width">[eq:fit-small-width]</a>, its limit at width zero is $$\lim_{s\downarrow0}I(h(s),s)
 =k(t)-t\phi''(t)-d
 =-t^2\left.\frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{k(u)-d}{u}\right)\right|_{u=t}
 <0.$$ Thus $I$ is strictly negative near the peak.

<a id="eq:intercept-zero-curvature"></a>
Consider any positive-height point on the characteristic at which $I=0$. At that point $\lambda=d+h\kappa$. We claim that $$\label{eq:intercept-zero-curvature}
 \kappa>\frac{k(h)-d}{h}.$$ Indeed, if the reverse weak inequality held, then for every $u>h$ the hypothesis would give $$\frac{k(u)-d}{u}>\frac{k(h)-d}{h}\ge\kappa,
 \qquad
 k(u)>d+u\kappa=\lambda+(u-h)\kappa.$$ Integrating from $h$ to $u$ shows $$\phi(u)-\phi(h)
   >\lambda(u-h)+\frac{\kappa}{2}(u-h)^2
 \qquad(u>h).$$ The strict translated-profile comparison of Lemma <a href="#lem:variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:variation">3</a> then makes the actual midpoint at the fixed base height and width strictly larger than the quadratic midpoint. This contradicts Equation <a href="#eq:fit-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-identities">[eq:fit-identities]</a>, proving Equation <a href="#eq:intercept-zero-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intercept-zero-curvature">[eq:intercept-zero-curvature]</a>. In particular $\lambda=d+h\kappa>k(h)$ at any zero of $I$.

Equation <a href="#eq:fit-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-transport">[eq:fit-transport]</a> gives the exact identity <a id="eq:intercept-transport"></a>$$\label{eq:intercept-transport}
 DI=\frac{S+hR}{G}\bigl(\lambda-k(h)\bigr).$$ At a zero of $I$ its right-hand side is strictly positive. The outward characteristic derivative is therefore $-DI/\alpha<0$. If $I$ had a first zero after its initially negative interval, its outward derivative there would instead be nonnegative. This contradiction proves $I<0$ everywhere along the characteristic. Every fit belongs to such a characteristic, so the assertion holds for all $h,r>0$.

For a strictly negative derivative, apply the proved assertion to $-\phi$ and $-d$, and use the reflection of fitted parameters from the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:third-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:third-derivative">20</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-fitted-curvature-range"></a>
<div id="lem:fitted-curvature-range" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fitted-curvature-range"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Range of the fitted curvature). *Let an arc have positive base height $h$, peak height $t>h$, and width $r$. Its fitted curvature satisfies $$\label{eq:fitted-curvature-range}
 \min_{h\le u\le t}\phi''(u)
       \le\kappa(h,r)\le
 \max_{h\le u\le t}\phi''(u).$$ If $\phi''$ is nonconstant on $[h,t]$, both inequalities are strict.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fitted-curvature-range"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fitted-curvature-range</summary>

*Proof.* Write $m=\min_{[h,t]}\phi''$ and $b=\max_{[h,t]}\phi''$. If the fitted $\kappa$ were less than $m$, the actual curvature would be strictly larger than this constant reference curvature along the whole characteristic. Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature-comparison">9</a> would then make the actual final $L$ strictly larger than the reference $L$ with the same final angle, contradicting the fit. A fitted curvature greater than $b$ gives the reversed contradiction. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:fitted-curvature-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fitted-curvature-range">[eq:fitted-curvature-range]</a>.

If $\phi''$ is nonconstant and $\kappa=m$, the forcing in Equation <a href="#eq:curvature-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:curvature-error">[eq:curvature-error]</a> is nonpositive and strictly negative on a positive interior width interval. Its integral representation gives $E\le0$ everywhere and $E<0$ on a nonempty interval. Equation <a href="#eq:label-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-derivative">[eq:label-derivative]</a> makes the reference label nonincreasing and nonconstant. Relative to its final constant label, the actual earlier angles are therefore no smaller and are strictly larger on a nonempty open interval. As in Equation <a href="#eq:matched-angle-integrals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matched-angle-integrals">[eq:matched-angle-integrals]</a>, their $L$ integral is strictly larger, a contradiction. The case $\kappa=b$ reverses all these inequalities. Thus both bounds are strict whenever the actual curvature is nonconstant. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:application"></a>
### The quintic upper bound

We now apply the comparison results to the full polynomial class. First replace $y$ by $y-F(0)$, so that $F(0)=0$. If $$X(t)=-x(-t),\qquad Y(t)=y(-t),$$ then $$\dot X=Y-F(-X),\qquad \dot Y=-X.$$ This reflection and time reversal preserve the geometric periodic orbits and their isolation, and negate the coefficient of $x^5$. We may therefore assume that coefficient is nonnegative. No normalization of the other coefficients is needed.

On either open half-plane, put $u=x^2/2$ and use $y$ as coordinate along the orbit. Equation <a href="#eq:original-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:original-system">[eq:original-system]</a> becomes <a id="eq:quintic-profiles"></a>$$\label{eq:quintic-profiles}
 \frac{du}{dy}=\phi_\pm(u)-y,\qquad
 \phi_\pm(u)=d_0u+b_0u^2\pm p(u),\qquad
 p(u)=e u^{1/2}+c u^{3/2}+a u^{5/2},\quad a\ge0.$$ The plus sign corresponds to $x>0$. Indeed, $\phi_\pm(u)=F(\pm\sqrt{2u})$; if $F(x)=\sum_{j=1}^5 f_jx^j$, then $$d_0=2f_2,\quad b_0=4f_4,\quad
 e=\sqrt2\,f_1,\quad c=2^{3/2}f_3,\quad a=2^{5/2}f_5.$$ Thus $d_0,b_0,e,c$ are unrestricted. The profiles are smooth on $(0,\infty)$ and continuous at zero, where they vanish.

Let $J$ be the common open interval of transverse height-zero widths from Proposition <a href="#prop:cycle-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cycle-matching">6</a>. If $J$ is empty there is no periodic orbit. For $r\in J$, define $\lambda_\pm(0,r),\kappa_\pm(0,r)$ by applying the inverse in Theorem <a href="#thm:quadratic-fit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:quadratic-fit">13</a> to $(M_\pm(0,r),M_{\pm,r}(0,r),r)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:axis-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:axis-extension">5</a> makes these data interior to the fit domain; continuity of the inverse identifies the resulting parameters with the limits of the positive-height fits. Put <a id="eq:matching-difference"></a>$$\label{eq:matching-difference}
 \Delta(r)=M_+(0,r)-M_-(0,r),\qquad r\in J.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:cycle-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cycle-matching">6</a> shows that it suffices to bound the isolated zeros of $\Delta$ on this single interval.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The model comparison at a matching height

<a id="eq:V-derivatives"></a>
<a id="eq:midpoint-ode"></a>
Recall the model width derivative after fitting a prescribed midpoint, defined in Equation <a href="#eq:fit-conditional-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fit-conditional-data">[eq:fit-conditional-data]</a>: $$\widehat v(r,M,\kappa)
 =H_r\bigl(\lambda_*(r,M,\kappa),\kappa,r\bigr),
 \qquad r>0,\quad |M|<r,\quad \kappa\in\mathbb R.$$ Implicit differentiation of the midpoint fit gives $$\label{eq:V-derivatives}
 \widehat v_M=\frac RP>0,\qquad
 \widehat v_\kappa=\frac GP>0.$$ All model derivatives here are evaluated at the corresponding parameters. For either actual profile, $$\label{eq:midpoint-ode}
 \frac{d}{dr}M_\pm(0,r)
 =\widehat v\bigl(r,M_\pm(0,r),\kappa_\pm(0,r)\bigr).$$ The smooth dependence at height zero in Lemma <a href="#lem:axis-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:axis-extension">5</a>, together with the joint smooth inverse in Theorem <a href="#thm:quadratic-fit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:quadratic-fit">13</a>, justifies these identities and the limits used below.

Lemma <a href="#lem:fitted-curvature-range" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fitted-curvature-range">22</a> bounds each fitted curvature between the minimum and maximum of the actual curvature along its arc. We will use this local bound before passing to height zero.

<a id="stmt-lem-isolated-zero-count"></a>
<div id="lem:isolated-zero-count" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23**. *Let $f$ be continuously differentiable on an open interval and suppose $f'=bq$, where $b$ is continuous and positive and $q$ is continuous and nondecreasing. Then $f$ has at most two isolated zeros. If a continuously differentiable function has strictly positive derivative at each of its zeros, it has at most one zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-isolated-zero-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-isolated-zero-count</summary>

*Proof.* For the first statement, the sets where $q$ is negative, zero, and positive occur in that order. The zero set of $q$ is an interval, possibly empty or a singleton. Accordingly $f$ strictly decreases, is constant, and strictly increases on the respective sets. Each strict part has at most one zero. If the constant part has nonzero value it contributes none. If it has positive length and value zero, every point of it and each endpoint lying in the domain are nonisolated zeros, and neither strict part contributes another zero. If it is a singleton at which $f=0$, that point is the sole minimum zero. These possibilities prove the claim.

For the second statement, suppose $r_1<r_2$ were two zeros. Positive derivatives give $a,b$ with $r_1<a<b<r_2$, $f(a)>0$, and $f(b)<0$. The first zero $c$ in $[a,b]$ exists by continuity. Since $f>0$ on $[a,c)$, its derivative at $c$, which exists, is nonpositive. This contradicts the hypothesis. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The four coefficient cases

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### Case 1: $e\ge0$ and $c\ge0$.

Either $p\equiv0$, in which case the two profiles and their midpoint functions coincide and no root in $J$ is isolated, or $p(u)>0$ for every $u>0$. In the latter case even one periodic orbit is impossible. To see this, suppose the two positive arches $u_\pm(y)$ had the same two endpoints $A<B$ at height zero. Their difference $w=u_+-u_-$ satisfies $$w'=\bigl[d_0+b_0(u_++u_-)\bigr]w
       +p(u_+)+p(u_-),\qquad w(A)=w(B)=0.$$ The coefficient is continuous on $[A,B]$, and the inhomogeneous term is strictly positive on $(A,B)$. The integrating-factor formula from $A$ gives $w(B)>0$, a contradiction.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### Case 2: $e\ge0$ and $c<0$.

Here <a id="eq:third-odd"></a>$$\label{eq:third-odd}
 p'''(u)=\frac{3e-3cu+15au^2}{8u^{5/2}}>0.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:third-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:third-derivative">20</a> gives $\partial_r\kappa_+(h,r)>0$ and $\partial_r\kappa_-(h,r)<0$ for $h,r>0$. Passage to the height-zero limits at any two specified widths in $J$ shows that $$q(r)=\kappa_+(0,r)-\kappa_-(0,r)$$ is nondecreasing on $J$.

Subtract the two instances of Equation <a href="#eq:midpoint-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-ode">[eq:midpoint-ode]</a>. The fundamental theorem of calculus, first in $M$ and then in $\kappa$, gives <a id="eq:Delta-transport"></a>$$\label{eq:Delta-transport}
 \Delta'=A(r)\Delta+B(r)q,\qquad B(r)>0,$$ with continuous coefficients. More explicitly, suppressing $(0,r)$ in the actual parameters, one can take $$\begin{split}
 A(r)&=\int_0^1
 \widehat v_M\bigl(r,M_-+\tau(M_+-M_-),\kappa_+\bigr)\,d\tau,\\
 B(r)&=\int_0^1
 \widehat v_\kappa\bigl(r,M_-,\kappa_-+\tau(\kappa_+-\kappa_-)\bigr)\,d\tau.
 \end{split}$$ The midpoint segment remains in $(-r,r)$ and there is no restriction on the curvature segment. Equation <a href="#eq:V-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:V-derivatives">[eq:V-derivatives]</a> therefore proves the asserted positivity.

Fix $r_*\in J$ and multiply $\Delta$ by $\exp(-\int_{r_*}^r A(s)\,ds)$. The derivative of the resulting function is a continuous positive factor times $q$. Lemma <a href="#lem:isolated-zero-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:isolated-zero-count">23</a> bounds its isolated zeros, and hence those of $\Delta$, by two. This argument includes multiple isolated roots and zero intervals.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Case 3: $e<0$ and $c\ge0$.

In this case <a id="eq:second-odd"></a>$$\label{eq:second-odd}
 p''(u)=\frac{-e+3cu+15au^2}{4u^{3/2}}>0,
 \qquad p''(u)\longrightarrow+\infty\quad(u\downarrow0).$$ Fix $r_0\in J$. The peaks of the positive-height arcs at width $r_0$ approach finite positive peaks as $h\downarrow0$, by Lemma <a href="#lem:axis-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:axis-extension">5</a>. Choose $T$ larger than both peaks and all sufficiently nearby ones. The function $p''$ has a positive lower bound $\delta$ on $(0,T]$: it tends to infinity at zero and is continuous and positive away from zero. Consequently, throughout the relevant arcs, $$\phi_+''\ge2b_0+\delta,\qquad
 \phi_-''\le2b_0-\delta.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:fitted-curvature-range" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fitted-curvature-range">22</a> and passage to the limit give <a id="eq:curvature-gap"></a>$$\label{eq:curvature-gap}
 \kappa_+(0,r_0)\ge2b_0+\delta
 >2b_0-\delta\ge\kappa_-(0,r_0).$$ At every zero of $\Delta$, Equation <a href="#eq:midpoint-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-ode">[eq:midpoint-ode]</a> and $\widehat v_\kappa>0$ now imply $\Delta'>0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:isolated-zero-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:isolated-zero-count">23</a> gives at most one zero.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Case 4: $e<0$ and $c<0$.

<a id="eq:intercept-odd"></a>
<a id="eq:positive-height-intercepts"></a>
<a id="eq:strict-slope-gap"></a>
We have $$\label{eq:intercept-odd}
 up''(u)-p'(u)=
 \frac{-3e-3cu+5au^2}{4\sqrt u}>0.$$ Writing $k_\pm=\phi_\pm'$, Equation <a href="#eq:intercept-odd" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intercept-odd">[eq:intercept-odd]</a> is precisely $$\left(\frac{k_+(h)-d_0}{h}\right)'>0,\qquad
 \left(\frac{k_-(h)-d_0}{h}\right)'<0.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:intercept" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:intercept">21</a> therefore gives $$\label{eq:positive-height-intercepts}
 \lambda_+(h,r)-h\kappa_+(h,r)<d_0,\qquad
 \lambda_-(h,r)-h\kappa_-(h,r)>d_0
 \quad(h,r>0).$$ The strict inequalities persist at height zero, but this requires more than simply taking limits. Fix a transverse peak of the plus profile, and let $r(h)$ be its width at height $h$, with $r(h)\to r_0\in J$. The endpoint data converge to an interior triple $(M_0,v_0,r_0)$, with $|M_0|<r_0$ and $|v_0|<1$. The joint smooth inverse in Theorem <a href="#thm:quadratic-fit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:quadratic-fit">13</a> shows that $\lambda(h,r(h))$ and $\kappa(h,r(h))$ have finite limits. Put $$I(h)=\lambda(h,r(h))-h\kappa(h,r(h))-d_0.$$ The derivative along this fixed-peak characteristic is <a id="eq:strict-boundary-intercept"></a>$$\label{eq:strict-boundary-intercept}
 \frac{dI}{dh}=DI=\frac{S+hR}{G}\bigl(\lambda-k_+(h)\bigr).$$ Since $e<0$, $k_+(h)\to-\infty$, whereas $\lambda$ remains bounded. Thus $dI/dh>0$ for all sufficiently small positive $h$. Choose one such $h_1$. Equation <a href="#eq:positive-height-intercepts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-height-intercepts">[eq:positive-height-intercepts]</a> and integration in decreasing $h$ give $$I(h)\le I(h_1)<0\qquad(0<h<h_1).$$ Taking the limit yields $\lambda_+(0,r_0)<d_0$. On the minus side, $k_-(h)\to+\infty$, so the same argument with all signs reversed gives $\lambda_-(0,r_0)>d_0$. Therefore, throughout $J$, $$\label{eq:strict-slope-gap}
 \lambda_+(0,r)<d_0<\lambda_-(0,r).$$

At a zero of $\Delta$, the two model midpoints agree. Because $H_\lambda,H_\kappa>0$, Equation <a href="#eq:strict-slope-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strict-slope-gap">[eq:strict-slope-gap]</a> forces $\kappa_+(0,r)>\kappa_-(0,r)$: otherwise increasing $\lambda_+$ to $\lambda_-$ and then $\kappa_+$ to $\kappa_-$ would strictly increase $H$. Equation <a href="#eq:midpoint-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-ode">[eq:midpoint-ode]</a> again gives $\Delta'>0$ at every zero, and there is at most one.

These four cases exhaust all $e,c\in\mathbb R$ with $a\ge0$, including zero leading coefficients. By Proposition <a href="#prop:cycle-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cycle-matching">6</a>, they prove the upper bound in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> for every allowed polynomial, without a restriction on amplitude or on the multiplicity of a limit cycle.

<a id="sec:sharpness"></a>
### Sharpness

We finish the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> by giving a direct two-cycle construction. It also makes clear why a perturbative calculation suffices for the lower bound, although it was not used to prove the upper bound. The method is a direct first-order return-displacement calculation, in the classical perturbative tradition discussed in <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[LMP](#ref-LMP), [LlibreZhang](#ref-LlibreZhang)]. We derive the exact energy quotient needed for the argument below.

Set <a id="eq:sharp-polynomial"></a>$$\label{eq:sharp-polynomial}
 F_\varepsilon(x)=\varepsilon f(x),\qquad
 f(x)=4x-\frac{20}{3}x^3+\frac85x^5.$$ At $\varepsilon=0$, the solution through $(0,s)$, $s>0$, is $$x(t)=s\sin t,\qquad y(t)=s\cos t.$$ For $s$ in a neighborhood of either $1$ or $2$, smooth flow dependence on a neighborhood of this compact circle, together with transversality at $t=2\pi$, gives a smooth return time $\tau(s,\varepsilon)$ near $2\pi$ and a smooth positive return ordinate $P(s,\varepsilon)$ on $x=0,y>0$. These are the first positive-axis returns for small $|\varepsilon|$. Indeed, away from small time neighborhoods of the two axis crossings the unperturbed $x$ is bounded away from zero; near each crossing transversality gives a unique nearby crossing. These statements hold on one common parameter neighborhood after shrinking the two neighborhoods if necessary.

<a id="eq:energy-quotient"></a>
For $E=(x^2+y^2)/2$ the exact energy identity is $$\dot E=-\varepsilon x f(x).$$ Hence the one-turn energy displacement has the factorization $$\label{eq:energy-quotient}
 \frac{P(s,\varepsilon)^2-s^2}{2}
 =\varepsilon Q(s,\varepsilon),\qquad
 Q(s,\varepsilon)=
 -\int_0^{\tau(s,\varepsilon)}
 x(t;s,\varepsilon)f(x(t;s,\varepsilon))\,dt.$$ The integral is smooth in $(s,\varepsilon)$ and defines the quotient at $\varepsilon=0$ exactly. Using $$\int_0^{2\pi}\sin^2t\,dt=\pi,\quad
 \int_0^{2\pi}\sin^4t\,dt=\frac{3\pi}{4},\quad
 \int_0^{2\pi}\sin^6t\,dt=\frac{5\pi}{8},$$ we obtain <a id="eq:sharp-averaged"></a>$$\label{eq:sharp-averaged}
 Q(s,0)=-\pi s^2(4-5s^2+s^4).$$ Its zeros at $s=1,2$ are simple: $$Q_s(1,0)=6\pi,\qquad Q_s(2,0)=-48\pi.$$ The implicit function theorem gives roots $s_1(\varepsilon),s_2(\varepsilon)$ in disjoint positive neighborhoods, for the same sufficiently small $|\varepsilon|$. Fix any nonzero positive $\varepsilon$ in that common range.

At these roots, $P+s>0$, so Equation <a href="#eq:energy-quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-quotient">[eq:energy-quotient]</a> implies $P=s$. Thus each root gives a periodic orbit. Differentiating the energy displacement in $s$ at a root gives $$s(P_s-1)=\varepsilon Q_s\ne0.$$ Each return fixed point is therefore simple and isolated. The two orbits are geometrically distinct, since their positive intercepts lie in disjoint neighborhoods and a periodic orbit has just one positive intercept, as proved in Proposition <a href="#prop:cycle-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cycle-matching">6</a>. After decreasing the common upper bound on $\varepsilon>0$ if necessary, continuity from $P_s(s,0)=1$ gives $P_s>0$ at both fixed points. The signs of $Q_s$ then give $P_s>1$ on the inner cycle and $0<P_s<1$ on the outer one. Thus both cycles are hyperbolic: the inner one repels and the outer one attracts. This proves sharpness. 0◻

(.30,3.0)\n               .. controls (1.08,3.0) and (1.85,2.45) .. (2.45,.85);\n  \\fill (-1.65,.85) circle (1.5pt);\n  \\fill (2.45,.85) circle (1.5pt);\n  \\fill (.30,3.0) circle (1.5pt);\n  \\draw[densely dotted] (.30,3.0)--(-2.25,3.0);\n  \\node[left] at (-2.25,3.0) {$t$};\n  \\node[above] at (.30,3.0) {$(\\phi(t),t)$};\n  \\draw[densely dotted] (-1.65,0)--(-1.65,.85);\n  \\draw[densely dotted] (2.45,0)--(2.45,.85);\n  \\node[below] at (-1.65,0) {$y_<$};\n  \\node[below] at (2.45,0) {$y_>$};\n  \\draw[densely dotted] (.4,.85)--(.4,-.55);\n  \\draw[<->] (-1.65,.53)--(.4,.53) node[midway,below] {$r$};\n  \\draw[<->] (.4,.53)--(2.45,.53) node[midway,below] {$r$};\n  \\draw[densely dotted] (-.45,.85)--(-.45,-.55);\n  \\node[below] at (-.45,-.65) {$\\phi(h)$};\n  \\node[below] at (.95,-.65) {$\\phi(h)+M$};\n  \\draw[thin] (.4,-.55)--(.95,-.65);\n  \\draw[<->] (-.45,-.42)--(.4,-.42) node[midway,above] {$M$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A Cartesian coordinate system with vertical axis $u$ and horizontal axis $y$. A downward-opening arc begins at height $h$ with left endpoint $y_<$ and right endpoint $y_>$, rising to a peak at point $(\\phi(t), t)$ marked with a solid dot. Horizontal dashed lines extend from the $u$-axis at height values $h$ and $t$. Vertical dashed lines drop from $y_<$, the midpoint of the arc base, and $y_>$ down toward the horizontal baseline. Dimension arrows at height $h$ show the half-width $r$ from $y_<$ to the midpoint and $r$ from the midpoint to $y_>$. On the baseline below, ticks mark positions $y_<$, $\\phi(h)$, $\\phi(h) + M$, and $y_>$. A double-headed horizontal arrow spans the distance between $\\phi(h)$ and $\\phi(h) + M$, labeled $M$."
    }
